In 1931, GunnarNylund(1904-1989)was appointed Rörstrand's artistic director, and the following year the factory's operations were expanded to include a new site in Lidköping. Five years later the whole factory was relocated there. During the post-war period, Rörstrand produced simple stoneware designs by, among others, Gunnar Nylund and Hertha Bengtson (1921–1995), which reflected the contemporary taste for abstract organic forms. During the 1950s the factory also produced a number of informal tableware ranges designed by Marianne Westman (b. 1925), includingMon Amie (1951), Pomona (1956), and Picknick (1956), which incorporated colourful printed patterns of highly-stylized flowers, vegetables and fish. Later in the 1960s Rörstrand manufactured the porcelain Pop service(1968) by Inger Persson Δ Inger Persson, Pop stoneware teapot for Rörstrand, 1969 (b. 1936) available in a variety of colours, the most popular option being a vibrant orange-red. Later Persson won a Design Plus Award for the Spisa range (c. 1978–1980) of high-fired earthenware, which with its brown earth-coloured glaze and simple fluted decoration exemplified the somewhat bland functional yet homely aesthetic of industrially produced Scandinavian ceramics from this period. In 1985, Rörstrand issued a limited edition of teapotsthat were retailed by Rosenthal's Studio House stores. This collection comprised eighty variations by four different designers, including Signe Persson-Melin (b. 1925), who worked as a designer for Rörstrand from 1980 to 1987. In 1983 Rörstrand was purchased by Arabia, then four years later merged with Gustavsberg, and in 1990 was taken over by the Hackman Group. Since then Rörstrand has manufactured innovative ceramics by various Scandinavian designers, most notably Jonas Bohlin and Pia Törnell (b. 1963), which testify to the continuing Swedish delight in functional simplicity and beautiful forms. ![](images/9b3bef360371f9ccc399d892b91dc7ffb68ad3a042ff3123dc3029c94ac69554.jpg) Jonas Bohlin, Qvint tableware for Rörstrand, 2000 ∇> Pia Törnell, Arcus vase for Rörstrand, 1995 > Pia Törnell, Grade tableware for Rörstrand, 2000 ![](images/2dda7833f711ccd00cfc496167eab55dceed7433ff17fde2746e1230b2fb0c7c.jpg) ![](images/36f12fc58b775cfdd17ea25c93505a7cf8166af7951624c97aa4027c5b799e5c.jpg) Anton Rosen, detail of chair designed site-specifically for the Palace Hotel, c. 1909-1910 ![](images/0d11ac578d7c8f72c7376fe95160eb4685b2dafd2605aab06d4c6f9d809b5d09.jpg) > Anton Rosen, stoneware tobacco jar, 1925 #

The Danish architect and designer Anton Rosen wasthe principal architect for the Landsudstillingen (Danish National Exhibition) held in Århus in 1909, and was also responsible for the design of the Palace Hotel in Copenhagen (1907–1910), which was intended to provide luxurious accommodation for officials visiting the nearby Rådhuset (Town Hall). For this latter project, he designed site-specific furniture, including a mahoganyarmchair (now in the collection of the Danske Kunstindustrimuseum in Copenhagen), which incorporated decorative metal fittings that shared a similar aesthetic to Johan Rohde's stylized floral designs for Georg Jensen. Rosen would certainly have been aware of the metalwork being produced by Georg Jensen, as later in 1917 hedesigned a ceremonial silver cup for the Butchers' Guild that the silversmithy executed for him. This unique design was richly encrusted with hand-sculpted figurative elements and incorporated decorative seals suspended from stylized rams heads and an Arts & Crafts style band of galleons with billowing sails. Around this time, Rosen also designed various other silver objects for Georg Jensen that were notable for their use of coral, malachite, amber and other semi-precious stones. During the 1920s, however, he adopted a less ornate style, as demonstrated by his celadon green glazed tobacco jar designed in 1925. Like other Danish designers at the time, Rosen was influenced on the one hand by the emergence of Modernism in Western Europe, and on the other by the art of Japan. With his well-craftedand thoughtfully detailed work, which formed the transition between the New Art of the early 20th century and the Modern Movement, Rosen sought to enhance the beauty of everyday living while reviving a national Danish identity in the handcrafts and design. ![](images/c9ef103f95e5aea0db095c740641be57475e9372fd5219b519ce67d2120f9907.jpg)

A&E Design, 1230 dishwashing brushes for Jordan, 1974 ∇ Hans Ehrich and Tom Ahlström with Clean Shower and toilet chair, 1999 ![](images/640ec2cd88a104c9546ed86791de75271e756d844632e72cab3f9efd7a3af6c7.jpg) # a&e design fond 198 ool en) A&E DESIGN Stockholm - Sweden ![](images/3af164b220a90af5571254e69f22e10dd4186c5923afc6e6e0eff533b2a738de.jpg) One of the leading Scandinavian industrial design consultancies, A&E Design was founded in 1968 by Tom Ahlström (b. 1943) and Hans Ehrich (b. 1942), both of whom had previously studied design and metalwork at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design) in Stockholm. From the outset, this Stockholm-based practice researched applications for plastics while specializing in design for disability. A&E Design has since developed numerous aids and implements for use by the elderly and handicapped – from a lift-and-ride wheelchair for transporting patients to the shower, to bath seats and locking armrests for toilets. The consultancy has also developed other ergonomically-led designs, including tools for Anza and dishwashing brushes for Jordan. A&E Design is renowned for innovative product development using meticulous working models. Unusually, around 20% of the work undertaken at A&E Design is dedicated to the development of its own products, which "seek an optimal balance between aesthetics, function and market”. In 1982, Ahiström and Ehrich founded another design consultancy, InterDesign, that similarly produces models – from mock-ups to fully functional prototypes – in its own workshop. While A&E Design is one of the most eminent design practices in Scandinavia, many of its product solutions are bestowed with attributes more commonly associated with Italian rather than Scandinavian design. Its founders readily acknowledge the influence of Italian designers, most notably Joe Colombo (1930–1971), and have always main tained close links with Milan. A&E Design's bestknown product, the Stockholm // folding stool, which was originally designed specifically for the Nationalmuseum in Stockholm, is now used by weary art-lovers in museums all over the world. Another similarly ubiquitous design by the practice is its queue ticket dispenser that is used widely in supermarkets throughout Scandinavia and Europe. A&E Design has received fourteen Excellent Swedish Design Awards and three Red Dot Awards for Highest Design Quality from the Design Zentrum Nordrhein-Westfalen. ![](images/bad47a2fc2dcbbd2a926417bfdb035fd67007c4071624bd771f43b145fa8d536.jpg) ![](images/295c249e567d9a662f9f449c1b2787310cbfe8266f6ce5078132af5edded3202.jpg) ![](images/5e3419194ddb1c2a753a49c91d0400db3640875534d608c976165a7d53bf511c.jpg) Δ A&E Design, Stockholm I/ folding stool for Lectus Office, 1995 44 A&E Design, Turn-omatic queue ticket dispenser for Esselte Meto, 1991 4 A&E Design, Fresh bath boards for Etac, 1998 ![](images/0687818e6c9bf971c961ff6d5cf9e14304937f4cf87417570527bdbf80435531.jpg) Aino Aalto, Bölgeblick pitcher for Karhula, 1932 – later renamed Aalto andproduced from 1988 by littala ## aino alto (inish 194-1949) ![](images/1bfcbdefa0636d744b4034e0ac197525f5c0b19c5cf1245ca9970dd15911ccd9.jpg) Aino Aalto (née Marsio)studied architecture at the Teknillinen Korkeakoulu (Institute of Industrial Arts) in Helsinki, graduatingin 1920. In 1924, she was employed by fellow architect AlvarAalto, whom she married the same year. They subsequently collaborated closely on various projects, including the development of techniques for bending wood. These researches eventually enabled the Aaltos to co-develop a number of landmark furniture designs that utilized bent laminated wood and plywood. In 1932, Aino participated in a glassware design competition sponsored by theKarhula-littala glassworks. This competition arose partly as a result of the annulment of Finland's prohibition laws (which were enforced from 1919 to 1932) and the glassworks' subsequent expectation of a sudden demand for new drinking glasses. Aino's submission, known as the Bölgeblick(WaveView) pressed glass set, included a pitcher, a drinking tumbler, a bowl, a shallow dish, a sugar bowl and a creamer. The simple yet elegant design of the set, which was born out of AinoAalto's concern for practicality and standardization, was awarded second prize in the competition. The relatively chunky stepped and ribbed forms of the individual pieces helped to disguise the inherentimperfections of pressed glass, while their solidityconveyed a sense of quality not generally associated with utility glassware. With only a few minor modifications, Karhula put the Bölgeblick pieces into production in 1934, with other items such as plates, vases, mugs, schnapps glasses and a decanter being added to the range later. Originally these were only available in either azure blue, brown, green and smoky grey. Later the Bölgeblick set was re-named Aalto and from 1988 it was produced by littala. For the same competition, Ainoalso submitted a proposalfor “art glass” – the ABCD series – which included a vase with a wave-like base for flowers of different lengths. Although this vase did not win any prizes, its unusual form anticipated Alvar Aalto's famous Savoy vase by some four years. From 1932, Aino worked as a freelance designer for Karhula-littala, and the following year she co-founded the Artek furniture company with Alvar Aalto, Nils-Gustav Hahl and MaireGullichsen. In 1933, the Aaltos participated in the Riihimäki glass design competition by jointly designing the 244 series of stacking glasses and vases (which was entered only under Alvar Aalto's name). Having been ![](images/79b48e1810cc14402d5a540f964b8ce9f3febac8afa1800ae97d8193855f2f8d.jpg) awarded second prize, this blow-moulded series became known as the Riihimäki Flower series because of the curious floral shapes the pieces made when stacked. The simplicity and practicality of the series reflected the growing tendency towards functionalism in Scandinavian design, but at the time of its appearance the general public was still not fully accustomed to such starkly pure and undecorated forms. One contemporarycommentator described the Aaltos' Riihimäki Flower series as “modern, matter-of-fact utility which almost resembles laboratory equipment."Aino Aalto was awarded a gold medal at the 1936 (VI) Milan Triennale and continued to collaborate closely with her husband until her death in 1949. ![](images/450bf0539844f0d4cc259f45ddac58b0f33e40ad66b453e3ca47dab4ef037b47.jpg) ![](images/7b7de49982ff5da78d7e329b6efd029295b366243d8036c6a16506db1f1665e4.jpg) △Aino Aalto, Model no. 615 chair for Artek, 1935-1937 △△ Aino Aalto, Aalto bowls and plate for Karhula, 1932 △AinoAalto, design drawings for the Bölgeblick pressed glass range, c. 1932

Blue Fluted (Plain) service for Royal Copenhagen, pattern introduced c. 1775 – This design was inspired by oriental precedents and more specifically a pattern by Meissen. It was re-modelled by Arnold Krog in the 1880s. ![](images/b010bbe187402c6936aa61f5d7ad962551ff9401377bd49fb510343b63790302.jpg) # royal copenhagen ![](images/418e66dfc88f97a3df739570658f2e259dd2e4998f86a669613c6019d35f0403.jpg) (founded 1775, Copenhagen,Denmark) The origins of Royal Copenhagen can be traced back to 1775, when Denmark's first porcelain factory to enjoy royal patronage was established. Following this, over the next four years the factory's chemist Frantz Heinrich Müller (1732–1820) experimented with Bornholm kaolin in an unsuccessful attempt to producehigh-quality biscuitware. Around the same time, the factory's debts were mounting to such an extent that in 1779, King Frederick V stepped in to assume personal financial responsibility for it. The rationale for saving the factory stemmed from his hope of establishing Denmark as one of the leading cultural centres in Europe, and the production of fine porcelain – then known as“white gold” – was one of the surest ways of achieving this. The factory was subsequently re-named Den Kongelige Danske Porcelains Fabrik and a ban was imposed on all imports of European porcelain into Denmark. During the 1780s and 1790s, the factory mainly produced blue-painted services for everyday use, often copying or adapting shapes and patterns from Meissen and other European manufacturers. It also manufactured multi-coloured wares and designs in the Rococo style that were more experimental both technically and artistically. The most notable design to emerge from Royal Copenhagen during this period was the exquisite Flora Danica (Danish Flowers) service, which was commissioned by the Danish court in c. 1789 for presentation to Catherine theGreat of Russia. This famous service, which was ultimately retained by the King of Denmark, was painstakingly decorated with flowers and plants copied from botanical engravingsthat illustrated G. C.Oeder's Flora of the Danish Kingdom. The engravings had been devised by a Nuremberg artist living in Copenhagen, J.C. Bayer, who was employed by the factory from 1776. Impressively, Bayer virtually single-handedly painted all the highly detailed naturalistic floral motifs on the service's pieces, the forms of which were designed by F.L. Bradt. When the Danish court finally ceased production of the service in 1802, it comprised a staggering 1,802 items(1,530 surviving today), making it one of the largest services in the world. During the early years of the 19th century, the patterning of the factory's blue-painted porcelainwas simplified in order to make it easier and less expensive to produce. In 1813, Denmark was facing an ![](images/3d80b462f9c6897d849cb7b78adef46d2a1f481719e384c9aebc04a2a51eced9.jpg) Flora Danica service for Royal Copenhagen, c. 1790 ∇Blå Blomst (Blue Flower) service for Royal Copenhagen, 1779 ![](images/6459d8bd94e695b908643e9c722cbdca0d0bf868d67f1552d352247142445da7.jpg) economic crisis, and the following year its confidence was dented still further by the loss of Norway. During this period, the factory put the development of new products on hold and relied on the production of Rococo style dinner, coffee, tea and chocolate services with its famous Blue Fluted and Blue Flower patterns. It was not until the 1820s, on the instigation of the architect and designerG.F. Hetsch(1788–1864), that Neo-Classical wares in the new Empire style began to be manufactured by the company. Apart from these, duringthe 1830sand 1840sthe factory also produced a number of biscuitware figurines and reliefs modelledon works by the Danish Neo-Classical sculptor, Bertel Thorvaldsen (1770–1844). The superlative quality of Royal Copenhagen's biscuitwareproducts led the factory to become one of Europe's most respected porcelain manufacturers. In 1868, the company passed into private ownership, and over the following years the once state-subsidized enterprise gradually became a profitable commercial venture. Royal Copenhagen was subsequently acquired in 1882 by the engineer PhilipSchou, who had previously founded the successful earthenware works Aluminia in 1863. Two years later, the factory was moved from its premises in Købmagergade to a larger purpose-built sitein Frederiksberg, then a suburb of Copenhagen. Having dealt with the production side of the business, Schou set about revitalizing the artistic aspects of the manufactory. To this end, he hired Arnold Krog in 1884 as a designer, and the following year appointed him as the firm's artistic director. The same year, Krog and Schou went on a study tour of Holland, Belgium, England and France, and while in Paris made the acquaintance of Siegfried Bing (1838–1905), who owned an oriental warehouse in Paris that catered to the increasing demand for Aesthetic Movement objets d’art. On his return to Copenhagen, Krog not only oversaw a renewal of the company's Blue Fluted pattern but also introduced a new decorative technique in which cobalt blue pigment was combined with glaze and then applied to unfired porcelain blanks. The pigment was then gently scraped with a knife to reveal areas of the underlying white material. This layered underglaze painting technique was mainly used for designs that were decorated with motifs inspired by Japanese engravings and woodcuts, such as those illustrated in Siegfried Bing's journal, Le Japon Artistique. Krog was also responsible for the introduction of other eclectic and revivalist styles, including Rococo, Renaissance, Viking, Persian, Chinese and Russian. It was, however, the formal simplicity and delicate abstracted decoration of his Japanese-style wares of the 1880s that had the greatest stylistic influence. Apart from soft cobalt blue, other muted coloured pigments, including a dusky pink, a dark green and a misty grey, were developed to withstand the high firing temperatures used for Krog's innovative underglaze painting technique. During his tenure, Krog recruited a number of progressive artists to produce designs for the factory, including Carl Frederick Liisberg(1860–1909), Carl Mortensen (1866-1945), Marianne Høst (1865– 1943), V.T. Fischer (1857–1928), Gotfred Rode (1830-1878)and Bertha Nathanielsen(1869– 1914). During the early decades of the 20th century, Royal Copenhagen established workshops dedicated to the design and manufacture of stoneware products, such as Krog's Cobra vase (1914), and the development of special glazes. Between the World Wars, the factory became renowned for its production of sculptural figures designed by artists such as ArnoMalinowski, Jais Nielsen (1895-1961), Gerhard Henning(1880-1967) and Knud Kyhn (1880–1969). In 1933, Axel Salto joined the design team at Royal Copenhagen and subsequently developed numerous stoneware art pieces with expressive sculptural forms that predicted the post-war Scandinavian fascination with organic shapes. During this period Nils Thorsson (1898-1975) also designed a number of stoneware objects as well as practical tableware for the company. From the post-war period to the present day, Royal Copenhagen has continued to produce its well-known historic designs, such as Flora Danica and Blue Fluted, alongside more contemporary products, such as the Blåkant (Blue Rim)service(1965) byGrethe Meyer, the shell-shaped Konkylle service (1977) by Arje Griegst (b. 1938), and the Ursula faïence dinnerware(1993) by Ursula Munch-Petersen (b. 1937). Over recent decades, Royal Copenhagen was amalgamated with the Holmegaard glassworks(1975), theGeorg Jensen silversmithy (1986), and the Bing & Grøndahl porcelain factory (1987), and today is part of the Royal ![](images/f1649fbaa28b8b210c771e1b6fdc017e5a489b157e3a6e2f75cba5947ca1cdfa.jpg) Patrick Nordström, stoneware jar for Royal Copenhagen, c. 1915 Royal Copenhagen, wall plate decorated by Bertha Nathanielsen, 1903 ![](images/49b1604995b58b34464c48c625f9e7a9630a10df93647595034633e430244cb4.jpg) ![](images/e79fde96140b019460dd82642d72dc4445e92ed04b7801a19c7da81be3980d14.jpg) ▶ Ole Jensen, Ole citrus press/jug for Royal Copenhagen, 1995 >>Ursula Munch-Petersen, Ursula service for Royal Copenhagen, 1993 Scandinavia Group owned by Carlsberg – the brewery that has dominated Danish cultural life for over a hundred years. Like so many other Scandinavian design-led companies, Royal Copenhagen manufactures high quality industrially produced domestic wares, such as the Ole citrus press/jug (1997) by Ole Jensen (b. 1958), alongside exclusive art pieces. For over two hundred and twenty-five years, the company has been at the forefront of the Scandinavian manufacturing industry and has played a vital role in defining and promoting a Danish identity in the applied arts. ![](images/0c8334c3c3bdcbef06ac1b36c20f5beca1f184c93695086ff8358d7baf6edef8.jpg) ![](images/0821689f6416ef00da1d104ab31f6d039faaef55b7db39320ef3d3729dabb121.jpg) Sixten Sason, Saab 92, 1947 ## ![](images/fed153c4947ecbc8c3071e6c2a93ee68dfcde61159a0ed3875b59c191950d9be.jpg) The history of the well-known Swedish car manufacturer Saab can be traced back to the Svenska Aeroplan AB (Swedish Aircraft Company), which was founded by the financiers Marcus Wallenberg and Axel Wennergren in response to the Swedish government's re-arming initiative prior to the outbreak of the Second World War. Having recruited some forty engineers from Boeing, this early manufacturing venture initially produced versions of Northrop and North American aircraft before developing its own designs, such as the Saab 17 bomber, the Saab 21, which featured the world's first ejector seat, and the Saab 29, which was the first European swept-wing fighter. In 1945, the Swedish Air Force stopped purchasing aircraft, so Saab decided to transfer its skills and expertise to the design and production of automobiles instead. The company assigned fifteen of its aircraft engineers to the development of its first car prototype. Because the engineers had never worked on such a project before, they were “entirely unfettered by the conventions and traditions of the established automobile industry”, and as a result, developed a completely different kind of car with front-wheel drive, a transverse engine, a strong safety cage and an aerodynamically shaped stabilizing wing. This remarkable prototype – now known as the Saab 92001 (1947) – formed the basis from which Saab's first production car evolved. With a truly revolutionary streamlined monocoque body that was styled by Sixten Sason and engineered by Gunnar Ljungström, the Saab 92 was wholly influenced both formally and technically by aeronautical design. It was also the first small fourseater “people's car” to be produced in Sweden and reflected Sason's design philosophy that while form should follow function, products should not look too utilitarian. The Saab 92 evolved over the following decades into other Saab models, including the well-known Saab 95 estate car (1959) and the highly successful Saab 96 (1960), which was one of the company's best-sellers with more than 547,000 sold within a twenty-year period. Later, Sason and Ljungström developed a new car, the Saab 99, which was compact yet spacious inside, aerodynamically efficient, and economical to run. Launched around 1968, the Saab 99 introduced a number of innovative features over the following years, such as headlamp cleaning (1970), selfrepairing bumpers, heated seats (1971) and sideimpact door beams (1972). Bjorn Envall, who assisted Sason with the styling of the Saab 99, once tellingly remarked, “It's a very dangerous thing if you're getting too serious. The exterior of the car should tell you something. It should speak to you somehow." This remark reveals a very Scandinavian perception of design – one that understands the importance of imbuing a product with a gentle aesthetic that communicates fundamentally humanist goals. The Saab 99 also became the blueprint for the company's later cars, including the Saab 90 (1985) and the Saab 9-5 SE (1997). During the 1970s the company began producing “medium segment" cars, and later in the 1980s began manufacturing even larger vehicles for the executive market. Having previously merged with the truck manufacturer Scania in 1969, Saab eventually came under the aegis of General Motors in 1990, when the American automotive giant acquired a 50% stake in the company. Typical of a Scandinavian company, Saab is committed to sustainable development – from the reduction of emissions to the minimization of "resource use”. The company also operates a highly ethical social programme, which includes the sponsorship of ![](images/9188e9137ddec05a827db9716d50fbd62ff6476adbafee7b23414cee645a4d09.jpg) various environmental projects and the running of its own rehabilitation centre, known as the Component, for staff who “fall victim to disease, accidents and work-related injuries”. Throughout its history, Saab Automobile AB has combined design, performance and safety in the development of its high-quality and unmistakable vehicles, while its sister company Saab Aircraft AB manufactures both commercial and military aircraft that are based on the continuing application of innovative engineering concepts. Δ Sixten Sason's Saab 92 in front of the Saab J21 plane, c. 1948 ![](images/38945c9d5a8f6fee6c3ba378052767c3eef62100ebe2befd856d5724a28ed42a.jpg) ![](images/70a433b80d3bbd2f173bbe2da8c9e274c68784a768e1c52ef8c501c31f954d3d.jpg) △ Saab 90, 1985 ∇ Saab 9-3 convertible, 2002 ![](images/c53cb59354e316e4b26005c7800413e34eda56e4648844b7730e51b6e547f32f.jpg) 9 a6 ![](images/cf55e9bc99de7ffccdb4d05f6fc7fa8cfd1142e7779fdbc06e7d05fd2fd200e3.jpg) Δ Saab J-29F Tunnan, Swedish Airforce F3 Wing, introduced in 1948 and operational from 1951 to 1956 ∇Saab2000(SE-003) airplane demonstrated at Farnborough Air Show, 1992 ![](images/eb3fd801d9fb6467ad9bc73d4a2aba86d3a03e4d7e6a2ebfce3ed464908c32c2.jpg) ∇ Saab Viggen with Sidewinder and Sky Flash missiles, c. 1982 – This plane was introduced in 1967 and has been operational since 1971 ![](images/81e150dbb368a01ca51d3a5f8be5063d01dbe30ec84b76809ba342102b7c355b.jpg)

Eliel Saarinen, wooden chair with heart motifs, 1909 - This design shows the strong influence of the British Arts & Crafts movement, especially the work of Charles Rennie Mackintosh and Charles Voysey ![](images/dc40a060ae690a1f9dc4316730fd99e8ea3bd6430a8591104ba5edbf9f0d28a1.jpg) # eliel saarinen (b. 1873 Rantasalmi, Finland – d. 1950 Bloomfield Hills, Michigan, USA) ![](images/4216011aa7f03e470d5909b8e10cbb6c69ea5cc1aa24e696df791781148ae16a.jpg) One of the great figures of Finnish design, Gottlieb Eliel Saarinen studied fine art at the University of Helsinki and later architecture at the Helsinki Polytechnic, alongside Herman Gesellius(1874– 1916)and Armas Lindgren(1874–1929). After graduating in 1898, the three young architects formed a partnership and were subsequently commissioned to design the Finnish Pavilion for the 1900 Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale”. With its Karelian-style roof-line and ceiling painted by Akseli Gallén-Kallela(1865–1931), the resulting building exemplified the emerging National Romantic Movement. In reaction to the political suppression of Finland by Russia and the intensifying programme of “Russification” of Finnish culture, Saarinen and other protagonists of National Romanticism sought to define and promote an autonomous national character within all the arts. Looking to ancient Karelian culture for an untainted Finnish identity, they embellished and romanticized historical fact in order to create the mythology of “Karelianism”, which was a projection of their own nationalistic aspirations. Gesellius, Lindgren and Saarinen were also strongly influenced by the Glasgow School and the Wiener Sezession, as the bold massing of elements in their buildings demonstrates, for example the Finnish National Museum (1902-1912)and the Helsinki railway station (1904-1919), the latter of which Saarinen alone designed.By the turn of the century, the practice was inundated with architectural commissions and the three architects decided to flee Helsinki for a rural retreat. Their destination was a sixteen-acre site on a forested ridge above the shores of Lake Hvitträsk, where they each built a house around a central grassed courtyard and a large communal studio that connected the Saarinen and Lindgren houses. Each house was an intensely personal Gesamtkunstwerk– the interiors of the Saarinen House included furniture, lighting, textiles, wall paintings and carpets, all designed site-specifically by Eliel Saarinen. In 1905 Saarinen subsequently purchased Lindgren's share of the estate, and later in 1916 he acquired Gesellius'. During this period, Saarinen continued to promote the National Romantic Movement, and in 1912 he joined the Deutscher Werkbund. In 1922, he won second prize in the Chicago Tribune Tower competition and a year later, the Saarinen family emigrated to the United States (although they returned almost every summer to Hvitträsk until 1949). Initially, they resided in Evanston, Illinois, while Saarinen worked on the development of the Chicago lake front. The family later settled in Ann Arbor, where Eliel Saarinen was appointed a visiting professor of architecture at the University of Michigan. In 1923, he met the wealthy newspaper baron and philanthropist George C. Booth, who asked him to draw up plans for the development of the Cranbrook Educational Community. In 1925, Saarinen moved to Bloomfield Hills and in 1932, the Cranbrook Academy of Art was officially founded with Saarinen as its first president. Under his directorship the Academy became the premiere design institution in America and produced many of the country's most talented designers, including Charles and Ray Eames. As director of the Cranbrook Academy, Saarinen promoted the humanist ethos of Scandinavian design and had an enormous impact on the evolution of modern design in the United States.Saarinen's only son, Eero, became an architect and was one of the greatest pioneers of American post-war design. ![](images/3fc145ff01ff4b08304e2c414b65ff8c1c99d5acaa51e08b1870535411f16d63.jpg) △Eliel Saarinen, watercolour interior design for a country house in Kirkkonummi, 1902 ![](images/b36931acd99323a0ae4c2bb1423d4d0c1e027daf7a511b47e06a8109ead6bbe7.jpg) Grup I N: 46 "Reusu" bliel Taavinon. ![](images/3f605422f7a7f8e9f0248b3f576fa4bb4643af462dcd3a94c4e9404e41f0d3f9.jpg) ![](images/b63b1f6275b671c79d4f0174edafff9564389ecb54039e01ae5018821a1f01cc.jpg) 44Eliel Saarinen, design for Ruusu (Rose) ryjiy rug, 1904 △ South wing (Saarinen House) of Hvitträsk buildings viewed from the west (lakeside), 1906-1907 Eliel Saarinen, watercolour interior design of library at Suur-Merijoki, 1903 eli s7n ▶ The studio at Hvitträsk – Eliel Saarinen believed that "Work is the key to creative growth of the mind." ![](images/98eb74239ca0b0032746c96a8252ce07770489fc3b7fae70d44e239698cb3c5a.jpg) ∇ Living room in the Saarinen House at Hvitträsk Eliel Saarinen, chair from the Koti (Home) collection, 1903 – This unusual design alludes to Karelian artistic tradition and as such can be seen as an expression of Finnish patriotism ![](images/fbee9cc8944740cecfa24f404983513160aae2b370cea1358197b93cedd25676.jpg) ![](images/f6f22ed2c5370c65e5fc103e98a6eca8614c4782ebc3f75ab5e805f9e83d14ca.jpg) ![](images/83cc260e361321cb87ed3d099b759082025585c1ee27dc7bbc76442497a2a865.jpg) Timo Salli, Tramp chair (prototype) for Cappellini, 1996 ## timosalil 1 siki,Filand) ![](images/c9e340ef14b4155139119a126557240e979d859b91a1e1a5e3e7e342a0ff8a2e.jpg) Well-known amongthe international design community for his innovative and thought-provoking design work, the Finnish designer TimoSalli began his working career as a welder. He later studied furniture design at the Lahti Design Institute, graduating in 1992, and then studied craft and design at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki, graduating in 1996. While still at college, he worked in the design office of StefanLindfors before establishing his own practice in 1993. Three years later, he designed the Tramp chair for Cappellini, which was constructed from a two-piece steel frame covered with a transparent nylon net bag. Although large in scale, this design “gives a light and immaterial illusion”. In 1997 he designed the quirky Jack-in-the-Box television that can be closed away into its box when not in use, so as to produce “space for human action without TV's dominating, catching eye”. The same year he also designed the collapsible and semi-transparent Zik Zak chair as a one-off for the Snowcrash exhibition in Milan. Following this, in 1998he collaborated with Ilkka Suppanen on the design of the Nomadhouse exhibition. Salli's work, which playfully contests the conventions of daily living, continues to exemplify the cutting edge of contemporary Scandinavian design. Describing his work as “low-tech choices for a digital era”, Salli seeks to make more direct and meaningful connections between objects and users, particularly within domestic realms, where according to him our homes have become “so full of things we no longer even see”. His designs are intended to challenge our perceptions of everyday “relics”, with many being multi-functional, such as the Lamp-Lamp (2000) – a mirror that also functions as a light. Like other contemporary Scandinavian designers, Salli is very aware of changes in living patterns, especially within the home environment, and so he also promotes his designs as “basic living equipment for the urban nomad”. As one of the designers associated with Snowcrash, Timo Salli demonstrates through his idiosyncratic designs an originality and inventiveness that verge on the poetic. ![](images/ae172cfb87929fc156f97fda3a33c0d458c3ee4d9743c2716d216b70065a8a57.jpg) Timo Salli, ScreenLamp light (prototype), 1998 ∇Timo Salli, Jack-in-the-Box television (self-production), 1997 ![](images/2428d60947065cfaa0f92a906c6723e86eb06a291725da5a2627560e5ff186bf.jpg) ![](images/d9c3872e6b1f89b1aea397ab812be0f62f58c3cfa1641b6e3cd608ed1c9af281.jpg) TimoSalli, Firecase fireplace (prototype), 2000 ![](images/2f2f813326b17eeb1213054ff6b8d9d24c71305340c4248ceeca76548c6f4b46.jpg) Axel Salto, stoneware vase with Solfatara glaze for Royal Copenhagen, 1943 ## ![](images/fa8bbe0e581abd0ce4d30ba80230937b3310030ba05b9a7ca499f737df148246.jpg) The Danish ceramicist Axel Salto studied painting at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) in Copenhagen before co-founding the art periodical Klingen in 1917. From 1923 to 1925, he worked as a designer for Bing& Grøndahl alongside Jean Gauguin, the son of the celebrated French Post-Impressionist artist. At Bing & Grøndahl, Salto executed several biscuit porcelain designs embellished with enamelled decoration, which were exhibited in the Swedish Pavilion at the 1925 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes”. These objects generated much interest, especially in the French press. Around the same time he began collaborating with the ceramicist Nathalie Krebs (1895-1978)on stoneware art pieces, which were produced by Saxbo – a small independent pottery that Krebs had established in 1930. These stoneware vessels were instrumental in the development of the classic Saxbo style that relied on simple forms and mottled oriental-style glazes. Like other Danish designers, Salto was deeply influenced by the formal and aesthetic qualities of antique Chinese ceramics. In 1933, he joined the design team at the Royal Copenhagen porcelain factory and worked alongside the skilled potter Carl Halier. Over the succeeding years, he modelled numerous powerfully expressive stoneware designs that possessed distinctive formal and decorative qualities. His bold ceramic pieces were distinguished by simple yet sculptural and often organic forms. Some recalled the abstracted shapes of pine cones and seed heads and featured speckled litchenous Solfatara glazes that produced a real sense of mossy, damp and earthy nature. Others possessed soft “ideal” forms decorated with incised and repeating fluted patterns. Apart from being one of the leading ceramic designers in Denmark during the inter-war years, Salto also devised patterns for book-binding papers (1934), designed a number of printed textiles for L.F. Foght (1944), and directed the restoration of the frieze (designed by Jørgen Sonne in 1846) that adorns the Thorvaldsens Museum in Copenhagen. He exhibited his work widely and received several prestigious awards, including two Grand Prix at the 1925 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes” and the 1937 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts et Techniques dans la Vie ![](images/7a72bbd2f295c261ad541d0c34d5d8655a9b5da57ca2b7c2697615c9cacf84ed.jpg) Moderne”, and another Grand Prix at the 1951 (IX) Milan Triennale. He also wrote and illustrated a number of books, including AxelSalto's Keramik (1930), and illustrated several publications by other authors. Salto's evocative stoneware designs, with their organic forms and mottled glazes, powerfully expressed the abstract essence of nature and signalled a revival of studio pottery within Scandinavian ceramic design. ![](images/58208cd54ed9c0060f0b84c3a7c4f1408ab488dc9f7af3a76ddb1594b941d2c9.jpg) ΔAxel Salto, unglazed stoneware vessels inlaid with differently coloured chamotte clay for Royal Copenhagen, C. 1950 4Axel Salto, stoneware vase with Solfatara glaze for Royal Copenhagen, c. 1943

Astrid Sampe, detail of Jobs Blommor (Jobs' Flowers) textile- blue version, c. 1950 ![](images/8c637783ab98706e30196cfc770e191a0f0e7926a3c1a9633936a423c94ab83a.jpg) >Astrid Sampe, Versailles polyester and viscose textile for Almedahls, 1972 # astrid sampe (swedish 19092002) ![](images/da871128d8d7b22035f3c22dbf21f8de58a7e569b69bfaa350efb15068a0c317.jpg) The Swedish textile designer Astrid Sampe trained at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design) in Stockholm and later at the Royal College of Art in London. In 1937 she began working as a designer for Nordiska Kompaniet and exhibited at the“Exposition Internationale des Arts et Techniques dans la Vie Moderne". Ayear later, she became head of Nordiska's newly-founded textile design department, where she produced a number of influential designs that testified to her skill as both a colourist and a pattern maker. She later worked with the architect Sven Markelius (who had also produced designs for Nordiska) on the design of the Swedish Pavilion for the 1939 New York World's Fair. Like other designers working in the 1940s and 1950s, Sampe revitalized the long Scandinavian tradition of woven “art” textiles with her vigorous abstract patterns designed within a modern idiom. Sampe is also credited with being the first textile designer to introduce glass cloth to Sweden (1946), and was particularly influential in the development of textiles designed specifically for architectural settings. She was awarded a Grand Prix at the 1954 (X) Milan Triennale and two years later was the recipient of the Gregor Paulsson trophy. In 1948 she co-wrote withVera Diurson the book Textiles Illustrated, and the following year was elected an honorarymember of the Royal Designers for Industry in London. She also designed textiles for Knoll International in New York and Donald Brothers in Dundee, as well as for companies in Sweden. During the 1950s, Sampe executed striking patterns for the Wohlbeck and Kasthall carpet factories, which were instrumental in revitalizing the industrial production of woven rugs. In 1955, she produced several designs for domestic linens that employed repeating geometric patterns and folk-inspired motifs that could be used in an interchangeablemanner. In 1972, Sampe leftNordiska Kompaniet and established her own studio in Stockholm so as to concentrate on the design of textiles and interiors. Alongside a number of other prominent Scandinavian designers, Sampe adapted the craft ideals of the past for the industrial reality of the present and in so doing preserved and extended the traditions of Nordic textile design. ![](images/be43335883910294f460990665fc04764ee7317dfd99aad26cdcbe89ba3d627c.jpg)

![](images/88e4e50b11bc564224a61f07764af211ae0e2df7ccbf01f3e3ce53d76f15e026.jpg) FRAMHLID. Gudjón Samúelsson, architectural design for Yfirbygd Sundlang in Reykjavik, 1925 ▶Gudjón Samúelsson, white painted chair, 1926-1930 ![](images/8edb9f27db45a3d3e40830aa626bd49a0b7d8600dda0e5c84e527ddd1f5a32b3.jpg) PVERSKURDUR. # gudjón samúelsson i 10o) ![](images/363d7fb6a7739d8a708ed112dd1d61780228bf4da6d00f8936ffd64d98a1f72c.jpg) The greatest protagonist of Modernism in Iceland, Gudjón Samúelsson studied architecture at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) in Copenhagen under Martin Nyrop (1849–1921), the leading figure behind the National Romantic movement in Denmark. In 1919, Samúelsson was appointed the first National Architect of Iceland and subsequently designed a number of important public buildings. These were chiefly built using reinforced concrete – a material that had been imported into Iceland from the 1900s due to the lack of local construction materials. Samúelsson's buildings were constructed in a modern style that in Iceland became known as Steinsteypuklassík (concrete classicism). For his building projects, which included the Landsbankinn (National Bank, 1922-1924)andtheLansspítalinn (National Hospital, 1925-1930), Samúelsson designed site-specific furniture, such as his white painted chair (1930) made from imported pine. This design epitomized his curious synthesizing of Neo-Classical forms with Modern Movement functionalism. In the mid-1930s, Samúelsson also designed furniture for the Icelandic Parliament Building, including chairs whose rather austere forms were enlivened by subtle vernacular detailing that made reference to traditional Icelandic woodworking. Samúelsson's furniture was manufactured locally and because of the lack of available materials, was somewhat stylistically constrained. Based on rationalist principles, his buildings and furniture must be seen as distinctive national interpretations of Modern Movement precepts. Inspired by the functionalist approach to design, Samúelsson pioneered, along with others, such as the architect Sigurdur Gudmundsson (1885–1958), a new modern identity for Icelandic design that subtly embraced rather than rejected both national vernacularism and Scandinavian Classicism. ![](images/d35917e02d8ff64456a2fc1a2a61326458cacd3476b34da3d9f582d1054a6111.jpg)

![](images/ec95b4ee08f1b8f84fd8e5ae8bc37ebd5cd5ff16e4651c92c7086b5f34a2432d.jpg) Thomas Sandell, T. S. chairs for Asplund, 1994 # thomas sandell (b. 1959Jakobstad, Finland) ![](images/282c95d863bd5e93df4f10406a3d064d568909ec01d1205a26523723e1baa7ad.jpg) Thomas Sandell was born in Finland but his family moved to Sweden the same year. Between 1981 and 1985, he studied architecture at the Royal Institute of Technology in Stockholm and then worked in the architecture practice of Professor Jan Henriksson. In 1986 he executed illustrations for the Metallarbetaren magazine and worked on a number of saloon car projects for Volvo. The following year he headed a consulting group for the rebuilding of the Royal Library in Stockholm, and a year later collaborated with fellow Swedish designer Jonas Bohlin on the design of a Shaker-style interior for the Rolfs Kök restaurant. Having foundedhis own architecture anddesign practice in 1990, Sandell has since worked on numerous building and interior design projects, notably the Villa Börjesson in Aspö (1990), the café for the National Museum of Cultural History in Stockholm (1993), and interiors for the Modern Museum (1995)and the Architecture Museum (1996)in Stockholm. Apart from his architecture and interior design work, Sandell has also designed furniture for a number of leading manufacturers, including Artek, Asplund, B&B Italia, Gärsnäs and Källemo. Since the early 1990s Sandell has also designed furniture and various domestic products for Cappellini, including his wall-mounted Key Cupboard (1992), which takes the form ofan out-sized escutcheon, and his well-known polished aluminium 9235candleholders (1995). In 1995, he was one of several designers who contributed to the popular PS series of furniture for IKEA –his PS Armoire and PS Bureau with their cut-out drawer/ door pulls and wheels for easy transportability epitomize the strong functional and aesthetic clarity not only of his own work but that of contemporary Swedish design in general. Also in 1995 he cofounded with Ulf Sandberg the SandellSandberg partnership – a concept company with a broad approach to communication that unites advertising, branding, architecture, design and the Internet. More recently, Sandell has designed the London headquarters of Ericsson (1997), the “Insideouthouse” for Wallpaper\* magazine (1998), and interiors for the Swedish Parliament building in Stockholm (2000). He has received a total of five Excellent Swedish Design Awards and has acted as chairman of SAR(National Association of Swedish Architects) since 1999. Alongwith Thomas Erikssonand JonasBohlin'swork, ![](images/ef665f0d5fced872c3fe8b46a74d4fbd5afb0425608cbc48c63ad0bf65614591.jpg) Sandell's simple, practical yet stylish designs have greatly influenced the new-found confidence and vitality of contemporary Swedish design. 4 Thomas Sandell, Snow cabinet storage system for Asplund, 1993 - co-designed by Jonas Bohlin Thomas Sandell, Wedding stools for Asplund, 1992 ∇∇ Thomas Sandell, Air carpet for Asplund, 2001 ![](images/6ddc6435f2c30a2f8cc60c31f8b5d806e66d49227c1e91db36ee0c936fe8c43e.jpg) ![](images/e45b3e858358b69185dd1dde99714dd11408ca77e4f6eedd484390e7fe24b5b0.jpg)

![](images/3592fdb478c51d11943ae88d43fc96f35354be6ceb579fc0311063ff4478af5b.jpg) Timo Sarpaneva, Festivo candleholders for littala, 1966 # timo sarpaneva (b. 1926 Helsinki, Finland) ![](images/3755ecdddff6ca08f8f670072ed49addabbef0a80d836aa308f08e4a62fcb9c7.jpg) One of Finland's most prominent designers, Timo Sarpaneva studied at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design), Helsinki, from 1941 to 1948. He won secondprize in a glass competition held by the Riihimäkiglassworks in 1949 and the following year became a product designer and head of the exhibition section at the renowned Karhula-littala glassworks. During the early 1950s, Sarpaneva also worked extensively as a textile designer, receiving a silver medal for his textile designs at the 1951 (IX) Milan Triennale. He also taught textile printing and design at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu from 1953 to 1957, and briefly worked as art director for the Porin Puuvilla cotton mill from 1955 to 1956. It was, however, his ground-breaking glassware designs that brought him the greatest international acclaim. One of his first technical innovations at littala was a steam blowing method, which was used for his early work, such as the Kajakki (1953), Maailmankaunein (1954) and Linnunsilmä (1953) vessels, as well as for his delicately colour tinted thin glass plates known as Aquarelles. These remarkable art glass pieces reflected a new sculptural confidence and creative vibrancy in Finnish design. During the mid- 1950s, Sarpaneva introduced his i-Glass range, which attempted to bridge the gap between expensive art glass and utility glassware. This industrially produced product line comprised seventeen items that were available in several colours, including lilac-grey, blue-grey, smoke-grey and green-grey, which could be used together to produce different effects. The 1950s and 1960s saw a great dynamic synergy at the littala glassworks, with Sarpaneva and Tapio Wirkkala vying good-naturedly with each other to create new forms and techniques. One of Sarpaneva's most interesting and influential glass designs from this period was his Finlandia series of vases (c. 1964), which incorporated a technique using moulds made from slabs of roughly hewn and carved alder wood, which were charred by the red-hot glass. The patterns resulting from this technique bore the close imprint of the wood's structure and powerfully evoked a sense of icy rawness. The nature of this process also ensured that each design was unique, as each time a mould was used it was burnt further, meaning that the patterns would be changed. As Leena Kattunen noted in the catalogue that accompanied the 1992“Make GlassNot War” exhibition at the 4Timo Sarpaneva, Model no. i-401 and Model no. i-403 decanters from the i-Glass range for littala, c. 1955 & C. 1956 ![](images/4dac76fa8a950ece4becc43350e83d550c70f2258e9afb266c5030533da6cc9d.jpg) ![](images/7e46a88c401246630202ff7bcc61b2e17d054595bd54c0be7280cf283f5612a1.jpg) Timo Sarpaneva, Suomi teapot for Rosenthal, 1974 ∇ Timo Sarpaneva, enamelled cast-iron casserole with teak handle for W. Rosenlew & Co., 1960 ![](images/58bcc04989c1ef7fd61fd4e6f8706d5a3a84d4fd2181dcc259af55347c914bd5.jpg) ![](images/ddaa6353cc089ceae5127a913aac19e93c65b496f0762b0698b7f871391525b5.jpg) Δ Timo Sarpaneva, Arkipelago candleholders for littala, 1981 Suomen Lasimuseo(Finnish Glass Museum), “In the vases of Timo Sarpaneva's Finlandia series ... Sarpaneva has liberated himself from the strict discipline of design and progressed to self-awareness." The charred wood technique was also used to produce Sarpaneva's well-known Festivo candleholders(1966), but because of the expense of continually replacing the wooden moulds, metal moulds were eventually used instead. Both the Finlandia and Festivo ranges were highly influential and heralded the fashion for industrially mass-produced “frosted” glassware in the 1970s. Sarpaneva later developed other glassmaking techniques like that used for his Archipelago range (1978), in which different sized “trapped” bubbles produced a solid ice-like effect. For his Claritas range (1984), he used a similarly innovative technique that trapped and suspended large bubbles within a clear glass casing, while his elegant Marce/ series (1990) incorporated a new blow-moulding process that pushed the material to its very limits. Many of Sarpaneva's designs used closed “round-square” forms that relate to the smooth pebbles of the Indian Tantric cult, most notably his Claritas vases for littala and his cast-iron cooking pot with wooden handle (1960) for W. Rosenlew & Co. Other designs, such as his large Lasiaika glass sculptures, incorporated more open and expressive forms. In 1963, he was made an Honorary Royal Designer for Industry in London and in 1967 received an honorary doctorate from the Royal College of Art, London. In tribute to his achievements he also received a State Award for Industrial Art from the Finnish Government in 1985. Beyond his exquisite glassware for littala, Sarpaneva has also designed ceramics for Rosenthal, metalware for W. Rosenlew & Co. and Opa, textiles for Kinnasand, Finlayson Forssa and Tampella, plastic wares for Ensto, and other glassware for Corning and Venini, as well as books and theatre sets. Like so many other Finnish designers, Sarpaneva has worked with equal skill in the realms of both exclusive one-off studio production and industrial mass-production. Through his supreme mastery of materials and artistic sensibility, Sarpaneva's work evokes the spiritual essence and raw beauty of nature, and in so doing has had a crucial influence on the development of Finnish glass design for over fifty years. ![](images/64349be879942b58d4b5424a7069e21a14c7e88716db30c4e8c0281b1fc9d3be.jpg) ![](images/cbb9990613d38e8cc2f7a6b81ce25e2c73679d8c33ea3f1011e2d9530aeb0c97.jpg) Δ4 Timo Sarpaneva, Finlandia vase for littala, 1964 ![](images/73e69cbb50b6decd491bd6658b9eee434a26a8ddc492b16d6f0549ac97242e7a.jpg) Δ> Timo Sarpaneva, Finlandia vases for littala, 1964 – These remarkable vases were made using wooden moulds that were charred by the moulten glass. Basically cast from “life" these designs captured the essential spirit of the natural world and were hugely influential on mainstream glass design during the 1970s. 4Timo Sarpaneva, Finlandia vase for littala, 1964 ![](images/f7e0f5167240fadcecf245484a9d0af26bc4d5bb9493fb5d044b75bde65e1523.jpg) △ Timo Sarpaneva, Kajakki (Kayak) bowl/sculpture for littala, 1953 Timo Sarpaneva, Lansetti /// (Lancet III) vase for littala, 1952 ![](images/d3a09a977466c95dc70d87e814faf65093f2a3c370f7c89ea03b37396b5477e4.jpg) ![](images/f5aa96380d04812945efbcfceecefd76cd8e3d5d11fc1da685155a3d6a99a6a4.jpg) 4 Timo Sarpaneva, Orkidea (Orchid) vase for littala, 1953 ∇ Timo Sarpaneva, Claritas vase from the Pro Arte collection for littala, 1984 ∇∇ Timo Sarpaneva, Marcel vase for littala, 1990 ![](images/27f37899340485d9062c16f01b5da6cfa89edd6257e24f8258a5316ac606e322.jpg) ![](images/41b4963c94343d91d5d2260c72d705532ef5db23cf4f6a95b09d5f93152046fa.jpg) ![](images/e68625d54f4353837b68ba3ff2d69ebc7b19551f8fa06235de1d073c5a5315b5.jpg) Thomas Sandell (Stockholm Design Lab), new livery for the SAS fleet of aircraft, 1998 ## sas (founded 1946, Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/7e47f337bfd716b2abef850cf9bfb43764aa4baa1c0f1874222ad2a6a6c95546.jpg) SAS (Scandinavian Airlines System) was established in 1946 as a consortium of three national Scandinavian air carriers– the Danish airline Det Danske Luftfartselskab(DDL); the Norwegian air carrier Det Norske Luftfartselskap (DNL); and the Swedish airline AB Aerotransport – for the coordinationof joint transatlantic operations. InSeptember of the same year the “Dan Viking”(the first DC-4 tobe painted in the distinctive SAS colours) made its maiden transatlantic flight from Stockholm to New York via Copenhagen. Later in the 1950s SASbecame a high-profile ambassador of “Scandinavianess”with its eye-catching blue and white logo and the chic uniforms of its aircrew. The company helped promote its image even further by commissioning Arne Jacobsen to design a new landmarkterminal-cum-hotel adjacent to the central railway station in Copenhagen. The resulting Royal Hotel and SAS Terminal (1956-1961)was a flagship International Style building, which incorporated state-of-the-art communications systems and interiors that exemplified the cool understated elegance of post-war Scandinavian Modernism. Jacobsen conceived the project as a fully integrated Gesamtkunstwerk (a total work of art), which included numerous site-specific furnishings, from his well-known Egg and Swan chairs (1957–1958) to specially designed door handles and cutlery. In the late 1950s SAS restated its commitment to progressive design by commissioning Sigurd Persson to develop a forward-looking grey plastic in-flight service. One of the greatest pioneers of Scandinavian industrial design, Sigvard Bernadotte was also responsible for the design of an in-flight tray and cutlery for the company. Later in 1988 Ergonomi Design Gruppen designed an innovative and stylish plastic in-flight coffee jug for SAS that minimized the possibility of spillage. The following decade, SAS began a comprehensive programme of change with the intention of raising its levels of competitiveness and profitability, and a new updated corporate identity programme was instigated in order to strengthen the airline's brand recognition. Introduced in 1998, this corporate image makeover included new-look aircraft (both inside and out), new Euroclass lounges designed by Thomas Eriksson, a modernized logo, and new typography, graphics, colours and uniforms. All of this was intended to convey “the basics of Scandinavian design: simplicity, functionality, informal elegance and a respect for materials and resources” and to accentuate the airline's profile. In 2001, the new SAS corporate identity won the main category of the Core Design-Corporate Design Awards, testifying to the airline's continuing patronage of contemporary Scandinavian design and its commitment to “developing new products and concepts to increase the focus on customers in the areas of quality, service and comfort”. ![](images/0cd88322b408bf299535eb9e29526a8c771fa1b64ee177d24c320a3da4234f01.jpg) Δ Arne Jacobsen, entrance lobby of the SAS Royal Hotel, Copenhagen ∇ Ergonomi Design Gruppen, SAS in-flight service coffee jug for SAS, 1988 ![](images/1383c4f3ad3da8cf8d36c43cfda51555394fa05e81eee6b42144bf9be60a49c8.jpg)

![](images/c8fb40b69562247cb4febf39356d51272de3129538c590b3de467b9aed7b0304.jpg) Sixten Sason, Zig-Zag sewing machinefor Husqvarna, 1953 # sixten sason (b. 1912 Skovde, Sweden – d. 1967) One of the first Scandinavian industrial design consultants, Sixten Sason initially trained as a silversmith and studied with Swedish artists while living in Paris. He later trained as a pilot and then worked for Husqvarna making motor-cycles and small arms. In the 1930s he worked as a graphic designer and magazine illustrator. During the Second World War he joined Saab, then a fledgling aircraft manufacturing company, where he was initially employed as a technical illustrator. Later, he was appointed the company's designer-inchief – a position he held until his death. Around 1945Saab decided to diversify, and its chief engineer Gunnar Ljungström and his team began developing the company's first car, the Saab 92 (1947). Inspired by aeronautical design and American streamlining, Sason transformed this frontwheel-drive vehicle into a streamlined vision with integrated side panels, bonnet and boot – an innovation that waslater adopted by Pininfarina and Raymond Loewy (1893-1986). The Saab 92 was Sweden's first small car and became the blueprint for the company's subsequent models, such as the best-selling Saab 95 estate car (1959), which Sasson also designed. He later developed with Ljungström the ground-breaking Saab 99 (1969), but sadly died before it went into production. As one of the earliest European proponents of streamlining, Sason also designed several highly influential products for Electrolux, including the bulletshapedModel 248 vacuum cleaner (1943). In 1948, he developed the compact yet elegant housing for the 1600F Hasselbladcamera through the use of simple block-like wooden prototypes. This landmark design, which featured an easy-to-use arrangement of operating buttons, was the world's first single-lens reflex camera with interchangeable lenses, magazines and viewfinders, and when first launched at an exhibition in Cleveland it caused a sensation. Ergonomically refined and superbly functional, the seminal 1600F quite literally set the highest standards for camera layout and handling and quickly became a favourite choice among professional photographers. During the 1950s, Sason was also responsible for the design of various appliances for Husqvarna that were similarly aesthetically and functionally resolved, such as his Zig-Zag sewing machine of 1953. Unlike many designers in America, Sason used streamlining not solely for aesthetic purposes, but as a means of ![](images/92c22c2669a1e1c2338741547859c094d568da82a291d057e932fc4dbe153d1a.jpg) 4 Sixten Sason, design for industrial washing machine for Electrolux, 1950 ∇ Sixten Sason, concept design for Saab 92 automobile, 1947 enhancing his products' performance. By skilfully synthesizing engineering and styling with a concern for high build quality and product durability (both functional and aesthetic), Sason was instrumental in developing a distinctly Scandinavian approach to industrial design. ![](images/c845301e420243e168c35a6faf7295f44f38e89e41ba0e9fa683be618d3ead63.jpg) ![](images/5156c9b9b42e9a3fe71083b85eac0c1ea4a9021b551533b87587082df43c49a9.jpg) Sixten Sason, design for vacuum cleaner attachments for Electrolux, 1944 ∇Sixten Sason, design for Model no.248 vacuum cleaner for Electrolux, 1943 ![](images/b161f8a8c6407f306c1bc77c4592776798551590a46b105461b8da87c45d2743.jpg)

![](images/d90b23edc83814800c0bfa65cae30bc3cfcd9eaa6fab855c72978e8c6abec013.jpg) ![](images/0df5828ab087a16d4a6a2c0902dee7856f64d4b420d6fae52a5980dc72cb80d6.jpg) Teppo Asikainen & Ilkka Terho, Netsurfer computer work station for Snowcrash, 1995 # snowcrash (founded 1998 Helsinki, Finland) The Stockholm-based Snowcrash design company is renowned for its innovative and experimental products, which are born out of an approach that aims to go “beyond the obvious to explore contemporary needs and equip contemporary lifestyles”. The business evolved from an earlier design group known as Valvomo (Crisis), which was founded in 1993 by a collective of young Finnish designers and architects who had known each other since their student days. The original membersincluded Teppo Asikainen (b. 1968), Vesa Hinkola(b. 1970), Markus Nevalainen (b. 1970), Kari Sivonen (b. 1969), Ilkka Terho (b. 1968), Jan Tromp (b. 1969), Rane Vaskivuori (b. 1967), and Timo Vierros (b. 1967). In 1997 the group exhibited their work together with designs by Ilkka Suppanen and Timo Salli at the Milan Furniture Fair under the name of Snowcrash – reflecting not only their Nordic origins but also their desire to overthrow the “bent plywood” heritage of Scandinavian design in favour of “visionary things for a network age”. The following year Snowcrash was officially founded in Helsinki as a design-led organization that would oversee the development of furniture and lighting by both in-house and independent designers. Since then Snowcrash has launched a number of radical products, including the Netsurfer computer work station(1995) by Teppo Asikainen and Ilkka Terho (a tongue-in-cheek attempt at reconciling computer technology and furniture within the domestic environment); the Globlow lamp (1996) by Vesa Hinkola, Markus Nevalainen and Rane Vaskivuori (a “living light” that inflates when switched on and deflates when switched off); the Airbag chair (1997) byIlkka Suppanen (an inflatable chair/mattress for indoor/outdoor use); and the Soundwave panel system(1999) by Teppo Asikainen (a system of sculptural wall panels designed to enhance the acoustic properties of interior spaces). The Snowcrash brand was purchased by Proventus AB in 1998 and its operations were subsequently moved from Helsinki to Stockholm in 2000. The same year Snowcrash became part of Proventus Art & Technology group (which also owns Artek and Kinnasand), and since then has built up an international network of collaborating designers, including the Paris-based Israeli designer Arik Levy (b. 1963) and the Swedish designer, Monica Föster (b. 1966). Apart from considering practical needs, Snowcrash “aims to address values such as well-being, creativity and even playfulness”, which its members consider to be equally important for a humane workspace. ![](images/46ab604bd8140695ff531fcb1439323d76c05e01fe72a9c20e5440dfe1de45a5.jpg) Vesa Hinkola, Markus Nevalainen & Rane Vaskivuori, Globlow floor lamp for Snowcrash, 1996 ∇∇ Teppo Asikainen, Chip lounger for Snowcrash, 1996 ∇Ilkka Suppanen, Airbag chair for Snowcrash, 1997- codesigned with Pasi Kolhonen Teppo Asikainen, Soundwave sound-proofing panel system for Snowcrash, 1999 ![](images/e25877ba055ad8809818eca9946cdbd0e12672f74d3f0c6fe6936de149116065.jpg) ![](images/3244efb6ee6fa447898c79f320304f3e81f1b75f5ffe3fd5c3738401a57a8386.jpg) ![](images/51aa2e7b67b277deb06d060d1da1b49504fe3bb1549471ef6d64c6ef67402f1e.jpg) ![](images/425f828869a75138cdaf98d06f977fedc8bb7842b319fe24ea71a4f0e4b13f4c.jpg)

Carl-Harry Stålhane, Unic glazed stoneware vases for Rörstrand, c. 1950 ▶Carl-HarryStålhane, glazed stoneware vase for Rörstrand, c. 1948 ![](images/6d61647755fe4eaa43ef5aa853518793d2bea3b05a59a47fbfa00542d01c60ad.jpg) # carl-harry stålhane ## (b. 1920 Mariestad – d. 1990 Lidköping, Sweden) ![](images/2fbda2a7676bb2b639c7243f9b2c30676c523a585d2f3dc54c90aa0a8eac9fb9.jpg) The Swedish ceramicist Carl-Harry Stålhane trained as a sculptor under Ossip Zadkine (1890– 1967) at the Académie Colarossi in Paris. His studies also took him to Spain, Greece, Turkey, Egypt, Mexico and the UnitedStates. In 1939, he began working at the Rörstrand ceramics factory, initially assisting the painter Isaac Grünewald(1889-1946)in the production of colourful tin-glazed earthenware objects decorated with Fauvesque motifs. By 1948 he was designing his own ceramics, which were inspired by Oriental art and which explored, like the work of other contemporary ceramicists at the time such asBerndt Friberg, the ultimate in “classical” pottery forms. His soft-edged vessels with mottled matt glazes marked an important stylistic departure for Rörstrand and typified the new understated style of ceramics that began to emerge in Scandinavia during the immediate post-war years. In 1951 he was awarded a gold medal at the (IX)Milan Triennale for his highly influential work. By the early 1950s, Stålhane was also designing ceramics with geometric motifs, such as his Abstrakt series, which reflected the influence of contemporary French and Italian abstract art as well as Japanese raku pottery and calligraphy. Apart from his studio pieces, Stålhane designed functional domestic tableware for mass production, such as his SB, Blanca, and Vieta dinner services. During the 1960s, his work became increasingly sculptural and larger in scale, often functioning more as ceramic sculpture than as domestic pottery. Between 1963 and 1971 he taught in the ceramics department at the Konstindustriskolan (School of Arts & Crafts) in Gothenburg and in 1973, having designed ceramics for Rörstrand for forty years, established his own studio, Designhuset, in Lidköping, where he was assisted by Kent Ericsson. Beyond his work for Rörstrand, Stålhane also designed a simple hand-painted decorative motif for the Delfi tableware range manufactured by Bing & Grøndahl, the shapes of which were designed by Martin Hunt (b. 1942). Like so many other Scandinavian designers, Stålhane skilfully straddled the worlds of both craft manufacture and industrial mass production in pursuit of “ideal” forms. ![](images/d61623e13a11a5b04a47efadeb1d865555fd2845ecef95684d563d0eb8c6b656.jpg) ![](images/bc225e7400627562af0c335e9650c18daf90e689f40bd63894942bfadcf513d4.jpg) Arne Jacobsen, teapot from the Cylinda Line stainless steel hollowware range for Stelton, 1967 ## stelton fo rp, Denark ![](images/94c160f65796f68652fb18623b04ba6b3a4e4e2213c22915321885b8be733782.jpg) Renowned for its elegant metal and plastic products, the domestic ware manufacturer Stelton was founded in 1960 to sell the stainless steel hollowware – sauceboats, platters, salad bowls etc. – manufactured by the Dansk Rustfrit company based in Fårevejle, a little town in Northwest Zealand. The products, which were of mediocre design, were intended by Stelton to form the basis of a range of contemporary housewares aimed primarily at the Danish market. Salesman Peter Holmblad(b. 1934)was hired to market the line and in 1964, with a view to raising the company's profile, he persuaded the well-known architect and designer Arne Jacobsen to design a range of hollowware for the firm. Jacobsen subsequently produced some sketches of a few cylindrical forms that were “terse, logical and functional”, but the technology needed to translate his ideas into threedimensional products did not yet exist: stainless steel is an extremely demanding material to work, as its surface can become easily distorted if it is not handled in exactly the right way. Consequently Stelton first had to develop new machines and welding techniques, before it could produce Jacobsen's Cylinda Line, which was eventually introduced in 1967. In recognition of the superior quality of its design, the Cylinda Line was awarded an ID Prize by the Danish Society of Industrial Design in 1967, and the International Design Award by the American Institute of Interior Designers in 1968. The Cylinda Line initially comprised around 18 pieces, but Jacobsen continued to add new designs up to 1971, while others were introduced posthumously from drawings. Today, the range comprises 34 pieces in total. Described as “products which live up to the demand for a beautiful, functional, and practical hollowware range in contemporary design”, the success of the Cylinda Line can be largely attributed to Jacobsen's ability to express the inherent qualities of stainless steel through highly rational yet aesthetically pleasing, timeless forms. From 1976, Erik Magnussen also worked for Stelton and designed a number of well-known table pieces in both stainless steel and plastic, including his classic threepiece vacuumjug set of 1977. The understated elegance and high object integrity of Magnussen's products, has like Jacobsen's, enabled them to remain utterly impervious to the whims of fashion. The company's range today also includes designs by Peter Holmblad (the managing director and owner of Stelton since the late 1970s), such as his Model no. 100–15 house plant watering can, which has a similar rational aesthetic to the Cylinda Line. Stelton's high-quality products exemplify Danish design and are represented in the permanent collections of museums around the world, including the Museum of Modern Art and the Cooper-Hewitt Museum, New York, the Victoria & Albert Museum, London, the Neue Sammlung, Munich and the Kunstindustrimuseum, Copenhagen. ![](images/a8f9a217f085a957c4a4e431a1e03b08a91cbec4d635d7e812c9abf6b718573e.jpg) ![](images/c263f9de59cc542c16e63685fbb369172ee4dd3335c945b892329f69c10e18f2.jpg) △ Erik Magnussen, Model no. 460 cake knife, Model no. 462 pizza servers and Model no. 463 fish server for Stelton, 1980, 1990 & 1990 Arne Jacobsen, ice bucket and ashtray from the Cylinda Line stainless steel hollowware range for Stelton, 1967 ![](images/53cb63a38a9f4fbe7ac06df96a7959698c1a684fb994af1bc10ea24e80091969.jpg) Erik Magnussen, Model no.2000 vacuum jug, Model no. 2001 creamer, Model no. 2002 sugar bowl and Model no. 2005 serving tray for Stelton, 1999 ∇ Erik Magnussen, Mills spice jars and Model no. 580 salt & pepper grinders for Stelton, 1999 ![](images/0c704f8a1801cae1dc1963436dc5faaf95d8ddca03e73818e9de43561490dbc3.jpg)

![](images/6044fbd2833a8258b2af2906c82ee6c13ea11549f2928445615515309eb59f74.jpg) Magnus Stephensen, armchair for Fritz Hansen, 1932 # magnus stephensen (b. 1903 in Frederiksberg – d. 1984 in Helsingør, Denmark) ![](images/c8919544973776a0add13318b322d5f9cb57529022e309a388151bf24f000320.jpg) Magnus Stephensen studied at the Teknisk Skole (Polytechnic), Copenhagen, until 1924 and later trained as an architect at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts), Copenhagen, graduating in 1931. He also briefly studied architecture at the French School in Athens. Following his training he designed a number of buildings, but mainly concentrated on domestic architecture, which revealed his preference for precise and uncluttered lines. Between 1938 and 1952he designed tableware, including several flatware patterns and a teapot, for Kay Bojesen's workshop, that was remarkable for its stylish sophistication yet “serviceable utility”. He also began designing metalware for Georg Jensen in 1950, which led to hisTanaquil and Frigate cutlery patterns. In 1954, he designed his exquisite sterling silver saucepan, the handles of which were particularly notable for the way they made carrying easier. The same year he also designed a stainless steel coffee set as well as a range of high-quality stainless steel casseroles and saucepans for Georg Jensen. These beautifully crafted objects were made by drawing the steel plate over rotating steel cores and then hand polishing the forms so as to produce the highest quality finish. Because the steel plate used was of a sufficiently thick gauge, the rims of the pieces did not require additional reinforcing and sowere left straight-edged. Later, Stephensen designed the Patella range of oven-to-table ware(1957) for Royal Copenhagen, which featured a speckled brown glaze inspired by the colour of a withered oak leaf. The glaze also had the added benefit of effectively covering any iron spots in the earthenware, thereby reducing the number of rejected “seconds” in manufacture. Similar to his stainless steel hollowware for Georg Jensen, this product range was distinguishedby soft yet rational forms. The Patella range also heralded the trend towards more informal mix-and-match multi-functional tableware. Like many other Danish designers at the time, Stephensen was strongly influenced by Japanese art (to such as extent that he even wrote a book on the subject), and because of this many of his products possessed a quiet and understated beauty. His domestic ware also exemplified the Danish traditions of high quality craftsmanship, precision engineering and practical utility. Over the course of his long career Stephensen won numer- ![](images/3d1d23ed539c8bee66706efd632d3013a04922d2565b50a830a50247e7c0816a.jpg) ous awards for his excellent product designs, including three gold medals and two Grand Prizes at the 1954 (X)and 1957 (XI)Milan Triennale. ![](images/275f48d3bc4838951709956cafa79709b6c8399588fe2f32fb89f22d69daf093.jpg) △Magnus Stephensen, silver entrée dish and coffee pot with plaited wicker handles for Kay Bojesen's workshop, 1938 4MagnusStephensen, silver tea-pot with wickerwork handle for Kay Bojesen's workshop, 1938.

![](images/b8dc61b7709b0428cf88389e623c8eed307a0bdf3ffc4f90064648a9687c8978.jpg) Alvar Aalto, Model no. 41 Paimio chair for Artek, 1931-1932 # alvar alt . ae . si, Find) ![](images/c966fec52ae0f9228086626b28d1e17c9b05f177987ce90a72eaf236f43f4e23.jpg) The son of an engineer who specialized in geodetic surveying, Hugo Alvar Henrik Aalto is Finland's most celebrated architect/designer and one of the greatest pioneers of organic Modernism. At an early age Aalto showed an interest in and aptitude for the fine arts, and went on tostudy architecture from 1916 to 1921 at the Teknillinen Korkeakoulu (Institute of Industrial Arts), Helsinki. After completing his studies, he worked as an exhibition designer and travelled extensively throughout central Europe, Italy and Scandinavia. In 1923 he established his ownarchitectural practice in Jyväskylä, which was later moved to Turku (1927– 1933) and Helsinki (1933–1976), and the following year he married the architect AinoMarsio who subsequently became his closest collaborator. Between 1924 and 1929 they conducted numerous experiments in the bendingof wood – a material that occurs in natural abundance in Finland. This crucial research later enabled Aalto in the 1930s to develop several revolutionary chair designs, which came to symbolize a completely new direction in Scandinavian design. It was his work as an architect, however, that first brought Aalto international recognition. In 1927 he designed the Turun Sanomat Building in Turku, which was his first functionalist building, and two years later he codesigned an exhibition to celebrate Turku's 700th anniversary – his first complete modern structure for Scandinavian public display. These early yet important schemes were followed by a number of highly acclaimed architectural projects, including the Viipuri Library(1927–1935), the PaimioTuberculosisSanatorium (1929-1933)and the Finnish Pavilion for the 1939 New York World's Fair. Unlike his Modernist contemporaries in Germany and Italy, who promoted the use of synthetic materials such as steel and glass in furniture design, Aalto turned to laminated wood and plywood as his materials of choice, and in 1929 began investigating veneer bonding and the limits of moulding plywoodwith Otto Korhonen, the technical director of a furniture factory near Turku. These experiments resulted in Aalto's most technically innovative chairs, the No. 41(1931-1932) and the cantilevered No. 31 (1931–1932), both of which were designed contemporaneously with or as part of his Gesamtkunstwerk scheme for the Paimio Tuberculosis Sanatorium (which to this day still treats lung patients). These comfortable yet functional designs ![](images/e28e904d090ff61e32b1c5f9e519eb33e116360aad29bc2bf0d5317b367ebffa.jpg) ![](images/01e060ba7cfc3e0466cb12e032540dd8eed2b4ddb5033d7bb4584137ad098a36.jpg) ![](images/9feb92ef97eff03531d2e32216729500c92e87b1245108fa6858e5d5f0e08015.jpg) ![](images/e01eb82fd90a32f37fa09cbc83197d1b3e01d65ff823a125d2d279cca661704b.jpg) ## SPECIFICATIONS OF MODELS ILLUSTRATED ABOVE ROUND TABLE. Plain birch - £1 15 0 (25 inches diametcr) Curled birch 2 10 0 SPRING CHAIR. Plain birch or black, red or blue seat £2 17 60 Curled birch 4 0 STOOL. Plain birch or black, red or blue top 9 0 Curled birch 13 6 Dining Chair (Model 21) - £2 0 0 Verandah Chair (Model 51) - £1 12 6 Cafe Table (Models 81-5) from £1 2 6 Write or 'phone VIC. 2844-5 for free illustrated literature describing other Finmar models and address of your nearest dealer. Awarded the Certifcate of the Royal Instituts of Public Hcalth and Hygiene. FINMAR LTD., 44 RANELAGH RD., GROSVENOR RD., S.W.I ![](images/1e4caf6f24e5e2d5a64998c61d5ea500507ec4bddc85994d9de839f0a9888f7b.jpg) Finmar advertisement for Aalto furniture, c. 1935 – Finmar was the British subsidiary of Artek ∇ Alvar Aalto, hybrid armchair for Huonekaluja Rakennustyötehdas, 1931-1932 FURNITURE OF THE FUTURE FOR THE HOME OF TO-DAY signalled immediately to the international avant-garde a new direction in materials towards plywood, and the emergence of a softer, more humane vocabulary of form. The success of his furniture established Aalto as one of the pre-eminent designers of the 20th century, and led him and his wife to found the manufacturing company Artek in 1935. Aalto believed that his most important contribution to furniture design was his solving of the age-old problem of connecting vertical and horizontal elements. His bentwood solution developed in conjunction with Korhonen, and which Aalto dubbed “the little sister of the architectonic column”, allowed for the first time legs to be attached directly to the underside of a seat without the need for any framework or additional support. This novel technique gave rise to his series of L-leg(1932–1933), Y-leg (1946-1947) and Fan-/eg (1954) furniture. Apart from producing landmark furniture designs and buildings, Aalto also worked as a freelance designer for the Riihimäki (1933) and littala glassworks (1936). Like his furniture and architecture, Aalto's glassware designs are notably characterized by the use of organic forms – the Savoy vase of 1937 being his most famous. Originally entitled, ![](images/c302ba0d95437ebf2efb752971ff422bb2c0d9d5810372dd96496341c1654f05.jpg) ![](images/a7d3d42103bd90d3fd3766170d969443f41c71a08f224745e77c5f206895a136.jpg) Alvar Aalto, interior of the Finnish Pavilion at the 1939 New York World's Fair ∇ Alvar Aalto, Model no. 98 tea trolley for Artek, 1935-1936 ∇ Alvar Aalto, Model no. 31 armchair for Huonekaluja Rakennustyötehdas, 1931-1932 - later manufactured by Artek ∇Dining room on display at Bowman Bros., London, with furniture designed by Alvar Aalto for Artek, c. 1938 ![](images/08085834f61afcb1a7685f8cecc0716859d80ce0632b08f7aa691356faefc6fe.jpg) ![](images/be7041356a9fdcd950f5b1f62238109ad89d48ca9c6af2529f34ec087f8ebe0b.jpg) ![](images/c59abd2ab457b42f91acf2bd41f8f1b0cd744ae62cd1c32e88d9542b8f936d9f.jpg) ![](images/5f97a84804d84ea293187be153cf0cf8996a7e5ded01487a562215219ef5566c.jpg) Δ Alvar Aalto, armchair for Huonekaluja Rakennustyötehdas, Turku, c. 1931-1932 Alvar Aalto, entrance foyer of the Paimio Sanatorium, 1929-1933 OVERLEAF Alvar Aalto, main entrance of the Paimio Sanatorium, 1929-1933 Alvar Aalto, Model no. 3031 Savoy vase for Karhula, 1936 – later produced by littala “Eskimoerindens skinnbuxa”(Eskimo woman's leather trousers), the Savoy vase was reputedly inspired by the lake shorelines of his native Finland. Its wave-like form may also have been a play on his surname, which translated literally means “wave” in Finnish. Certainly the rhythmic asymmetrical form of the Savoy vase did capture some of the abstract essence of nature and in so doing, predicted the amorphous shapes that became so identified with Scandinavian design during the post-war years. Aalto believed strongly that design should be humanising, so he not only rejected severe geometric forms but also, in the case of furniture, man-made materials such as tubular metal, because for him they were inappropriate to the human condition. Aalto's work was especially wellreceived in Britain and America during the 1930s and 1940s, and as one of the founding fathers of organic Modernism, his design philosophy was highly influential on post-war designers such as Charles and Ray Eames.
