As an early opponent of the Modern Movement's adherence to an alienating machine aesthetic and a stringently rationalist design approach, Aalto stated, “The best standardization committee in the world is nature herself, but in nature standardization occurs mainly in connection with the smallest possible units, cells. The resultis millions of flexible combinations in which one never encounters the stereotyped." Aalto's pioneering organic designs not only provided a new vocabulary of form, they also eloquently represented to the general public what was widely regarded as the acceptable faceofModernism. In 1952, he married the architect Elissa Mäkiniemi, with whom he collaborated until his death. Aalto's life and workwere celebrated by the Museum of Modern Art, New York, through three exhibitions held in 1938, 1984 and 1997. Inspired by the relationship that exists between mankind and nature, Aalto's holistic and human-centric approach to design provided one of the key philosophical foundations from which Scandinavian design evolved and flourished. ![](images/1add15d83ec9de942ed8c60f182453f18208cf96eb7d37a69693451f65bfd51a.jpg) ![](images/547c76370657002f2b486edc56af5385121f62175401f9d6b1cf5fd1524b39b7.jpg) ![](images/981d48d0918627a1614b72f954a84cbe91c5803baee2df6b9a00745ad72ba290.jpg)

![](images/adcb278b228bc27ed3331f9931cbeb15002fddbf3e730746f239201d75f7a188.jpg) NannyStill McKinney, Harlekiini (Harlequin) glassware range for Riihimäki, 1958 # nanny still mckinneyFiishb, 126) ![](images/c89b34e59d194f118af95d15621820bb0701ff2e35fad1f8a586717cae23a8bb.jpg) The Finnish designer Nanny Still McKinney studied at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki from 1945 to 1949. Upon completing her studies she joined the design team at the Riihimäki glassworks. Her work there included both blown “art” glass and moulded production glassware that was distinguished by unusual forms, textured surface decoration and functional utility. As Still McKinney noted, “Practicality has always been extremely important to me. I cannot make impractical objects. You must be able to wash them, and a beer glass must sit comfortably in the hand." At Riihimäki she also felt “a special urge to create colour”, so in collaboration with the chemical laboratory there she developed a number of memorable colours, including a brilliant turquoise that had been inspired by a shard of glass she found on a beach while holidaying in Capri. Her subsequent product designs, such as the cobalt blueHarlekiini range(1958), which included covered jars, jugs and glasses, and the warm amber coloured Ambra candlesticks (1961), which were included in the“Finlandia” exhibition that toured Europe (1961–1962), signalled a new and brilliantly coloured phase in Finnish glass-making.Some of Still McKinney's later blow-moulded glass designs, such as her Fantasma vases(1968–1971)and her daisypatterned decanter and shot glasses (c. 1970), were remarkable for their highly textured surfaces and bold relief decoration that reflected the informality of the period. She continued designing glassware for Riihimäki until 1976, when the glassworks discontinued its production of blow-moulded glass to concentrate solely on the mass-manufacture of commercial glass, such as bottles and jars. Although Still McKinney adopted glass as her material of choice, stating “It has many more aspects than anything else – stone, marble or bronze”, she revealed her extraordinary versatility as a designer by working in a number of other materials, such as metal, enamel, plastics and wood, and other disciplines, including ceramics, graphics, jewellery andlighting. Her polished teak and cane salad servers of 1954, for example, testify to her remarkable sense of line and handling of materials, while her Good Morning breakfast set (c. 1964) for La Porcelaine de Baudour – with its “tongued” lids to enable better control while pouring – reveal an innovative approach to ![](images/0174d5436902b5ce00c341d9fa9b48e9f5a65d9dbe51fb2e9527febaf67057db.jpg) functional problem solving. Still McKinney also designed ceramics for Cerabel-Porcelain, Heinrich Porzellan, and Rosenthal, glassware for Val Saint-Lambert, plastic products for Sarvis, and cookware for Hackman. In 1972, she was awarded a Pro Finlandia Medal for her original designs “that came straight from the heart”, and which also exemplified the uniquely stylish yet informal approach to product design in Finland. ![](images/e78eb33adfa64c9e215e9456a8a0f75571f552d8cc4b7e03e8c923c2a1802c57.jpg) △ Nanny Still McKinney, teak and cane serving spoons and forks (self-production), 1954 4Nanny Still McKinney, Good Morning flame-resistant breakfast tableware for La Porcelaine de Baudour, c. 1964 ![](images/4e510241647a481a6f94e21f043b6389d6249e1aee4c29fba307ecbfd543eee7.jpg) Peter Opsvik, Balans Duo chair for Stokke, 1984 ## stkke fond 193 e,Nway ![](images/2d985fd75b2a23acaa609a4df5583b5baa41ecba4a1e95dd02effd19a45f59b0.jpg) Stokke, the largest and most prestigious furniture company of Norway, evolved from a small business that was founded in 1932 by Georg Stokke and Bjarne Møller. Its “humble production” of chairs and sofas was significantly upgraded in 1936 when a new factory was established that enabled much higher rates of production. In 1955, Georg Stokke acquired sole ownership of the company and it subsequently became known as Stokke Fabrikker AS. A sister company, Westnofa, was founded in Åndalsnes in 1968 to specifically cater to the domestic seating market, while Stokke concentrated on the development of other types of furniture. In 1972, Stokke launched the Tripp Trapp high-chair/chair designed by Peter Opsvik, which could be height-adjusted so as to in effect “grow with the child” from the age of six months to eight years old. Since its introduction, over three million of these revolutionary chairs have been sold worldwide. Later, Opsvik began research with Hans Christian Mengshoel (b. 1946)into dynamic seating solutions based on ergonomic principles, which resulted in the introduction of the remarkable Balans Variable sitting tool in 1979. Based on the company's philosophy of “movement and variation”, thisinnovative rocking seat evolved from the concept that furniture should stimulate a good sitting position and freedomof movement, while providing enough flexibility for a variety of body sizes. The Balans Variable and other seating designs from the Balans range are renowned for promoting excellent posture, and as such are particularly recommended for backache sufferers. During the 1980s, Stokke produced Opsvik's unusual Garden Seat and the playful Ekstrem chair designed by Terje Ekstrøm (b. 1944), which function as much as interactive sitting sculptures as ergonomic seating products. In 1999, Stokke launched a new children's furniture concept designed by Grønlund & Knudsen known as Sleepi, which transmutes from a crib to a bed to a sofa or two chairs. Like the hugely successful Tripp Trapp chair, this innovative design can be adapted to the children's needs as they grow and underscores the Stokke Group's belief that “good design is good business”. Stokke's specialization in ergonomic seating and innovative children's furniture typifies the Scandinavian attitude towards not only health issues, but also children's development. It is interesting to note that as early as 1901 the progres- ![](images/ed31177b58d81c07a40af3133af4b4b56f79ef048b70c9e70fa1a75577a22199.jpg) sive Swedish social reformer Ellen Key(1849– 1926)was predicting Barnets århundrade (The Century of the Child), and that in Norway today there is a government ombudsman specifically concerned with the rights and interests of children. 4 Per Øie, Move stool for Stokke, 1984 – allows full freedom of movement through 360 degrees. ∇ Terje Ekstrøm, Ekstrem chair for Stokke, 1984 - designed so that the sitter can adopt a number of sitting positions while still maintaining full back support – first launched in 1987 ![](images/d66385405781dc122a717e3d1d4c1f3a0064d57f891e622585357d1aa3d7aef9.jpg) ∇ Peter Opsvik, Multi Balans folding sitting tool for Stokke, 1982 ![](images/6e0a84bf9a7fcc420a0725e03d2902d4597398328249795070be0d95f69e6cf4.jpg)

![](images/07bccfcab05543e45cb1daa4e240eae062fc0bb15af4bb31e9a3aed239d66e0e.jpg) Ilkka Suppanen, Nomad chair (limited edition/selfproduction), 1994 # ilkka suppanen (b. 1968 Kotka, Finland) ![](images/8065713d430698699c899e64be73687d753d1e4e1b0635bc01d5376bf8062a7e.jpg) One of the leading avant-garde Scandinavian designers of his generation, Ilkka Suppanen studied architecture and design at the Polytechnic and the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki, graduating in 1989. He later completed his design studies at the Rietveld Academy, Amsterdam, in 1992and the same year exhibited at the Fifth International Exhibition of Architecture at the Venice Biennale. In 1995 he began teaching at the Faculty of Design in Helsinki, and the following year designed the innovative Airbag chair for Valvomo, an inflatable chair/ mattress for indoor/outdoor use that can be folded away or hung up when not in use. He also became a founding member of the Scandinavian design co-operative Snowcrash in 1996 and devised the group's trademark. He later teamed up with Timo Salli in 1998on the design of the Nomadhouse, which included the Nomad chair (1994) with an undulating felt sling seat. The Nomad project focused on how “people are driven by two conflicting impulses: the desire to move and the desire to stay. ... Nomadic designs are made from as few materials as possible. They disassemble easily and are readily transported. They must be extremely functional." The same year he designed a sofa version of the Nomad chair for Cappellini known as the Flying Carpet – so called because of the way the seat section appears to hover in the air. Suppanen also received considerable recognition when he presented his work with Snowcrash at the 1998 International Furniture Fair in Milan. Utilizing materials that “demand and create new forms”, such as synthetic fibres and copper cloth, Suppanen designs highly innovative product solutions that are practical yet possess a strong aesthetic identity. As an independent designer, he has worked for, among others, Cappellini, Proventus, Nokia and Saab, and was nominated by Ettore Sottsass for the Dedalus prize for young European Designer as one of the four most important young designers under forty. Believing that “design is like poetry: absolute and precise, with the minimal use of means employed to achieve the maximal result”, Suppanen's nomadic and frequently multi-functional designs are primarily concerned with exploring the relationship “between the transitory and the timeless”. In 2000 he became the creative director of Snowcrash while continuing to operate his own design practice in Helsinki. ![](images/9cf16583b835039431c476ff5c7751a35e5dae3705e48fc846f4e8c164ce7fc0.jpg) His work has also been selected for the permanent collections of numerous institutions, including the Stedelijk Museum in Amsterdam and the Museum für Angewandte Kunst in Cologne. ![](images/a4ac2b7855974b286e628a90ecb621ed950c6d6bd889c11b66961381f8e47da7.jpg) △IlkkaSuppanen, Roll-light floor lamp for Snowcrash, 1997 △ Ilkka Suppanen, AV Rack cabinet (self-production), 1998 ∇Ilkka Suppanen, Game-shelf table for Snowcrash, 1999 ![](images/43b36deb17ac513749c217a4623d0aba0525a78f37ddf225a98562084cc492f3.jpg)

limari Tapiovaara demonstrating his Wihelmiina stacking chair for Wilhelm Schauman, c. 1959 ▶ IImari Tapiovaara, Wilhelmiina stacking chair for Wilhelm Schauman, 1959 ![](images/81d4b8cc9ecea38f45884f17eeb3b4399fbc4f8edff631866b14d37b85c6bf3e.jpg) # ilmari tapiovaaralms 194-10 ![](images/7cf9ac2f120e6d827b001ad1237586cf87d1931d4286211249a322f6cbfe1fa7.jpg) Throughout his career, Ilmari Tapiovaara dedicated himself to the design and development of the “ideal" multipurpose chair. From 1935 to 1936 he worked in the London office of Artek, a designled company founded by Alvar and Aino Aalto. He later studied industrial and interior design at the Taideteollisuuskeskuskoulu (Central School of Industrial Design) in Helsinki, graduating in 1937. Following this, he served an apprenticeship in the Paris office of Le Corbusier (1887–1965) before working as the art director of Asko (a furnituré manufacturer based in Lahtí) from 1937 to 1940. In 1946, he designed his well-known solid birch and moulded plywood Domus chair (1946) sitespecific for the Domus Academica student hostel in Helsinki. This pioneering knock-down design was manufactured by Keravan Puuteollisuus, a furniture company established and directed by Tapiovaara. Around 1950, he co-founded with his architect wife Annikki Tapiovaara (b. 1910) a design office in Helsinki, and subsequently worked as a freelance designer developing various “universal" seating solutions. As a functionalist and one of the most important pioneers of knock-down furniture, Tapiovaara believed particularly strongly in the principle of revealed construction. With an eye cn the ever-increasing furniture export market, he concentrated in the late 1940s and early 1950s on developing low-cost, efficiently constructed chairs that could be easily dismantled and packed for overseas shipment – an example of which he submitted to the 1948 “International Competition for Low-Cost Furniture Design" at the Museum of Modern Art, New York. His later tubular metal and plywood stacking Lukki / chair (1952) for Lukkisep po, which was first used in the Olivetti Showroom in Helsinki, featured a plastic cord wound around its arms that not only afforded the user much comfort, but also functioned as a protective device when the chairs were stacked. His elegant Wilhe/mjina chair (1959) for Wilhelm Schauman, with its moulded plywood seat and back elemerts, was similarly inventive in the way it could be stacked. He was awarded six gold medals in total for his chairs at the 1951 (IX). 1954 (X). 1957 (XI) and 1960 (XII) Milan Triennale and received a Good Design Award in Chicago in 1950. Apart from designing highly rational furniture, which also included pieces for Knoll International, Thonet, and Asko, Tapiovaara worked extensively as an interior ![](images/06032576304aa2b5a2727a24668ba263a8c9c0e91a1abb5b95f1904c967cb408.jpg) ![](images/1fa2ffac815ef3797b15de25494110f893ddd7119b8c366f4c1681caf7fcf676.jpg) ∆Ilmari Tapiovaara, ebonized wood and leather armchair, c. 1947 – This chair was designed for the Museum of Modern Art's “International Competition for Low-Cost Furniture Design” (1948)and was later distributed by Thonet △Ilmari Tapiovaara, Domus stacking chair for Keravan Puuteollisuus, 1946 - Later produced by WilhelmSchauman, this chair was retailed under the names Stax and Finnchair in Britain and America respectively Ilmari Tapiovaara, Domus stacking chair for Keravan Puuteollisuus, 1946 ![](images/83729d493be7d67c24bcac1d47b8b5dac6544c2277efedcbdd4115bfe91e9554.jpg) designer and extended his skills into other product disciplines, including lighting, glassware, stainless steel flatware (Hackman), stereo equipment, carpets, textiles, wallpaper and toys. Between 1950 and 1952, he headed a department at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki and from 1952 to 1953was a visiting professor at the Institute of Design, Illinois Institute of Technology in Chicago. During this American sabbatical, he also worked in the Chicago design practice of Ludwig Mies van der Rohe (1886– 1969). In 1971 he received the Finnish State Design Award, and later a prize from theFinnish Culture Foundation in 1986. Tapiovaara was one of the first Finnish designers to work extensively abroad and once noted, “Being a designer is rather like being a doctor: once you have the professional skill, you can practice wherever you like. If what you do is good, it's good everywhere." Having been directly inspired by some of the greatest names of the Modern Movement, Tapiovaara evolved a distinct language of design, which, while rooted in functionalism, imparted a sense of timelessness and character to his work. ![](images/2f582a416350801e0f892a8792c05d94bcd087d3436dad4db4d1e6c83a3583c5.jpg)

![](images/a91391e68441f747ba9ffa4dba6c38c213d76c1c12c48cec4d9dc335050db123.jpg) First Tetra Pak packaging, 1951 ∇Ruben Rausing, founder of Tetra Pak # tetra pak0 1  eden ![](images/e482278b85e02cf9a914342c262859085e0e1803fa15ad65a9598f7d60b42d4b.jpg) ![](images/61e515e62b5cfed930c627066367fd31b0d9c6470d2010464da7fe41bd7a23bb.jpg) A world leader in food packaging for over half a century, Tetra Pak revolutionized food retailing,distribution and hygiene with its simple yet ingenious product designs. The company was founded in 1951 by the visionary industrialist Ruben Rausing (1895-1983), who studied at the Stockholm School of Economics and later atColumbia University, New York. While in America, he was exposed for the first time to “self-service” stores and was convinced that the concept would eventually be adopted in European countries, which would in turn increase the demand for pre-packaged food stuffs. On his return to Sweden, he founded in 1930 with Erik Åkerlund the packaging company Åkerlund & Rausing, the first Scandinavian specialist packaging company, based on Rausing's belief that, “A package should save more than it costs”. In 1943 the company began researching and developing a form of milk packaging that would require the minimum amount of material while providing the maximum hygiene. These early studies of the laboratory assistant Erik Wallenberg ascertained that the optimum shape for a milk carton was a tetrahedron. The following year saw the invention of new techniques for coating paper with plastics and for sealing packages. In the late 1940s, Rausing and Wallenberg established AB Tetra Pak as a subsidiary of Åkerlund & Rausing, and the same year launched their new packaging system based on the tetrahedron-shaped carton. A year later, the Lundaortens Mejeriförening in Lund became the first to use a Tetra Pak machine to packagecream into 100 ml cartons. Several other Swedish dairies introduced the revolutionary packaging system in 1953, and the same year the company began using polyethylene as a coating for its paperboard. In 1954, the first Tetra Pak machine was exported to Germany and the halflitre packaging machine was introduced. The commercial packaging of milk in 300 ml cartons subsequently began the following year. The company eventually opened a much larger manufacturing plant in Lund and commenced the development of an aseptic packaging system (launched in 1961). By 1959, the factory was producing one billion cartons per annum and had begun working on a new brick-shaped design, the Tetra Brik (introduced in 1963). Over the next few years, the business expanded its production operations into Mexico, the United States, Lebanon and Italy. In 1965, ![](images/05dea6a2d9ae1af0834b65c579255bc1cb21a49eef1821e0ff5ebde3d088768d.jpg) ![](images/46d59e713ebd04e9ff201f5f7c03b95161e8a8d771db100bfe44b9041f865011.jpg) 4Tetra Pak packaging machine, c. 2000 Åkerlund & Rausing was sold, but Ruben Rausing retained its subsidiary, ABTetra Pak, and with the funds from the sale was able to invest more resources into the research and development of even better packaging designs, such as the ubiquitous gable-top package known as Tetra Rex (1965). During the 1970s, the company exported its packaging machinery all over the world, including the USSR, Japan, China, Australia and Iran. By the beginning of the 1980s, over 30billion packages were being sold per annum for the storage of milk, cream, fruit juice and wine. In 1991, AB Tetra Pak merged with Alfa-Laval and subsequently diversifiedits product line to includePET bottling equipment. Today, Tetra Pak remains the undisputed world leader in the field of packaging, selling over 250 million products a day in some 165 countries. In 2001 Rausing's son, Hans(b.1926), sold his share of Tetra Pak in order to purchase a majority shareholding in EcoLean(est. 1996), a small Swedish company making innovative packaging materials based on the egg. Recently, it has launched a revolutionary new “green” milk bottle made out of chalk – an odourless and tasteless material that breaks down into carbon, oxygen and ∇ Detail of Tetra Brik Aseptic Recap packaging, 1994 Tetra Brik packaging range, introduced in 1963 ![](images/eabb2d5c811ce71cf0f9520dc741b75a36630ef8d5c9e28bcf2b5011fc867dfa.jpg) chalk dust over a period of two to three months when exposed to sunlight. With a vast range of potential applications for EcoLean's products and technology, Hans Rausing looks set to follow in his father's footsteps by not only revolutionizing the packaging industry, but by helping to solve some of the world's litter problems.

Oiva Toikka, Kastehelmi (Dew) glassware for Nuutajärvi, 1964 – Although best known for his “art" glass pieces, Toikka has also designed for mass production, most notably this mouldedglass tableware range ![](images/42c2d7bd10fab780eefd33afebd5e8a9538ae479bf7dfef7baf35c5f9259a105.jpg) # oiva toikka (b. 1931 Viipuri, Finland) ![](images/0dd2555a6dae0cac18eeff5e2c36860d0e9b9cc5603d7e46bc202d570fbc82b2.jpg) One of Finland's most inventive glass designers, Oiva Toikka studied at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki from 1953 to 1956. Followinghis training, he worked as a ceramics designer for Arabia from 1956 to 1959, and later taught at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu from 1959 to 1961. He began working at Nuutajärvi in 1963 and the same year designed his gigantic free-blown Kurkkupurkki (Pickle Jar) glass sculptures, which were among his first one-off pieces in glass. His subsequent studio art glass, which was distinguished by the use of bright colours and bold forms, had an abstracted pictorial quality that was inspired by both folk-art and contemporary design. In 1969 he produced his well-known Lollipop Isle glass sculptures for Nuutajärvi which epitomized the joyful and carefree Pop aesthetic of the time and embodied the growing confidence of the Finnish glass industry. The 1960s were the golden age of Finnish glass design, and as Toikka noted, “The sixties was in a way a wonderful time, because one was still carried along by the idea that Finnish was good. It was also a happy time in that people still had faith in glass and design generally ... it was fun ... the sky was the limit as far as the appreciation of all the applied arts was concerned." Like many other Finnish artist-designers, Toikka also produced a number of designs for industrial mass production, such as his well-known Kastehelmi (Dew)glassware(1964), which became one of Nuutajärvi's best-selling product lines. Embellished with concentric circles of pearl-like beads, the Kastehelmi range included plates, bowls and candlesticks, and was available in a range of colours – grey, brown, red, blue, green and colourless. Toikka's later Pioni production tableware similarly explored the expressive potential of moulded glass. Apart from his glassware, Toikka also produced fanciful costume and scenery designs for opera and theatre productions in Tampere and Savonlinna. In 1977 he designed the first of his annual Vuosikuutiot (Year Cube) cubic paperweightsfor Nuutajärvi, which can be stacked to create a miniature decorative glass wall. Often disregarding function in favour of fantastic form, Toikka's playful, individualistic and frequently inventive designs possess a poetic quality that conveys the almost alchemical nature of glass making. He has received numerous awards for his glassware, ![](images/645fcc6ee33e27a40a0bdfdc60e711b30f98301f86da1172f4a008f57d92a564.jpg) ![](images/85ca452d78c407d4770898d79576761a75c950def51591fe09fac6eeee8290b9.jpg) including the prestigious Lunning Prize in 1970 and the Pro Finlandia Medal in 1980. More recently, he has become best known for his distinctive glass bird figures that have been produced for over two decades, witha new “species” being added to the range each year. Speaking of these widely admired pieces, Toikka has said that, “Natureis an unending source of inspirationfor me – and glass, as a natural material, is its best interpreter”. Through the extraordinary range of his work, Toikka has blurred the boundaries of art and design while creating some of the most expressive pieces of studio and production glassware to have ever come out of Finland. ΔOiva Toikka, Oz vases from the Pro Arte collection for Nuutajärvi, 1995 △Oiva Toikka, Kynttilänjalna (Lollipop)glasssculpture for Nuutajärvi, 1969 ∇Oiva Toikka, detail of Dawn glass sculpture for Nuutajärvi, c. 1972 ![](images/ae3a278ffed51c2595d933b854d88012f01af2893ab42e249d0a6eb802413386.jpg) ![](images/a91b55fa52cc275c47e62e61dcce2023e17a5cf4d5c7cd31bd2b00c200068af7.jpg) Oiva Toikka, Blue Magpie glass sculpture for Nuutajärvi, 2000 ∇ Oiva Toikka, Golden Dove glass sculpture for Nuutajärvi, 2001 ![](images/289de9b958a8b5ff5c08f3927a8e58b7b2be2a3f5d98bf3770aef1658732ef15.jpg) ![](images/42443d9305523f04c41120b4a689db12964648b69236176aff85b31ee025f765.jpg) Oiva Toikka, Wall of Vuosikuutiot (Year Cube) paperweights for Nuutajärvi, 1977 to 1995 ∇ Oiva Toikka, Vuosikuutiot (Year Cube) paperweight for Nuutajärvi, 2000 ![](images/083e7ba3da1a6d6aad5467b2dea9eda36b72122f55ec622b43f95ca1c8f4202a.jpg)

![](images/0652a082b7dfed9a3db92d778a945db33d2f80fc53d9ce67c308af9653eee602.jpg) ▶ Paavo Tynell, brass and leather table lamp for Taito, 1941 ▶> Paavo Tynell, brass chandelier for Taito, 1948 # paavo tynell (b. 1890 Helsinki – d. 1973 Tuusula, Finland) Paavo Tynell studied metalworking at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design, Helsinki)in Helsinki and then taught there from 1917 to 1923, instructing plate iron, tin and copper-smiths and playing an important role in disseminating handcraft skills. In 1918 he founded his own manufacturing company, Taito Oy, and subsequently produced a wide range of metal objects, including the enormous bronze doors of the Finnish Parliament building in Helsinki. Under Tynell's directorship during the 1920s and 1930s the firm employed a number of young designers such as Alvar Aalto, Gunnel Nyman and Kaj Franck, who went on to become key exponents of Finnish design. By the 1940s, Taito Oy was producing innovative light fittings designed by Tynell, including a brass and leather table lamp (1941) with a scallop-shaped shade and an extraordinary chandelier (1948) with a cascade of brass snowflake-like elements suspended from delicate wire arcs. Inspired by antique crystal chandeliers and traditional Finnish Yuletide himmeli mobiles made of straw, Tynell's highly decorative chandelier was intended to cast interesting shadows and produce a shimmering light effect reminiscent of candle or gas-light. In 1948, Taito Oy began exporting light fittings to the United States, which were retailed by the Finnish company Finland House in New York. Through economic necessity Taito Oy was merged with Idman Osakeyhtiö in 1950, which resulted in Tynell's designs becoming less fanciful and more suited to industrial production. During this period he designed a number of lamps that were manufactured under the Taito label, many with perforated shades and sweeping sculptural forms. While Tynell's lighting products were notable for their structural simplicity, their constructions often featured unusual combinations of brass and glass, which gave them an opulent yet elegant appearance. In many cases his lights were hand-made, and so production was frequently limited to only a few examples of each design. Tynell received numerous awards for his work, including the Ornamo golden badge of honour in 1961. ![](images/2b50c1ec9af35ccbb7c227c761b3966c3f995f0a930688d9bf2ee826d95e8ca7.jpg) ![](images/df0f1fd46e235af1a87ef4c8919745a2e00aec2a6babe4cf08c70679be57c5c7.jpg) ▶ Jørn Utzon, Floating Dock sofa for Fritz Hansen, 1968 # jorn utzon ( 1, oehgen Dnnmark) ![](images/e3143313181129b056faa0a994b84fc73478bbafe08cf6be107b7860090cf582.jpg) The Danish architect and industrial designer Jørn Utzon is best known for his dynamic, highly innovative, but problematical design for the Sydney Opera House (1957–1973) – one of the most famous buildings in the world. He studied under Kay Fisker and Steen Eiler Rasmussen (1898– 1990) at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) from 1937 to 1942, and following this worked for three years in the design practice of the great Swedish functionalist Erik Gunnar Asplund. It was, however the unified architectural schemes of Alvar Aalto and Frank Lloyd Wright (1867–1959) – the two great patriarchs of Organic Modernism – that had the biggest influence on his work. Having established his own design practice in Zurich and Ålsgarde in 1947, Utzon went on to win a number of building competitions, most notably first prize for the design of the Sydney Opera House and Concert Hall in 1956. The resulting building with its magnificent sail-like vaults brought him much international fame, but from the start the project was dogged by controversy. Construction of the prefabricated concrete roof shells, which were inspired by the cellular structures of nature that Utzon described as “additive architecture", posed a variety of problems. He eventually worked out a solution for their construction with an ingenious piece of spherical geometry, but was nevertheless forced to resign from the project in 1966 (the building and interiors were completed by an Australian firm of architects). In addition to buildings, during the 1960s and 1970s Utzon also designed lamps (for Nordisk Solar), glassware, and furniture. His Floating Dock furniture collection (1967) for the Danish furniture manufacturer Fritz Hansen was also born of his concept of additive architecture. Comprising a number of differently sized tables and chairs (including a curious sofa-bed style chair), the construction of the Floating Dock range was based on an innovative system of arclike components of anodized triangular-section extruded aluminium that locked together at a fortyfive degree angle. His undulating, expressionistic 8101 chair (1969), which could be linked with additional pieces to create a wider seating unit or a sofa, was similarly inspired by his architectural concepts, and reflected his fascination with inventive construction operating “at the edge of the possible”. Beyond his love of nature, Utzon also ∇ Jørn Utzon, Floating Dock modular seating with matching table for Fritz Hansen, 1968 ∇∇ Jørn Utzon, lounge chair and ottoman for Fritz Hansen, 1968 ![](images/2ce15d76dc81eb7c32f3323a51d1dabe1de07b04c69cdfc7e32e05b2de84025a.jpg) found much inspiration. in the buildings of other ages and cultures, such as Chinese, Islamic and Mayan. Throughout his career he received numerous awards, including the Eckersberg Medal (1957), the German Institute of Architects Medal (1965), the Danish Furniture Prize (1970), and a gold medal from the Royal Institute of British Architects (1978). ![](images/a00ecb22347cb0d5674ae568fc3889da3d02503301fa37bbf37226ef68f4a6c6.jpg)

![](images/1d378d7d36b7a28efc0cf44bde37fc8e30243f0fe6c128f4b765928872dfe678.jpg) > Gertrud Vasegaard, glazed porcelain and bamboo lea service for Bing & Grøndahl, 1955-1957 Gertrud Vasegaard, stoneware vase, 1937 # gertrd agaardh101 10 Dm) ![](images/4122585abf879fee1b21f3bd570018a28aab42276972e60353ca4a044f807cf6.jpg) The Danish ceramicist Gertrud Vasegaard came from a family of potters who lived on the island of Bornholm. She studied at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School of Arts & Crafts) in Copenhagen from 1930 to 1932, and then trained in the ceramics studio of Axel Salto and Bode Willumsen (1895–1987) in Saxbo from 1932 to 1933. Following this she set up a studio with her sister, Lisbeth Munch-Petersen (1909–1997), in Gudhjem, Bornholm. She later established her own independent workshop/studio in 1936 in Holkadalen, Bornholm, where she produced high-guality handmade stoneware ceramics inspired by Oriental antecedents. The influence of Oriental art on Danish designers such as Vasegaard not only reflected Denmark's centuries-old sea-trading links with the Far East, but also a general respect for economical forms and high levels of craftsmanship. Between 1949 and 1959 Vasegaard worked as a designer (on both a freelance and full-time basis) for the Bing & Grøndahl ceramics factory in Copenhagen, where she executed a number of designs suitable for industrial mass production, including decorated bowis and vases. She also designed a porcelain dinner and tea service (1955–1957) for Bing & Grøndahl, which with its hexagonal teapot complete with a wicker handle and its curious flask-like tea-box was directly inspired by traditional Chinese forms. Awarded a gold medal at the 1957 (XI) Milan Triennale exhibition, this design predicted the mainstream taste in the 1970s for all things Oriental. Between 1959 and 1975, Vasegaard also designed a number of ceramics on a freelance basis for the Royal Copenhagen factory, including her stoneware Gemina tableware range decorated with an abstract Oriental-style pattern in Chinese blue. Like many other Scandinavian ceramicists working in industry, Vasegaard used traditional craft methods – hand throwing on a potter's wheel – to produce the prototypes for her mass-produced designs. Her choice of stoneware for the Gemina range ensured a slightly uneven texture that gave the service a hand-made effect. In 1959 she established a studio with her daughter, Myre Vasegaard (b. 1936), in Frederiksberg, and also worked with Aksel Rode (1905– 1989) until 1969. ![](images/6cdd01bed95913343230f3b6f599c16a15d293fa6436abcc51365121e1f78664.jpg)

![](images/2563ff13bb67070a3f414e389ff9cc83cab398d2fdd21b20973d457607a715c4.jpg) Kristian Vedel, adjustable child's chair for Torben Ørskov & Co., 1957 # kristian vedel (Danish b 19223) ![](images/00492928cd846176260afa3129555bf328349df7cb61c37afd0f89c0e3c8f89f.jpg) The Danish furniture designer Kristian Vedel served an apprenticeship as a cabinet-maker and became a journeyman joiner in1942. He trained under Kaare Klint in the furniture department of the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) from 1944 to 1945, and graduated from the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School of Arts & Crafts) in 1946. Following this, he was employed as an assistant between 1946 and 1949 by the architecture and furniture design husband-and-wife team Tove and Edvard Kindt-Larsen (1906-1994 & 1901-1982). He later worked for Palle Suenson (1904-1987)from 1949to 1955, and was a teacher at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen in Copenhagen from 1953 to 1956. Strongly influenced by Klint's standards of economy, simplicity and function, during this period Vedel designed an innovative child's chair, which was formed as a curved plywood shell and fitted with slots at various heights for seat adjustment. Two of these chairs could also be joined together to make up an integrated chair-cum-feeding table. This highly practical design, which was manufactured by Torben Ørskov & Co., was based on Vedel's belief that, “The starting point for an industrial artist's work must always be that he, from his own point of view, and as objectively as possible, takes a position with regard to what he feels society and his fellow men need; he must personally take a stand on the existing possibilities and responsibilities." In 1955 he established his own design practice and the following year produced a range of multi-functional melamine kitchenware – storage jars, bowls, beakers, salad servers and an egg-cup – that was remarkable for its sculptural simplicity. Five years later he designed his well-known Modus furniture system with its low and high-backed lounge chairs, armless dining chairs and matching glass-topped tables.Manufactured by Søren Willadsen from 1963 onwards, the Modus range was notable for its use of padded leather slings that formed the arm and back sections when fitted onto the supporting rosewood frame. In 1962, Vedel was awarded the Lunning Prize for his utilitarian consumer products in plastic that “demonstrated his acceptance of materials beyond those traditional to the furniture craftsman". Between 1966 and 1968 he was chairman of the IDD (Industrial Designers of Denmark), and from 1969to1972 ∇∇ Kristian Vedel, children's furniture executed by I. Christiansen, 1949 –This furniture was designed to grow with the child ![](images/f2e02517db1af2aebf8e6ce637a2c74b04237127dba3406412aac411293f2033.jpg) organized and led the department of industrial design at the University of Nairobi in Kenya with the aim of meeting “the needs of the population as a whole”.On his return to Denmark in 1972, Vedel and his wife,Ane, established a design studio in Thyholm and began exploring different uses for wool. As one of the leading Danish furniture designersin the late 1950s and 1960s, Vedel received numerous accolades including silver and gold medals at the 1957 (XI) and 1960(XII) Milan Triennale. ![](images/9b0b1725bf06b5f0300373634e685c0ed2cc306fe6c3582137825e48485ceb7f.jpg)

# Poul Volther, teak dining chair for FDB, 1951 Poul Volther, Model no. EJ 605 Corona chair for Erik Jørgensen, 1961 # poul volther ns s 923-200) ![](images/46735fb97abfd263e396cf6ea4ae45ea62092de8e214fc5757f5c0244faed4e7.jpg) The Danish architect and furniture designer Poul Volther worked as a journeyman joiner before studying at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School of Arts & Crafts) in Copenhagen. Upon graduating in 1949 he established his own architecture and design practice, which he operated until 1985. Over his career Volther designed numerous pieces of furniture, including an elegant teak dining chair (1951)for FDB(Association of Danish co-operative stores), which can be seen as a radical reworking of the traditional ladder-back chair. A decade later he used similar floating elements for his wellknown Corona chair (1961) which were reminiscent of time-lapse photographs of solar eclipses. Constructed of moulded plywood upholstered with cold-cured polyurethane foam, these elliptical seat pads could also be used as armrests. The frame of the chair to which they were attached was first produced in solid oak, but in 1962 this was changed to chromed spring steel. Similar to the upholstered furniture designed by Arne Jacobsen and VernerPanton, the Corona chair, which was and still is manufactured by Erik Jørgensen, exemplified the trend among the Danish avant-garde away from the re-working of existing furniture types towards a more sculptural and “synthetic" language of design. But while forward-looking designs such as these embodied the optimistic spirit of the period, they did not embrace the Pop culture ethos of ephemerality and disposability – they were highquality products intended to stand the test of time. Beyond his architecture and design work, Volther also taught at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (until 1953) and later at the School of Furnishing and the Polytechnic in Copenhagen between 1955 and 1980. As a leading Danish architect, he was also a member of the Academic Architects' Society from 1955. In keeping with other Scandinavian designers at the time, Volther combined traditional craftsmanship with modern technology so as to produce furniture that was not only durable and comfortable, but also visually exciting. ![](images/426faf124c1a184f936e8434ff1cc9b1fead6fdb74383c612883767c8099f836.jpg) ![](images/c61b677a66f26a72542f0dc9e75b37df664afcbf61aa391f21cf7fb78968ed17.jpg) Volvo P/800, launched 1960 – the car driven by Roger Moore as “The Saint" ∇ Gustaf Larson & Assar Gabrielsson, founders of Volvo ## volvo (founded 1927, Gothenburg, Sweden) ![](images/28c945721aa84114af0349f8865d884a26a6210c3cdd9dfd70b0d2f002339c51.jpg) Volvo is recognized around the world as one of the greatest pioneers of safety systems within the automotive industry. The origins of this remarkable company can be traced to the 1920s, when Sweden was still heavily reliant on car imports – at the time between 12,000 to 14,500 automobiles were being imported annually. To remedy this situation, Gustaf Larson (1887-1968)and Assar Gabrielsson (1891-1962) decided to join forces and produce their own automobiles in the summer of 1924. Gabrielsson had previously studied economics and was a successful businessman, while Larson was an engineer and a designer who had worked on the development of Morris engines at White & Poppe in Coventry, England. In 1926, they came up with the idea of designing high quality car components that would be manufactured to their exacting specifications by selected suppliers, and then assembling them with the assistance of experienced car builders. Having secured the necessary financing from the company for which they both worked, Larson and Gabrielsson acquired factory premises and established their own firm, which they named Volvo (Volvo meaning “I roll” in Latin). Their first series-manufactured car, the ÖV4, left their factory in Gothenburg in April 1927, thus marking an important turning point in Swedish industrial design history. Nicknamed Jakob, this first Volvo production car was based on contemporaneous American models. With its strong chassis and four-cylinder engine, the Jakob had a top speed of 90 kph, but sales were slow during the first year partly because the car's high build quality meant that it was relatively expensive – the opentopped version cost 4,800 Swedish krona, while the sedan cost 5,800 Swedish krona. In 1929 Volvo introduced its first six-cylinder model, the PV651 – “PV” meaning PersonVagn (People Wagon) – which had a more robust chassis than the Jakob and was especially favoured by taxi fleets. In 1935 the company launched the streamlined PV36 designed by Ivan Örnberg (1881– 1936), who had spent the whole of his career working in the US car industry. With its integrated headlamps and sleek design the PV36, which was also known as the Carioca, was undoubtedly inspired by Carl Breer's famous Airflow of 1934. During the Second World War Volvo produced several military vehicles, including a jeep, and also developed a number of civilian prototypes. 1944 saw the launch of the PV444, which combined American styling with an unconventional, unitized all-steel body. Developed by Helmer Petterson (1901-1980), the PV444 was the first Volvo that “could be afforded by the average-man-in-thestreet”. In 1956, the classic 120(known as the Amazon in Scandinavia) was introduced and featured numerous innovative safety features, including a padded dashboard. This stylish design was exhibited at the New York Motor Show in the spring of 1956, and within three months Volvo had gained more than one hundred dealers in America. With its emphasis on safety, in 1959 Volvo became the first manufacturer in the world to equip all its cars with three-point safety belts which were invented by Nils Bohlin and lessened the risk of internal injuries by having the buckle placed on the side rather than over the abdomen. During the 1960s and 1970s, models such as the 144 sedan (1966), 145 estate (1967), and 240/260 series(1974) became best-sellers while pioneering other innovative safety features such as disc brakes all round, split steering columns and outer rear-seat three-point safety belts. In 1976, Volvo presented the world's first catalytic exhaust ![](images/e0fe31483856792ba6a5854c7c4261cc8a4c623c3a2200b44359f7a295c374d5.jpg) Volvo ÖV4 (Jakob) automobile, introduced in 1927 ∇Helmer Petterson, Volvo PV444, introduced in 1944 - Volvo's first small car. It stayed in production in modified form for more than 20 years. ![](images/a5277fd7c0c9fe20a805ba69b9b1670c1fdd1e638dc37fdd32710fbdcd22af03.jpg) Volvo P120 (Amazon), introduced in 1956 Nils Bohlin, three-point safety belt, introduced in 1959 – making Volvo the first car company in the world to offer safety belts as standard equipment ![](images/c46c0d8e70c2898d34b190bbb4d040da10fb3533f45dadcceef4bb5fb22d6934.jpg) ![](images/3e0599e11664c1fcd4e317fdcec510fcbc41222dc5a1d639fe2a97c19cea3b5a.jpg) ![](images/57508b79c75926d5c0dd12205b2fd650639a9c1e7eb7d7e4680ab9cc2c3ac512.jpg) emission control and by 1988 seventy percent of its new cars were fitted with catalytic converters. The highly successful 700 series was launched in 1982, with the subsequent 740/760 estate models (1985) totally dominating their market segment. During the 1990s, the company attempted to shake off its conservative image and introduced a number of new cars that featured smoother styling, yet retained a strong Volvo identity, such as the S80 (1998). More recently, Volvo's truck division has introduced its VN series of vehicles, which has taken the US market by storm: Way ahead of its competitors in terms of design, Volvo has developed a truck that is renowned as much for its safety as for its comfort. With its roomyand ingeniously planned integrated cab and sleeper compartment, within a yearof its launch the VN defiedall expectationsand became the best-sellingtruckin the United States. As a leading Scandinavian company, Volvo has always been guided by a humanocentric approach to design that has dismissed superfluous styling in favour of product integrity and high build quality. This approach has also ensured that Volvo cars have become renowned as durable workhorses that just keep on going. Indeed, Volvo has historically added much value to its brand and enjoyed increasing success and brand loyalty by raising the overall durability of its products. Thus annual sales of used Volvo cars actually surpass the number of new vehicles it produces each year (around 400,000). The huge and ever-increasing secondary market for its vehicles and branded parts is massively profitable for the company. But while durability equals profitability at Volvo, the company's success can also be attributed to its outstanding reputation for safety. From the seat belt tensioner and the integrated child booster seat to the self-adjusting belt reel and the integrated side impact protection system, Volvo's innovative design programme has afforded its drivers and passengers superior protection and in so doing has set the standard in the automotive industry. Since 1999, the car manufacturing division of Volvo has been fully owned by Ford Motor Corporation and forms along with Jaguar, Aston Martin, Lincoln, and Land Rover the Ford Premier Automotive Group. △ Volvo promotional photograph of two-tone Volvo P120 (Amazon), introduced in 1956 ∇ Volvo 245 Estate, part of the Volvo 240 series introduced in 1974 ![](images/52738355b0ce3b400a071dfd2a8f5f18c39003e71dc318737ecc59c6c01185ea.jpg) ![](images/761fad7190167b55d6772490da915bafa034dcd16f94f50e3ab3a2cdfe509dca.jpg) △△ Volvo Duett, introduced in 1953 – The name of this car expressed the company's desire for “two cars in one" (a car for work and leisure) Volvo 145 Estate, introduced in 1967 ΔVolvo Amazon Estate, introduced in 1962 ![](images/d665cbd919858a7bd2958d55d8e7bae1b2a2b0fa335e897fa4d6ce017fb2a8fa.jpg) ∇∇Volvo 740 Estate, introduced in 1985 ![](images/611e8019c4e9a9bccf2b05dcd373dabf83435c5edf6a8e651941909116553b83.jpg) ![](images/98a5addcc179b6f0dfebe0ba2e7d868111bad7963e41218d042b5720cf258484.jpg) ![](images/51d592a12a1ba5986b798027e470d30b926a509e2360a13a0e6794f3717ab8e5.jpg) △Interior of VolvoV70, introduced in 1996-1997 ∇ Volvo V70 Estate, introduced in 1996-1997 Detail of Volvo V70, introduced in 1996-1997 ![](images/cebe4cb144c6ccdd16942ef4397c154452f8d09c3003bfd705f93ea62859363c.jpg) ![](images/bf35ca8a02804bfe17bcd6527db37a447abdb50deeb146c32c7d7b78ae1b9be8.jpg) ![](images/2ac1f4c58d63ecbf29dc27146e7528ca26051fd8e8f62f91df18b87b16f74b41.jpg) ![](images/b4f799b7ad43fc182cf728f8510bcb240f13d718869602e9a814b5aeabe68428.jpg) △ Volvo XC90, launched at the 2002 Detroit Motor Show 4 Volvo VN truck, 1996 ![](images/ac12335709d36e521a73a14d68bbec9dcd6a7fbd6bbd2db296cbdadcf1936415.jpg) ## pia walén 1 Una, Seden) ![](images/5f3e5efa4ef244106736b58a84a7b8426525cadf2908207d5a83c3d3ae97e8d6.jpg) Pia Wallén founded her own design company. Pia Wallén Form, in 1979 before studying fashion design from 1981 to 1983 at the Beckman School of Design in Stockholm. Following this, she designed exhibitions for the Museum of Architecture in Stockholm and was commissioned to participate in the nationwide project “Knitting with Tradition and Rejuvenation” (1985–1986). Since 1983 she has explored in depth the textural and sculptural properties of various textiles – especially felt, which she describes as “the oldest form of textile material" and as a "material of survival" —with the objective of reviving traditional skills through the development of innovative contemporary products. In 1989 Wallén received the Designer of the 90s Award from the Swedish magazine Sköna Hem, and in 1992 she was commissioned to design the Felt Programme for Oggetti Cappellini. Since 1998 Asplund has retailed a number of her product designs, including her ubiquitous Felt slippers with distinctive contrasting stitching and her Krux blankets and rug, which use a cross motif as a “powerful symbol” that transcends global cultures. In 1998 she also designed a range of sleek silver jewellery with felt linings. She has participated in numerous exhibitions, including “New Scandinavia" in Cologne (1999), “Swedish Style in Tokyo” (1999) and “Design World 2000” in Helsinki Her more recent work includes the Dot Collection (2000), which focuses on tennis ball construction and comprises clothes, bags, bowls, and jewellery in different coloured felts. As a prominent member of the Swedish design community, Wallén's work reveals the continuing attraction of natural materials among the Scandinavian avant-garde, and the belief that “continuity between tradition and the present is important". 4 Pia Wallén, Krux (Cross) blanket for Element Design, 1991 ![](images/5013bcdaae5a6c86d616507fd3f040da98b9c5a13b20e584ed1e84adeb2b87f6.jpg) ## # Pia Wallén, Dot carpet for Asplund, 2000

![](images/b09d0b7c3879ba59fdefe293728ff1ee477768c39c979cce7b08145fdfbf8a5f.jpg) Ole Wanscher, Egyptian Stoo/ for A.J. Iversen, 1960 # ole wanscher Danish 1903-1985) The Danish furniture designer and historian Ole Wanscher studied under Kaare Klint at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts). His father was an art historian who influenced Wanscher's life-long interest in furniture design history. He made several trips to Europe and Egypt to study vernacular and historical furniture types, which provided the background research material for his many books, including Furniture Types (1932), Outline History of Furniture (1941), English Furniture c. 1680–1800(1944) and History of the Art of Furniture (1946–1956). Wanscher was one of the founders of the influential annual exhibitions arranged by the Cabinetmakers' Guild in Copenhagen and designed elegant, classical furniture for numerous manufacturers, including P. Jeppesen, Rud. Rasmussen, France & Son and A.J. Iversen. Like his mentor Kaare Klint, Wanscher firmly believed that designers should study historical furniture types that had been functionally and formally honed over time. His own work was especially influenced by 18th century English furniture and Chinese, Egyptian and Greek antecedents – the folding form of his mahogany and leather Egyptian Stool (1960), for example, was based on an ancient exemplar in the collection of the Staatliche Museen zu Berlin. Wanscher's furniture was also notable for its high quality construction and finish, and assuch, it exemplified the sheer breathtaking virtuosity of Danish craftsmanship. In 1955, hesucceeded Klint as professor of furniture design at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (1955-1973), where he continued to promote the study of period furniture. In 1960 he was awarded a gold medal at the Milan Triennale and the Copenhagen Cabinetmakers' Annual Prize. He also participated in numerous exhibitions, including “DesigninScandinavia”(USA 1954-1957) and “Neue Form aus Dänemark”(Germany 1956–1959). Although Wanscher's furniture was exquisitely crafted and used luxury materials such as rosewood and leather, not all of it was intended solely for theexclusive end of the market. In actual fact, he was one of the first Danish designers to develop furniture for serial mass production. The exquisite detailing of his designs underlined his masterful understanding of furniture-making techniques, while their elegant, timeless forms reflected his scholarly interest in the analysis of period types. ![](images/d95b0eb14c6933ae56d7799238e9bd9bd504164caf87e912a84386ac6ab9b76c.jpg) OleWanscher, oak and cane chair for Rud. Ras mussen, 1951 ∇Ole Wanscher, rosewood nest of tables for A. J. Iversen, 1953 ![](images/4502e640ee8da97c00619e1c55e178f19974401a07240e9bc70a8787abdf2071.jpg)

Eero Aarnio, Pastilli chair for Asko, 1967 – later produced by Adelta ![](images/694e232a6abd972a9a363427cc8ccabc238cd1ccb349a54de2e23c3a2e801e03.jpg) # eero aarnioO(. 192 elsinki,Fiand) ![](images/f7697c95817f98d94041cdd596b7888f6c779d3f023cef8a0dbf94103439e828.jpg) One of several designers to bring international recognition to Finnish design in the 1960s, Eero Aarnio studied at the Taideteollisuuskeskuskoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki from 1954to1957. He subsequently established his own design practice in 1962 and since then has worked mainly as an interior and industrial designer. Following the precedent set by earlier Finnish designers during the first half of the 20th century, Aarnio initially designed furniture in natural materials using traditional handcraft techniques, such as his Jattujakkare wicker stool (c. 1958). Believing, however,that, “Design means constant renewal, realignment, growth,"Aarnio began experimenting during the 1960s with fibreglass and in particular the bold and exciting forms that this material permitted. His best-known series of designs from this period included the Ball or Globe chair (1962), the rocking Pastilli chair (1967) for which he won an AID (American Industrial Design) award in 1968, and the Perspex hanging Bubble chair (1968). While these iconoclastic seating solutions captured the spirit of the 1960swith their visually exciting space-age forms, Aarnio did not embrace the Pop culture ethos of ephemeralityand disposability. In 1973 Aarnio designed his playful toy-like vet adult-sized Pony seat, which was marketed as the Mustang by Stendig in the United States. This unusual design made of upholstered moulded foam reflected Aarnio's belief that, “A chair is a chair, is a chair, is a chair ... but a seat does not necessarily have to be a chair. It can be anything as long as it is ergonomically correct." This unusual Pop design could either be sat on sideways or “rode” presumably into a “fantasy” landscape limited only by one's own imagination. During the 1980s Aarnio developed a prototype plywoodchair and later in the 1990s designed two fibreglass tables – the Copacabana (1991) and the Screw (1992) – for Adelta, as well as sculptural metal door furniture (1996) for Valli & Valli. Aarnio looks forward optimistically to a time when, “The personal approach of the past and the robot manufacture of the future clasp hands ... (so that)... industrial production will be so completely mastered that we can forget it." Although best known for his furniture and interior schemes, Aarnio has also worked in the areas of graphic design and photography. ![](images/c056314170e9876d3c20382b235b2409881c14798c7f66c7428b2538ee0f0f2b.jpg) Eero Aarnio, Ball or Globe chair for Asko, 1962 - later produced by Adelta ![](images/15fdda1d954a39ecb3aeefec249dc31558034a37fde95bc3bb5e8155b4db167d.jpg) ![](images/a2993d5ca325b616e295b0c2a36dd8c4dab88ae67c7c7942ef98c03002fe7548.jpg) △ Graphic portrayal of Bubble chair, published in Design from Scandinavia, no. 3, late 1960s Eero Aarnio, Bubble chair for Asko, 1968 ![](images/f4f9422c92ea1e0d372abd164fb359da5775e4afd7994eaee2c5c77ea2f55996.jpg) 4Eero Aarnio, armchair for Asko, c. 1967 Eero Aarnio, Pony chair for Asko, c. 1970 ∇ Stendig showroom in New York with Pony chair for Asko, C. 1970 ∇> Eero Aarnio, Asko poster advertising Pony chair, c. 1970 ![](images/287bf2f8977fbff4e3a8c88c160b6c5acdbfcaf4568197186f1fe5a3dafb996d.jpg) ![](images/5e2b9b998cb80bb06999cb65f786860d137fa6d7f3ede79926fed34664f5aa90.jpg) ![](images/524b8a9a7cafedbc15143af34811310d58a05f6bf1d43c0a772d06ccd65f6674.jpg)

![](images/1287fd51666c5fa9025490c06b2bbe4ac74a46d5ae707bad33101ed3bd6927b9.jpg) Björn Weckström, Planetaariset Laaksot (Planetoid Valleys) necklace for Lapponia, 1969 # björn weckström (b. 1935 Helsinki, Finland) ![](images/3d80ab1194c434d0e8a9b699034c0049bc8e6f7af110e614243749a80fbeab93.jpg) As a child Björn Weckström dreamt of becoming a sculptor, but his family insisted he pursue a more practical career and so he studied at the Kultaseppäkoulu (Goldsmith's School) in Helsinki, graduating in 1956. Following this he studied in Europe, America, Mexico and the Far East prior to working as a freelance designer from 1956 to 1963. He established his own workshop and gallery in Helsinki in 1958 and a year later received second and third prize in a cutlery design competition arranged by Hackman and Sorsakoski. In 1961 he received a challenging commission from a client who asked him to design and execute a piece of jewellery using raw Lemmenjoki gold nuggets. The resulting piece emphasized the “true, primordial nature” of the material and came to be seen as the first example of so-called “jewellery of Lapland” – highly sculptural jewellery inspired by the raw and rugged landscape of Weckström's native land. The same year his Fairy Castle ring made of gold and smoky quartz was purchased by the Victoria & Albert Museum in London, and in 1962he was awarded second prize in a jewellery design competition held by the Finnish Goldsmith's Association. The following year he was appointed principal designer for Lapponia Jewelry, which had been established in 1960 as a manufacturer of art jewellery. Like his fellow countryman Tapio Wirkkala, Weckström was captivated by the romanticism of Lapland and took annual trips there so as to capture the “northern mystique and its beauty” in his work. Using the ancient “cire perdu” method of casting from wax, and inspired by the sculptures of Henry Moore (1898–1986), Weckström has produced new organic forms that celebrate the inherent characteristics of gold ("a hot material” that has a dramatic and charged character) and silver ("a cool material" that is soft and requires tranquil design). Freeing jewellery from its traditional forms, Weckström's pieces, such as the Flowering Wall pendant (1965)and the Planetoid Valleys necklace (1969), which was worn by Princess Leila in Star Wars, not only function as “sculpture in miniature”, but also produce a sense of “complete landscape”. In 1968, Weckström was awarded the Lunning Prize and shortly thereafter made a trip to Uganda, where he found much new inspiration. During the late 1960she began incorporating acrylic elements into his jewellery pieces, and from 1974 also began designing glass and large bronze sculptures and domestic glassware. With his Lappgold and Space Silver collections, Weckström brought a new expressive sensibility to jewellery design. While his informal, modern and pioneering designs are primarily aimed at the luxury market, the manufacturer Lapponia has explained the nature of their exclusivity: “Aesthetics which are not based on the principle of fulfilling social needs remain open, forever seeking new possibilities. Theyare the free aesthetics ofan intermediate zone. A process." As a sculptor, Weckström approaches jewellery design as an art form in itself and states, “To me, jewellery is a sculpture with a human background." ![](images/f06b64514e1aac30091152c1bf2aa7805c68ba6dd2af4d9925189816eb0a9bd5.jpg) ![](images/d36259f8da8de8d0fad3aded42901c9def80cc31be8a288d632f1a39ee5778c4.jpg) △BjörnWeckström, Kukkiva Muuri (Flowering Wall) necklace for Lapponia, 1965 ∆ Björn Weckström, Big Drop pendant for Lapponia, 1971 4Björn Weckström, Kukkiva Muuri (Flowering Wall) pendant for Lapponia, 1965

![](images/a5bf0c8a1e4abf92d86fd5010b4143d163f76861bb1c0a2348d1fb704022ba24.jpg) Hans Wegner, 3-benet ska/stol (Three-Legged Shell Chair) for Johannes Hansen, 1963 # hansr wegner 1 de t tad, Deneark ![](images/e1a8f4d99bc16377460665af3686656bc67e170e3fd1f3c6555426f2a0f0f92b.jpg) One of the greatest Danish designers of all time, Hans Wegner was the son of a master cobbler who grew up with a deep appreciation of handcraftsmanship and the inherent qualities of materials. At an early age he developed a real love and understanding of wood, which led him to undertake an apprenticeship in H.F. Stahlberg's carpentry workshop. After becoming a qualified cabinetmaker, Wegner remained at the workshop for three years until he began his military conscription. He later studied joinery at the Polytechnic in Copenhagen, where some of his lessons involved measuring period furniture displayed at the Copenhagen Museum of Decorative Arts. This experience, as well as the annual furniture exhibitions held by the Copenhagen Cabinetmakers' Guild, so influenced the young carpenter that he eventually decided to become a designer. With this intent, he began his training under the furniture designer Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School of Arts & Crafts) in 1936. Two years later, Wegner worked in the design practice of Erik Møller and Flemming Lassen in Århus, and in 1940 began working in the office established by Møller and Arne Jacobsen for the design of Århus Town Hall. For this project, Wegner designed simple yet well-crafted furniture that epitomized his approach to design at this point in his career, which he described as “stripping the old chairs of their outer style and letting them appear in their pure construction”. From 1943 to 1946, Wegner ran his own Århus-based design studio and with Børge Mogensen designed a range of good quality yet inexpensive furniture(1945-1946) for the director of theDanish Cooperative Union, Frederik Nielsen, which was intended for small post-war apartments. Following this, Wegner worked in partnership with the architect Palle Suenson(1904-1987)in Copenhagen from 1946 to 1948, prior to establishing his own independent design practice in Copenhagen. From 1940 he also worked with the furniture-maker and chairman of the Cabinetmakers' Guild, Johannes Hansen, and designed numerous chairs for his manufacturing company, including the famous Round chair (1949), which became known as The Chair or the Classic Chair. By the 1950s, Wegner had become one of the leading Scandinavian designers and was internationally celebrated for his exquisitely balanced and beautifully crafted furniture that for the most part was constructed of solid wood. Manufactured by Johannes Hansen, Fritz Hansen, Andreas Tuck, Getama, A. P. Stolen, Carl Hansen &Søn and PP Møbler, Wegner's furniture was intended, as he explained, to be “as simple and genuine as possible, to show what we could create with our hands, to try to make wood come alive, to giveitspirit and vitality, and to get things to be so natural that they could only have been made by us”. His characteristically Scandinavian organic approach to design, as exemplified by hisChinese chair(1943),Peacock chair (1947), Y-chair (1950) andValet chair (1953), focused on the importance of the physical, psychological and emotional connections between users and their tools for living. By simplifying and purifying formand construction, Wegner created timeless, supremely beautiful modern re-workings of traditional furniture types that expressed both a material and structural honesty.Over his long career he won numerous awards, including the Lunning Prize in 1951 – the first year it was established. A gifteddesigner and master craftsman, Wegner made Danishdesign world famous. ![](images/be5e8216212d8b9fc9ab4cca99f5d2445cf31366ab341ea2c86deabf6137d430.jpg) Δ Hans Wegner, Model no. CH24 Y-Stolen (Wishbone Chair) for Carl Hansen & Søn, 1950 Δ Hans Wegner, Chinese chair for Fritz Hansen, 1943 ![](images/ce14934e89485ec893efaa5ce651b2f6a7d4593122e9223a1a9af7ff849d36bc.jpg) 4 Hans Wegner, Model no. 550 Peacock chair for Johannes Hansen, 1947 - later produced by PP Møbler Hans Wegner, Model no. 501 Round chair for Johannes Hansen, 1949 – later produced by PP Møbler ![](images/6e55fd85129b6d1b17944cd969dc63183418013021a082a135f4518ae431b2f8.jpg) ∇Hans Wegner, Design for the Round chair, 1949 ∇ CBS television station purchased twelve Round chairs for the famous television presidential candidate debate between John F. Kennedy and Richard Nixon in 1960 ![](images/0b753f3eb996986c74eb3c1091e5698fbe657b4280e12238e1958e4470ff072c.jpg) ![](images/eb1b2acd0bf3120ef8ec760505dc0ab8528effc1a7eebc1dba1ac2b9c12da7d2.jpg) ∇Interior of Hans Wegner's house, Tinglevvej – The house was designed in 1962 ![](images/f7aaa27a46d5066204e797ec7b577b4c185acb2cfd938ecfce9aaddb1856f73e.jpg) ![](images/b162bf16b4144e05ed8d40d9ac1ad4c447835b2a91187ef27bc00b1334e3ad80.jpg) ∇Hans Wegner, Model no. PP512 folding chair for PP Møbler, 1949 ![](images/3436c19742605447e43b1b0986b73e63fb14089d5e7e01b42d724787554605a2.jpg) ![](images/9459642dc26593079dc7c72f1dfe55853a38b558505117de5ffff0ce3ca5f627.jpg) 4 Hans Wegner, Model no. 27 easy chairs for Carl Hansen & Son, 1949 ![](images/8282c01ae95282f6b5e710252330acef6653d998f5b3e858738f712a34ce263e.jpg) ![](images/8a5aeb30016946cf3c57bba3b615ba50cdc939c051fe6a978d1684b9a590db7b.jpg)

Gunnar Wennerberg, Iris vase for Gustavsberg, 1897 ![](images/947bdc190bc22b1df7ae8a81935c137aa8f7bc0a8fed25fc96a50473783fb5bd.jpg) ▶ Gunnar Wennerberg, Gullviva and Liljekonvalje (Cowslip and Lily-of-the-Valley) plates for Gustavsberg, 1897 # gunnar wennerberg (b. 1863 Skara, Sweden – d. 1914 Paris, France) ![](images/ca407743236a47d3b5f5db9b455fe3e36d69328a767d720f9f705485fe9b6b17.jpg) The Swedish painter, ceramics, glass and textile designer Gunnar Wennerberg studied painting under François Bonnat (1811–1894) and Henri Gervex (1852-1929)in Paris from 1886, and also trained at the Sèvres Porcelain Factory. On returning to Sweden in 1895 he was appointed art director of the Gustavsberg ceramics factory. During his thirteen-year tenure there, Wennerberg introduced a new stylistic direction to the company that drew inspiration from the natural world. As a gifted painter of flowers, Wennerberg produced a number of exquisite designs that were decorated with Art Nouveau style floral motifs. HisGullviva (Cowslip) and Liljekonvalje (Lily-of-the-Valley) plates were meticulously hand-painted in soft natural colours with stylized representations of wild flowers native to Sweden. These designs were displayed at the 1897 Stockholm “Konst- och Industriutställningen”(Arts and IndustryExhibition) and revealed not only the growing influence of the “New Art”, but also the respect with which nature was commonly heldin Sweden: while the flowers Wennerberg depicted were stylized, they did not display the frenzied swirling and almost tortured quality of similar floral motifs found in French Art Nouveau or German Jugendstil designs. Wennerberg also executed a number of designs that incorporated high-relief modelling, such as his Iris vase of 1897. Between 1898 and 1909 he also designed cameo glassware for the Kosta factory, which bore more than a passing resemblance to designs byÉmile Gallé(1846–1904), but the floralmotifs Wennerberg embellished these with were treated in a much more naturalistic way. From 1902 to 1908 Wennerberg taught painting at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design), and from 1908 he designed ceramics for the Sèvres Porcelain Factory and woven textiles for Handarbetets Vänner. He returned to Paris in 1909 and lived thereuntil his death in 1914. As one of the leading Swedish designers at the turn of the century, Wennerberg's simplified and poetic interpretation of the Art Nouveau style was of much influence in Scandinavia. ![](images/a41af80cfb34a8dd18e4336d1cdbf9515729a83bdc6c7d08b2caf3b580b7cf17.jpg)

![](images/c182b7d41376413b3e24fdeed3ab30656c465866e7d9c31786c7fbd121767b0d.jpg) Bjørn Wiinblad, Lotus cutlery for Rosenthal, 1964 Bjørn Wiinblad, nativity Christmas plates for Rosenthal, first introduced in 1971- A new plate with a different motif was produced annually until 1983 # bjørn wiinblad (b. 1918, Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/1900a186cc4988b77ef68bd4dd1a9c25125b409f5103dda8792a0a3413bc7b61.jpg) While the Danish designer Bjørn Wiinblad has worked in numerous design disciplines including textiles, glassware, silverware and graphics, he is best known for his exceptionally decorative ceramics adorned with highly stylized and poetic motifs. Wiinblad trained at the Polytechnic in Copenhagen(1936-1939)and then later studied illustration at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts, 1940-1943). Following this, he worked as a ceramicist under Lars Syberg at Taastrup (1943-1946)and was later employed as a designer under Jacob Bang by the Nymølle faïence factory(1946–1956). In 1952, he established his own design studio at Kongens Lyngby, where for the first year he worked alongside Gutte Eriksen (b. 1918). From 1957 onwards Wiinblad designed numerous ceramic pieces for the renowned German porcelain factory Rosenthal, which are distinguished by a whimsical exuberance. Some of Wiinblad's designs possess the playful spirit of Scandinavian folk art, while others (often depicting religious subjects) capture the solemn beauty and mysticism of Byzantine art. His curious hybrid style, which mixes the age-old with the modern, came to epitomize the decorative revival that occurred in Scandinavian design during the 1960s. Wiinblad has also received numerous commissions for the decoration of restaurants and hotels, and has worked as a theatre designer for the Dallas Theater Center and the Pantomime Theatre in Copenhagen's Tivoli Gardens. ![](images/7422a5d8ab9fa62461d64c4c3a5f89a94dd31f4ca11701080bd6f3ca98febbe0.jpg)

![](images/5d92c2fe9ac509c2fa25394db2d328560c745053b274aeabb494656e4bfd0166.jpg) Tapio Wirkkala, Kantarelli (Chanterelle) vase for littala, 1947 – Originally designed for a competition organized by the littala glassworks, this vase was later exhibited at the 1951 (IX) Milan Triennale # tapio wirkkala ![](images/8e6198252f4b6f377ba386a49cb54d737cb575590789252f6c50bc9ed8e614cf.jpg) ## (b. 1915 Hanko – d. 1985 Esbo, Finland) Tapio Veli Ilmaari Wirkkala was one of the great figures of Finnish design, whose work evoked a strong sense of national identity by embracing the traditional materials and processes of Finnish handcraft and the emotionally seductive forms found in nature. He was a designer of remarkable versatility, who worked in a diverse range of disciplines, including glass, ceramic, metalware, furniture, lighting, jewellery, exhibition, graphics, packaging and industrial design. His output ranged from the everyday to the highly exclusive and often blurred the boundaries between art, craft and design. A bushybearded pipe-smoker, Wirkkala took a workmanlike approach to designand acknowledged the need to “get away from the drawing board, to see just how a product is made and get to know the people who will help make it ... [in order to] get a real contact with the job”. Beyond his technical virtuosity, Wirkkala also possessed a remarkable empathy for the materials he used. As he noted, “All materials have their own unwritten laws. This is forgotten far too often. You should never be violent with the material you're working on, and the designer should aim at being in harmony with his material." By sensitively exploring the formal potential of different mediums such as metal, wood, glass, ceramics and plastics, Wirkkala produced innovative and poetic designs that elegantly expressed the materials' intrinsic qualities. Through his extensive body of work he pioneered an expressive language of organic form that was astonishingly modern, yet also projected a sense of timeless beauty. This aspect of his work resulted from the way in which he tirelessly honed his designs, often drawing a hundred sketches just to perfect one model. Deeplyinspired by and spiritually connected to the natural environment that surrounded him, Wirkkala used forms that captured the abstract essence of the natural world – from birds and leaves to the frosted patterns made by ice. He was also interested in the Lapp(Sami) culture and drew much inspiration from it: he employed the age-old form of a traditional Lapp kurska (a wooden drinking vessel), for example, as the starting point for a number of his ceramic cup designs. Guided by a dialogue between “eye, hand and thought”, Wirkkala producedobjects that can be seen as a unity of opposites – traditional and innovative, rational and romantic, organic and geometric, artistic and functional, visual and tactile, ![](images/cf8eb80714e1667aee1278d47af97e8dc1aba57e906b4b90f7d4c72b2d9d0175.jpg) Tapio Wirkkala, Century tea service for Rosenthal, 1979 ∇ Tapio Wirkkala, Model no. 3851 Paperbag vase for Rosenthal, 1977 ![](images/8177846ffab0d4c30ead35fbc3a580a6bd5c7493c9f5757c146cb7ecb71102a6.jpg) ![](images/01a2e74ef42f191bb160c5963c6772884a7c0514650b93378892e2bbb6e6fdda.jpg) national and international. Frequentlyhe brought ancient craft sensibilities to the design of modern objects for mass production, whether it was for a range of inexpensive pressed glassware or a plastic-handled hunting knife. Having studied sculpture at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design), Helsinki, from 1933 to 1936, he later worked as a sculptor and graphic designer. In 1947, he shared first prize with Kaj Franck in a glassware competition organized by littala and subsequently produced designs for the glassworks on a freelance basis until his death in 1985. His blown-glass Kantarelli vases, the forms of which were inspired by chanterelle mushrooms, captured the fluid organic abstraction that came to exemplify post-war Scandinavian design. This series of vases was produced from 1947 to 1960 and helped establish his reputation internationally. Wirkkala was also widely celebrated for his laminated wood leaf-shaped bowls and furniture, which although made from an essentially man-made material, possessed an extraordinary natural beauty. The multi-coloured wood laminates were laid up by him and then cut and scooped to reveal a remarkable striped effect. Using this technique, he also produced a series ofoutstanding plywood sculptures and sculptural reliefs that demonstrated his complete mastery of expressive abstracted form. His designs were shown at the 1951 (IX)and1954(X)Milan Triennale wherethey were awardedsix Grand Prix. Over his career he won many other awards, including the Lunning Prize in 1951, the Golden Obelisk, Domus Italia in 1963 and the Prince Eugen Medal in 1980. From 1951 to 1954 Wirkkala was the artistic director of the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu, Helsinki, and later, between 1955and 1956, worked brieflyin the New York office of Raymond Loewy (1893–-1986). While Wirkkala worked extensively for the littala glassworks, producing a plethora of memorable and highly influential designs – such as his Ultima Thule range (1968), which spawned the widespread fashion for frosted glassware in the 1970s – he also designed colourful glassware for Venini, ceramics for Rosenthal, knives for Hackman, furniture forAsko and lighting for Airam. He designed a number of exquisite hand-made jewellery pieces that were executed by Mexican silversmiths, packaging (including the well-known Finlandia vodka bottle), domestic appliances, electrical fittings and ![](images/377217269182bcb28fdfb6154a50856d70a270b9fa252123b8c3adaa64148a40.jpg) ![](images/cff13c3dfeb8db7daa100dbec58dd2a5b0364395611fa71cdbf0773cf7f0cfac.jpg) 4 Tapio Wirkkala, Kurve flatware for Rosenthal, 1968 Δ Tapio Wirkkala, Pollo vases for Rosenthal, 1970 ∇ Tapio Wirkkala, Tapio silver flatware for Kultakeskus, 1955-1957 ![](images/92835b175dba77bfd06165301311108dc13f757708300db3eeed672396bccb37.jpg) ![](images/c816b3c89d399135c29b78fce056ac9a6489a057db64b590e4f690e370c250b1.jpg) Tapio Wirkkala, Model no. TW412 silver candelabrum (hand-made to order), 1969 Tapio Wirkkala, Model no. TW2 silver-plated alpaca vase executed by Nils Westerback, 1954 ![](images/a144973a8cf97198eaecc0b23c10032e83a09bd1511808be331850abc29343a0.jpg) ![](images/1b582f52ece9c838f2715aabe7329dc81454f2e063088200849daa2dea2ed533.jpg) Δ Tapio Wirkkala, Tapio glassware range for littala, 1954 – The air-bubble in the stem of each glass was made with a wet stick ▶ Tapio Wirkkala, doubleheaded cased glass vases for littala, 1955-1956 even a lavatory seat. A recent travelling retrospective exhibition(2000-2001) has led to a profound reappraisal ofWirkkala's work, which has entranced and mesmerized audiences through the sheer breadth of its expression. Although Wirkkala once observed, “No one has ever invented new forms. It's only a question of the way in which line and shape are used”, his unquestionable form-giving skills enabled him tocreate objects of extraordinary originality that are imbued with intelligence, gestural simplicity, inviting tactility and enchanting beauty. More than any other designer, Wirkkala's extensive body of work powerfully embodied the underlying humanist ethos that has shaped and continues to define Scandinavian design. ![](images/336b4c6822432fc3d03d88182e97600daedbbfa05ba4436a9f82ee300ec15a9d.jpg) Carl-Axel Acking, bentwood chair for Svenska Möbelfabrikerna, Bodafors, 1944 # carl-axel acking (Swedish 1910-2001) ![](images/2b8924041bef1fe7263f0938db72ea826d25776a009bfc7e265c6a07a94eb4a7.jpg) The Swedish architect and designer Carl-Axel Acking trained inStockholm at the Konstfackskolan (UniversityCollege of Arts, Crafts and Design, 1930–1934) and at the Kungliga Tekniska Högskolan (Royal Institute of Technology, 1934– 1939). Duringthe early 1930s, he also worked for the great Swedish functionalist Erik Gunnar Asplund, assisting withthe design of the courthouse in Gothenburg. Acking participated in the 1937“Exposition Internationale des Arts et Techniques dans la Vie Moderne” in Paris and two years later exhibited his work at the 1939 New York World's Fair. That same year he established his own studio in Stockholm. Unlike the majority of Swedish architects, Acking not only designed buildings but also interiors, furniture and lighting. In 1944he designed his award-winning bentwood chair with moulded plywood seat and back sections as an easy-to-assemble construction that was eminently suited to high-volumeproduction. Apart from factory-made furniture produced by Nordiska Kompaniet and SvenskaMöbelfabrikerna in Bodafors, Acking also designed more exclusive pieces that were executed by various craftsmenin Stockholm and sold through the Hantverket (the sales organization of Stockholm's handicrafts association). His numerous architectural designs included the Hässelby Hotel in Stockholm (1954-1956), while his interiorswork includedseveral important schemes for, among others, North Star Line ships, the Swedish Embassy in Tokyo, and the Hotel Malmen in Stockholm (1949). For this latter project he designedsome innovative armchairs with fully upholstered seat shells that could be removed from their cradle-like frames for cleaning and repair. For the same scheme he also designed a sturdy yet simple laminated beech dining chair that comprised just five easy-to-assemble elements. As a prominent member of theSwedish design community, Acking was commissioned to design a building for the landmark “H55”exhibition in Helsingborg in 1955.
