Over the course of his career he produced a number of designs for wallpapers, textiles, light fittings, a telephone booth (1944), and a postage-stamp vending machine. Acking also taught design for many years at the Konstfackskolan in Stockholm(1943-1964), and from 1965to 1976 he was professor of theoretical and applied aestheticsat the University of Lund. In recognition of his achievements, Acking became the first Swedish designer to be awarded the prestigious Lunning Prize in 1952. Although much of Acking's furniture did not possess the elegance normally associated with Scandinavian design, it did possess an honest constructional simplicity and robustness that made it highly suitable for contract use and mass production. ![](images/b91b2edaca600f23706f56871c67b613acb7fedaab010224274af18e8c4a73cf.jpg) Carl-Axel Acking, Trienna armchair for Nordiska Kompaniet,1957 – later reissued by Källemo ∇Carl-Axel Acking, interior with Trienna armchair, c. 1957 ![](images/e31c678f0f9ece3b2e021fd54a08bb39e345df29712fc4fd1f460e6f540d5881.jpg) ![](images/c6db676d1bde3d09b836ce47c51e466b2e17c2af56a942553c4107943cf4a986.jpg) Carl-Axel Acking, telephone booths for the Royal Board of Telegraph & Telereklam, 1944

![](images/5542acd34c2278f7caccf28b47d939004107d96c17c3c6080281d15d9f151be0.jpg) Lis Ahlmann, woven upholstering textile, 1958 > Lis Ahlmann, Pinjekogler “rya” panel, 1947 # lisaan ![](images/679fab8359b3ea9538c8eef08da4e9ac72bd3018be4469372a7878b15daf6383.jpg) Lis(Elisabeth) Ahlmann initially trained as a painter under Harald Giersing in 1919. Between 1917 and 1921, she also worked as a ceramics painter in Herman Kahlers' workshop in Næstved and later from 1922 to 1929 she apprenticed as a weaver at Gerda Henning's (1891-1951) workshop in Copenhagen. She subsequently worked as a freelance designer producing woven textiles for furniture by Kaare Klint, Børge Mogensenand Mogens Koch. Duringthemid-1930s, Ahlmann established her own weaving workshop in Copenhagen, and later in 1948her work was included in the exhibition “Danish Art Treasures through the Ages” held in London. She also participated at the Milan Triennale (from 1957), and her work was included in the American touring exhibition “Design in Scandinavia”(1954–1957). During the1950s, Ahlmann moved away from hand-woven textiles in favour ofindustrially-produced fabrics that were distinguished by their simple checked and striped patterning and muted earth tones. Like many Scandinavian designers, Ahlmann used traditional craft processes, such as weaving on a handloom, as an aid in the conception and planning of designs intended for industrial manufacture. From 1953, she worked with Børge Mogensen as a design consultant for the Copenhagen-based textile manufacturer C. Olesen, and also designed fabrics with him for the Cotil company. During this period, Mogensen persuaded Ahlmann to adopt a bolder use of colour and to design textiles that could be woven on more sophisticated power looms. For her design work she received the prestigious EckersbergMedal in 1964 and later the C.F. Hansen Medal in 1978. As a leading modern designer who made the transition from craft-based production to industrial manufacture, Ahlmann was one of the key individuals responsible for the post-war renewal of Scandinavian textile design. Her work became synonymous with the uncluttered yet homely Nordic look that became so popular in America and Britain during the late-1940sand1950s. ![](images/501a3af7c82a112b3e6829e43ff9212751d4947bd2acfba425daec5076a9a11a.jpg) ![](images/b9bf04eb64982ad174c8b6d1d3be1cad3f6317dac3a450597be0019cddbd92dc.jpg) Kurt Ekholm, Sinivalko (White-Blue) tableware for Arabia, 1936 ∇ Exterior of Arabia factory in Helsinki ## arabia (found 173 lsik lad) ![](images/14a96f9ec034259ccdcad3bddf9d46253dfc63251b0a3b614798a3da924ded64.jpg) ![](images/a655ac2ec6f294df88afa5d59a884fe95931b4422bdb382102707ef203d44daf.jpg) Finland's oldest and largest ceramics manufacturer, Arabia, was established in the Arabia district of Helsinki as a subsidiary of the Swedish company Rörstrand in 1873. Initially it was intended to produce wares for the Russian market anduntil 1900 the factory mainly manufactured designs originating from Rörstrand, including tableware (earthenware and porcelain), sanitary products, and tiled stoves. During the 1890s, the factory under the directorship of Gustav Herlitz appointed the architect Jacob Ahrenberg(1847–1914) as its artistic adviser. In 1896, Arabia also hired its first in-house designer, Thure Öberg, who designed and decorated a number of distinctive vases. Four years later the factory exhibited at the 1900 Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale”, where it was awarded a gold medal for the excellence of its wares. From 1900 onwards, Arabia manufactured products of its own design, such as the distinctive Capella earthenware range with printed ornamentation ofinterlocking forms, and theFennia ware range(1912)that was intended specifically for the American market. This latter product range, which wasdistinguished by its stylized decoration, was designed by the Finn Eric Ehrström, who was better known for his Arts & Crafts style architectural metalwork thatadorned buildings by Gesellius, Lindgren & Saarinen. In 1916, Carl Gustav Herlitz succeeded his father as managing director of the factory and the same year Arabia separated from its parent company, Rörstrand, marking a new chapter in its history. Between 1916and 1929, the factory underwent an extensive programme of modernization, which included the installation of the world's longest funnel kiln (in 1929)that measured an impressive 112metres. During this period, Arabia began producingnotable designs such as Thure Öberg's vases, which were decorated with Aubrey Beardsley-like female nudes. During the early 1930s Greta-Lisa Jäderholm-Snellman (1894-1973) also designed a number of pieces for the factory, includingan elegant black porcelain covered jar (1930) with a softly undulating yet classical form, and her earthenware Koti dinner service. The latter was simplydecorated with hand-painted motifs of flowers and countryside scenes, which revealed the influence of the Art Deco style. Her work became increasingly plain, and by the mid-1930s she was experimenting with different ceramic techniques, including Toini Muona, vases for Arabia, c. 1945 ∇Kaj Franck, Kilta tableware for Arabia, 1948-1952 ![](images/56029ebf17dad4cd37c9cda9a6f13258f9fd54b88cbec7c832b66e740d6a30dd.jpg) crackled glazes like her fellow-designer at Arabia, Kurt Ekholm, whofounded and headed Arabia's legendary art department from 1932to 1948. During his tenure there, Ekholm produced his practicalModel AH service(1935)and his wellknown utilitarian Sinivalko service(1936), which reflected the international flowering of functionalism. Under his guidance, Arabia began producing designs by, among others, Toini Muona, Aune Siimes(1909–1964),Michael Schilkin, Birger Kaipiainen, and Rut Bryk (b. 1916). The factory received international acclaim for its exhibits as wellas four awards at the 1937 “Exposition des Maître de l’Art Indépendant” in Paris. Two years later, Arabia had expanded its operation to such an extent that it had over 1,000 employees and had become the leading producer of ceramics in Europe. In 1940, Arabia became part of Wärtsilä Oy AB and during the remaining years of the Second World War continued to grow. In testimony to the factory's increasing importance, Ekholm establishedits own museum in 1948(which is still open today). Another major figure at Arabia during the immediate post-war years was Kaj Franck, who established and directed the design-planning ![](images/e895f46fbfed2feb7e372f15daa8cbea9df72aeff22caf4a0f7d782798e40b4a.jpg) ![](images/292ce1614fe3b34bbd8a4f256bfec09135b2d44ee72326566456de631374197c.jpg) ![](images/1feb33c8499a4df2f66d75d273311173e9f54d43a54373cb6b9d14c4ef3773de.jpg) Δ Pekka Paikkari, Toi salt and pepper shakers for Arabia, 1997 △Kati Tuominen-Niittylä, Storybirds pitchers for Arabia, 1993 Pia Törnell, Tilda stackable covered bowls for Arabia, 1997 studio from 1945. The studio was responsible for the design of utility wares, such as the revolutionary Kilta range(1948) made from heat-resistant earthenware. Available in a number of “mix-andmatch” plain colours – white, black, green, dark blue and yellow – this inexpensive standard range could be purchased as “open stock” rather than as a traditional service, and was multi-functional, being suitable not only as tableware but also for cooking and serving. The Kilta pieces were also interchangeable, for example, the small dish could also be used as a saucer. Its elemental undecorated forms ensuredits functional practicality aswell as its suitability for mass-production. A similar heatproof multi-functional cooking/serving range known as Liekki (Flame) was later designed by UllaProcopéin 1957. Procopé also designed the highly influential Ruska service (1960), as well as her much-loved blue and white Valencia tableware range (c. 1960), which has remained in continuous production since its introduction. Around1960, Birger Kaipiainen also produceda landmark tableware design for Arabia, the Paratiisi (Paradise) service with its colourful explosion of pansies, blackcurrants, plums, grapes and apples. Originally produced in earthenware, the range was later reissued usinghigher-fired stoneware in 1987. Alongside its mass-manufactured products, Arabia has always produced “art ceramics”, such as Kaipiainen's bead birds of the 1960s and the Pro Arte collectionsissued from 1987 onwards. In1990, the Hackman Groupacquired Arabia (as well as littala and Rörstrand), and the following year theproduction of bone chinawas moved from GustavsbergtoArabia. More recently, Arabia has produced innovative ceramics – both utilitarian and art-based products – by among others Stefan Lindfors, JamesIrvine (b.1958) and Harri Koskinen. ![](images/d3466da5f6890e2c44b3db52656e84ea26e4028d27d9e2aed91a76c6acc0458d.jpg)

▶ Folke Arström, Focus de Luxe cutlery for Gense, 1955-1956 ![](images/518b835afd2608e9af920cbfcad3ca673247cfc69a5d0ee654699aa57546d9bd.jpg) Folke Arström, cocktail shaker for GuldsmedsAktie-Bolaget, 1935 # fol arsröm (b. 1907 Brma, srden- d. 1997) ![](images/ee75695e777d48f803e200ad2b9c50171485bc13e61202d995846aa4567be793.jpg) Bornin Bromma, Folke Emanuel Arström trained at the craft school in Linköping prior to studying fine artat the Kungliga Tekniska Högskolan (Royal Institute of Technology,1927–1929) and later at the Kungliga Konsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts, 1929-1936)in Stockholm. During his studies, he also worked as a painter andgraphic designer and was most notably employed at the 1930Stockholm Exhibition. In 1931 he undertook a number of commissions for decorative painting in Linköping, including a scheme at the Röda Kvarn cinema. Three years later he established his own studio and began designing posters and maps for the Statens Historika Museum (NationalHistorical Museum, Stockholm), as well as decorative plans for a number of other museums. Between 1935 and 1940 he designed products in silver and pewter ware for the GuldsmedsAktieBolaget, including his famous cocktail shaker of 1935. The streamlined form of this design, which can be seen as a celebration of Modernity, was functionally well conceived in that its concave/convex profile enabled a good grip for vigorous shaking. From 1936 Arström was also the chief designer for Gense and later from 1940 to 1960 he served as the company's artistic director. For Gense, Arström designed simple undecorated stainless steel flatware such as his Thebe range (1944) and his well-known Focus range(1955-1956),which epitomized the understated elegance of post-war Scandinavian design. The Focus pattern was especially successful in the United States, where it helped to promote the use of high-qualitystainless steel flatware instead of silverware for formal dining. During his career, Arström also designed for Bahco, Skånska Ättiksfabriken, Primusfabrikerna and Annebergfabrikerna, and received numerous awards, including a gold medal at the 1951 (IX) Milan Triennale and the Gregor Paulsson Trophy in 1961 for his pioneering stainless steel and plastic product designs. ![](images/72b1f01709c6699832cac24883bb3c5cf5a685722daca34bf2d1caaa52051959.jpg) ![](images/95eec01e10c77e8b53e9659502b3c2bebc59d02d0a896928b6b46428674637ec.jpg) ▶Interior of Artek showroom, Helsinki, 1930s ## arte Tfod 1 i , Filan) ![](images/c0ad51a9db9556a0d36a2044da10d2e3b22fabaf80323596247caf7bdb0a2d72.jpg) During the 1920s, the Modern Movement began popularizing the idea of industrially produced furnishings for the home, while at the same time contemporary streamlined forms became increasingly accepted by mainstream consumers. Against this background of change within the domestic environment, Alvar Aalto and hiswife Aino Aalto began developing highly innovative modern furniture using state-of-the-art production methods. Their resulting bent plywood and laminated wood designs possessed soft yet elegant humanizing forms that contrasted strongly with the alienating steel and glass machine aesthetic associated with Bauhaus design. Aalto's alternative soft-edged Modernism was especially appreciated in Britain, where his furniture was displayed at an exhibition of Finnish design in 1933 that was sponsored by the Architectural Review magazine. Bolstered by the export orders that resulted from this exhibition, which washeld at the Fortnum&Mason department store in London, as well as the widespread interestgenerated atthe 1933 (V) Milan Triennale, Alvar Aalto, Aino Aalto, Nils-Gustav Hahl, and the fine arts patrons and collectors Harry and Maire Gullichsen, founded a sales and marketing company known as Artek Oy AB in October 1935. This new enterprise was intended as a showcase for modern furniture and household fittings, and as a forum for design exhibitions and the Modernist cause in general. By 1939, Alvar and Aino Aalto had designed the majority of the pieces in Artek's extensive collection, which ranged from traditionally joined straight-legged stacking chairs to formed plywood and laminated wood designs such as the seminalNo.41(1931-1932)andthecantilevered No. 31(1931–1932). Alvar Aalto also designed his ubiquitousL-leg stacking stools(1932–1933), which featured a revolutionary leg construction – one of his most functionally versatile innovations. During the 1940s, the Y-legrange was incorporated into the Artek collection, as was the fan-shaped X-/eg rangein the 1950s. With Artek, Aalto's aim was to produce the widest selection of furniture with the fewest and simplest elements. He believed that the soul of a designwas derivedfrom its most fundamental component and stated, “To make furniture you need a basic element, a structural standard part, which modified in some way, appearsin all pieces. Sine qua non: apart from its structural characteristics, the basic element must have a ![](images/8b321ab912485e6367da0cf7bb8abd2cba072c94943ae8d5dc88115d114e85e3.jpg) purposeful and style-creating form." Today, Artek continues to market Aalto-designed textiles, lamps and furniture (which is still made by Korhonen Oy, as it has been for over 60 years), while also producing newer designs that possess a complementary aesthetic by, among others, Ben af Schultén (b. 1939), and Hanspeter Weidmann (b. 1958). Artek furnishings are completely synonomous with the Modern Scandinavian interior and are distinguished by simple organic lines and warm natural materials that give a sense of homely practicality. ![](images/bb3c8df8614fbd70a516ab9ddc5bae3759f239412ec6f7f7e8045cd98af26fec.jpg) △Alvar Aalto, Model no. 41 Paimio chair for Artek, 1931-1932 4 Alvar Aalto, Model no. Y61 stool for Artek, 1946-47 OVERLEAF Alvar Aalto, Model no. 100 screen for Artek, 1935-1936 Ben af Schultén, Espa chair for Artek, 1999 ## 保

![](images/22a245dd24a5e22b4f2c2b9c647f5837ba30ff763b6fce2116b9218ddb17f3aa.jpg) Helena Bodin, Mug cups for Asplund, 2000 ## asplud een) ![](images/61b965b7e041ff35e177897ed2769f8ee0baac0547a52bd2df1691d93e5aadb8.jpg) The renowned Swedish design-led furniture and accessories manufacturing company,Asplund, was established by two brothers, Christian and Thomas Asplund (who had worked earlier as a bank clerk and art dealer respectively) in response to the revitalization of Scandinavian design activity that took place during the late 1980s and early 1990s. Their intention was to produce experimental “new wave” furniture, and in this they were quided by their belief that it was better to produce small production runs than to compromise aesthetics in order to achieve a greater universality of appeal. Soon after its founding, the Asplund gallery became famed for its Neo-Nordic furniture and rug collections, which were designed by Sweden's leading young designers, including Thomas Eriksson, ThomasSandell, Jonas Bohlin and Matz Borgström. Some Asplund products such as Matz Borgström's O rug (1995) powerfully communicate meaningthrough a system of code-like symbols, while others such as ThomasSandell's Lucia candlestick (1995) are characterized by a striking elemental simplicity. Often the carpets, such as Bohlin's Rug on Rug (1998), have a playful post-modern quality, while typically the furniture is functionally innovative, as in Helene Tiedemann's Towe/ Table (1997) with its rail for hanging tea-towels. Asplund's stylish products are informed by the classic Scandinavian humano-centric approach to design that focuses particularly on the demands of the domestic environment. Since 1995, the company has marketed its designs through the Swedish distribution organization Swecode, which was founded in 1994 by the furniture manufacturing company CBI. In the late 1990s, Asplund began manufacturing designs by avant-garde British designers, such as Tom Dixon (b. 1959) and James Irvine (b. 1958). Today, its Stockholm-based gallery has become one of the leadingshowcases of cutting-edge design in Sweden. ![](images/b7a7815ff4d2231af11f75179cef3cf8944ba0344fe8876bb929e6d7714af42a.jpg) ![](images/c919c070429c47a6d3201a4a86002a2567ec1370ca4b720868f53ed3f5255940.jpg) 4 Pia Wallén, CVX/CVE Convex/Concave rug for Asplund, 1996 - produced from 1998 △ Matz Borgström, O rug for Asplund, 1995 ∇Thomas Sandell, Lucia candlesticks for Asplund, 1995 – also produced by Cappellini ![](images/ac166748838307aaed7243eee2e15b140413f8a192a9999b94afe23881531482.jpg)

Erik GunnarAsplund, Model no. GA-2 armchair for Nordiska Kompaniet, c. 1931- later reissued by Källemo ▶Erik Gunnar Asplund, Karmsto/ chair for Nordiska Kompaniet, 1931 ![](images/6010a8f31c040885d5b059e715437099a567a9ca079f1c78c427fed8ebb66d54.jpg) # erik gunnar asplund (b. 1885 Stockholm – d. 1940 Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/574a1bc8411b297d64f5c94ba93270f0fbbc34ec0eddc3ad8fed1e5622c7b76f.jpg) One of the most prominent advocates of functionalism in Scandinavia and generally considered tobe Sweden's most important architect, Erik Gunnar Asplund fused common sense with artistic intuition in his life-long search for balanced unity and clarity of form. Having studied at the Kungliga Konsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts), Stockholm, from 1905 to 1909 he initially pursued a career in painting. From 1909, however, he concentrated on architecture and shortly thereafter opened his own architectural practice in Stockholm. He became editor of the journal Teknisk Tidskrift Arkitektur in 1917, and the same year received much praise for his interiors exhibited at the Liljevalchs ArtGallery in Stockholm. From 1911 to 1930, he designed a number of Neo-Classical inspired buildings, most notably the Scandia Cinema, Stockholm (1923), and the Stockholm City Library (1928). During this period he also designed furnishings that were similarly classicist in style, such as hisSenna chair of 1925. Asplund's work underwent a dramatic transformation when he was appointed chief architect for the 1930 Stockholm Exhibition organized by the Svenska Sljödföreningen (Swedish Society of Craft and Design). At this event, which drew significant international recognition, his Modernist glass and metal exhibition building introduced the International Style to Sweden for the first time. By combining Modern functionalism with the grace of Scandinavian Neo-Classicism, Asplund developed a language of design that was less severe than that of contemporary German Modernist architects. Among Asplund's many other achievements was the design of the Skogskykogården, Stockholm, and an extension to the Town Hall in Gothenburg. From 1931 to 1940, he was also professor of architecture at Kungliga Konsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts), Stockholm. ![](images/377ca7d0a11e5ad1bc69b3ff0d07d51f227fdbbec76684fc54a83f36ea343e38.jpg)

![](images/ca43cdf2e4ac3b1e2039034dfd28ac07d95c1600c9214de51e10000ad98e2aa1.jpg) ![](images/1d802e9005bb5b5859b06dcd34399a10e9b232ff88f6b7ed2acdb77baa04f7a4.jpg) David Lewis, Beavision 5 plasma television, 2002 v Peter Bang and Svend Olufsen, c. 1925 # bang & olufsen o 1 2 su, Denar) Known throughout the world for its stylish stateof-the-art consumer electronic equipment, Bang & Olufsen was founded in 1925 by Peter Bang (1900–1957) and Svend Olufsen (1897–1949) in the remote western Jutland region of Denmark. Peter Bang's father, Camillo, was manager of the progressive Copenhagen department store A. C. Illum (founded 1891), which achieved remarkable success through an innovative sales strategy of offering middle-class customers a wide range of high-quality products by means of a knowledgeable and friendly staff. The store was managed by Camillo Bang in a way that saw the “customer as a person", and this no doubt had a formative influence on the way Peter Bang would later approach the design, manufacture and retail of his electronic products. Indeed, as a child he began experimenting with radio equipment, but found that the many batteries he required needed continual recharging, which involved the use of potentially hazardous acid. Frustrated by the shortcomings of these batteries, Bang eliminated the need for them altogether by building a mains powered radio receiver. Later, Bang trained as an engineer at the Àrhus Electrotechnical School, where he graduated in 1924. One of his fellow students was Svend Olufsen, who subsequently undertook radio experiments at his family's estate in Quistrup. While attempting to build a mains receiver, Olufsen turned to Bang for help. Bang had just returned from America, where he had been researching the latest developments in radio technology as pioneered by the Radio Corporation of America, which under the visionary leadership of David Sarnoff had commenced manufacture of its revolutionary Radio Music Box. In November 1925, the two friends officially founded Bang & Olufsen, which was committed to the idea of “power for the radio directly from the mains." To this end they began working on an aggregate to connect battery receivers to mains power supply which resulted in the introduction in 1926 of the firm's first marketed product, the B&O Eliminator. A year later, they developed their own mains powered radio known as the Five Lamper, which had a wooden cabinet inlaid with classical motifs and three Bakelite dials, Although B&O equipment was at the cutting-edge of radio technology and renowned for the clarity of its sound, it was wrapped up as period-style furniture. In 1933, ![](images/553173600442376b67b42be272e677a452a5b041b71abe4825dc6aa88a067c10.jpg) the Five Lamper radio and radio gramophone were lambasted for their old-fashioned aesthetic when the prominent architect and designer Ole Wanscher commented ironically, “To make the radio fit in nicely is easily achieved through Caucasian walnut and badger legs or Chinoiserie – like a theatre design conceived by the furniture dealer's youngest apprentice",. Taking this criticism to heart, Peter Bang and Svend Olufsen decided to embrace a Modern aesthetic that would be in keeping with the advanced technology they were pioneering. The same year Bang designed the sleek Hyperbo 5 RG Steel radio-gramophone, which was inspired by Marcel Breuer's (1902– 1981) tubular metal cantilevered chairs. This remarkably forward-looking design was sold alongside other radios and radio-gramophones that continued to be housed in Art Deco inspired wooden cabinets. The real breakthrough for the company came in 1938, however, when it developed a radio that was housed in a casing made entirely of the new “material of a thousand uses” – Bakelite. While the elegant Beolit 39 was inspired by the streamlined forms of American industrial design, such as those pioneered by Raymond ![](images/9d1a4712220becd1d58e5cae224dae7dc86116a344bd63c8f199e93698219f25.jpg) 4Δ Bang & Olufsen team, radio gramophone Five Lamper 5 RG Funkis for Bang & Olufsen, 1932 – a "Chinoiserie" model that led to criticism from Ole Wanscher Δ Bang & Olufsen team, Hyperbo 5 RG Steel radio gramophone for Bang & Olufsen, 1934 – inspired by Peter Bang's office chair that had been designed by the Bauhaus designer, Marcel Breuer ∇ Bang & Olufsen team, Beolit 39 radio, 1938 – The idea for Bang & Olufsen's first streamlined design to have a casing made completely from Bakelite was generated by a listeners' competition ![](images/7758d8b3c935bbe2308e22cd77147ddd91f1c966f65019b6d9fd5ea1c7ffc918.jpg) ban lug 8 14 ![](images/6f9a9fc881f197e3d4913a9a03e0308cee7cf0b17ac1e0e9b50874e4f63342d6.jpg) ![](images/7be37c91ce72ab87c656596c5b2d778b678b4ba0036a24048f2471ff5efc6d33.jpg) ![](images/8772a2d3eb2d825e50f273ac99f603e5cbbd2c9559449d007c486e380dd287fb.jpg) ΔΔJacob Jensen (assisted by David Lewis), Beolab 5000 hi-fi system for Bang & Olufsen, 1967 - this revolutionary design introduced the new slide-rule dial Δ Jacob Jensen, Beolit 400-600 radios for Bang & Olufsen, 1970 >△ David Lewis, BeoCom 2 telephone for Bang & Olufsen, 2000 Loewy (1893-1986) and Harold L. van Doren (1895–1957), it also predicted the sleek rationalism of much post-war industrial design, such as that promoted by Dieter Rams (b. 1932) at Braun. During the Second World War,B&O established an illegal radio service for communications between the British and the Danish Freedom Fighters. Because of the company's links with the resistance, B&O's factory was bombed in 1945. Shortly afterwards, the factory was rebuilt and production of radios resumed, butagain the company was criticized, this time by the celebrated designer Poul Henningsen, who said its products had the appearance of furniture-like pastiches. To justify the company's by-line – “The Danish Hallmark of Quality” – B&O began working with some of Denmark's leading architects and designers in the mid-1950s, includingSigvard Bernadotte and Acton Bjørn.These collaborations produced spectacular results – the company's new generation high-quality products were completely integrated, and their refined Modern forms communicated a sophisticated design rhetoric to a new and highly receptive design-conscious audience. In the 1960s, B&O pioneered the design of integrated stereorecord players, “for those who discusstaste and quality before price”. Having become a pioneer of audio technology, the company later launched several landmark designs by JacobJensen, which married superlative quality with sleek good looks. Jensen's “easy touch” layouts and “flush-designed” systems quite literally redefinedthe design vocabulary of hi-fi equipment. Throughout the 1960s and 1970s, Jensen's designs came to epitomize both the technical and aesthetic refinement of Danish design. During the 1980s, however,the company suffered badly from Asian competition and eventually formed an alliance with Philipsin order to survive. In the early 1990s, B&O changed its approach to manufacturing, moving strategically from mass-production to order-production. There was also an important shift in design terms, which was implemented by David Lewis (b. 1939), who had been Jensen's assistant. Lewis' revolutionary Beosystem 2500 (1990), with its all-in-one layout, set itself apart from the anonymous stacked black boxes produced by B&O's competitors. More recently David Lewis has designed Bang & Olufsen's first plasmascreen television, the Beo-Vision 5, which can either be hung on or propped up against a wall – an innovative, flexible and elegant solution thatwas inspired by paintings in a gallery that were being removed to make room for a new exhibition. Believing that aesthetics are as important to consumers as function and ease of use, Bang & Olufsen skilfully balances external design with internal technological considerations in order to create distinctive products that powerfully communicate the company's values and vision about technology and product concepts. Today, the company continues its long-standing commitment to good design while remaining faithful to its founder's guiding principle: “A never-failing will to want to create the best and find new ways." ![](images/d9aed2943281c0ce2ece7b3dfd9726bf6cacb443c9faf5c326081e52d7866972.jpg) Δ David Lewis, Beosystem 2500 upright “all-in-one" system for Bang & Olufsen, 1991 Δ David Lewis, Beosystem 9000 CD-player forBang & Olufsen, 1996 – this system allows up to six compact disks to be played from any linked room in a house ![](images/2fa9908c27f6c4ea38f407b772a5bc9a522d86d488c8b9448cd3102c4985db91.jpg) Jacob Bang, Primula glassware for Holmegaard, 1930 ▶ Jacob Bang, Hogla wine glass for Holmegaard, 1928 #

![](images/fd538ce291727b85a498f4917513917f0f3eee6c16935c99c74b5e7d02b9db87.jpg) Jacob E. Bang initially studied sculpture and later trained as an architect at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) from 1916 to 1921. In 1924 he began working as an industrial designer for the Holmegaard glassworks and four years later was appointed artisticdirector. Having left Holmegaardin1942 Bang subsequently established his own studio and began working as a freelance designer. Between 1942and 1957 he designedceramics for the Nymølle faïence factory and metalware products for Pan Aluminium and the silversmith F. Hingelberg. In 1957 he was appointed artistic director of the Kastrup glassworks, which subsequently merged with Holmegaard in 1965. While at Kastrup, Bang designed no-nonsense glassware that corresponded with his guiding principle that designed products should be sensible, simple and pleasurable to use. As one of the leading functionalists in Denmark, Bang produced numerous glassware designs, such as his well-known Hogla wine glass and his extensive Primula range (both produced byHolmegaard), which were notable for their simplified rational forms. Although his work was influenced by the Modern Movement, Bang steered clear of the dogmatic functionalism emanating from Germany and produced designs that were softer and more human-focused. By refining his designs thoroughly until every detail had achieved near functional and aesthetic perfection, Bang was able to create some of the most beautiful and timeless pieces of glassware of the 20th century. ![](images/6a2ff0ed19ce607f4bf4dfa4a3f2dd8bdd1be2559d8dcb09515cb0c2953cd247.jpg)

Sigvard Bernadotte, Model no. 1015 silver coffee set with plastic bound handles for Georg Jensen, c. 1953 ![](images/2b403d9043770724143f629ed26a5fb11676441abb6cc2937d00966ba86b4565.jpg) Sigvard Bernadotte, Model no. 9 Bernadotte silver cutlery for Georg Jensen, 1939 # sigvard bernadotte (b. 1907 Stockholm – d.2002 Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/b8d0f4732f37e38a9f735401e45ed469c28cfe659187c217314d826c9cb09b06.jpg) The son of King Gustav VI Adolf of Sweden Count Sigvard Bernadotte was one of the earliest pioneers of industrial design in Scandinavia. Initially, he studied political science as well as several other subjects at the Faculty of Art, Uppsala University, but it was decided that his artistic talent should be cultivated and so he went on to study drawing and painting under Olle Hjortzberg at the Kungliga Konsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts) in Stockholm from 1929 to 1931. Bernadotte later completed his studies at the Staatsschule fürAngewandte Kunst (National School of Applied Arts), Munich, in 1931, and following this joined the GeorgJensen workshop. Here he became the first designer in the company to adoptgeometric forms as opposed to the naturalistic Arts & Crafts influenced formsthat were so characteristic of the silversmith's output. His Bernadotte flatware of 1939demonstrates the angularity of form that was typical of his work from this period. He also designed a volumetric metal and Bakelite cocktail shaker during the late 1930s for an English manufacturer. During the 1930s he travelled throughout Europe, Africa and, most importantly for his future career, America, where he visited several leading industrial design consultants including Raymond Loewy (1893–1986), Walter Dorwin Teague (1883–1960), Donald Deskey(1894-1989)and Henry Dreyfuss(1904– 1972). Leaving his position as a director of Georg Jensen, he established a Copenhagen-based industrial design consultancy in 1950 with the Danish designer Acton Bjørn (who had previously worked in collaboration with Douglas andBoeing on the design of aircraft for SAS). Known as Sigvard Bernadotte & Acton Bjørn, Industridesign, Merkantil Grafik, this multi-disciplinary practice's first notable success was a compact metal and plastic adding-machine(1952) for Odhner. The practice was later expanded, with offices being opened in Stockholm and New York. Over their careers together, Bernadotte and Bjørn designed a number of widely celebrated industrial products, includingthe melamine Margrethe stacking bowls for Rosti (1950), which had non-slip rubber bases and highly practical pouring spouts and handgrips, and the compact Privat typewriter (1960) for Facit in “Viking grey”, which reflected the influence of Marcello Nizzoli's(1887–1969)earlier Lettera typewriter for Olivetti (1950). Other prod- ## U ![](images/e7ac1ba63e00658537ba9510e61837f5873c20ff20f48288d8a558c9b8ebcc1c.jpg) △△Sigvard Bernadotte, Contex 10 adding machine for Contex, c. 1960 – codesigned with Acton Bjørn △ Sigvard Bernadotte, Odhner adding machine for Odhner, 1952 – co-designed with Acton Bjørn ucts designed by the practice included a cigarette lighter for Consul (a German company), kitchenware for Modernum, Morsø and Electro-Helios, an in-flight tray and cutlery for SAS, a modular kitchen for Formfac, the Silent extractor fan for Bahco, consumer electric equipment for Ericsson and Bang & Olufsen and a plastic picnic set for Husqvarna. Bernadotte & Bjørn was the first industrial design practice in Denmark and one of the earliest in Sweden, andin the 1950s became the first Scandinavian design consultancy to achieve international recognition. In 1964, Bernadotte went on to independently found the Bernadotte Design Studio, which specialized in heavy industrial design, from forklift trucks to outboard motors. As one of the most important pioneers of industrial design in Europe, Sigvard Bernadotte also became the first European designer to be elected to the American Designers' Institute. 4 Sigvard Bernadotte, electric iron for General Electric, c. 1965 ∇ Sigvard Bernadotte, Ginge Precision weighing scales for Ginge, c. 1952-1953 - codesigned with Acton Bjørn ![](images/c4159880a63d7e3553ca3f9abe46aef0ed0c116b99c04102e04334822fbab169.jpg)

Thorvald Bindesbøll, silver beaker for A. Michelsen, 1899 Thorvald Bindesbell, chair made by J.P. Mørck for the 1900 Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale" OVERLEAF Thorvald Bindesbell, earthenware plates made at G. Eifrig's workshop, c. 1900 – with characteristic Art Nouveau cloud-like motifs ![](images/2bf4897565b052d0e0bbc899560ab402139db1563e0c65535d92666eadad3d7b.jpg) # thorvald bindesbøll ## (b. 1846 Copenhagen – d. 1908 Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/83cdd40027631ea431ba1ca1d8eeed89d971dd9ff58cf5300877e398fe24fe1b.jpg) The son of the Danish architect M.G. Bindesbøll, Thorvald Bindesbøll trained as an architect at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design), Copenhagen, where he graduated in 1861. From 1872 he received numerous decorative art commissions that included designs for furniture, embroideries and bookillustrations. In 1882 Bindesbøll exhibited his work both in Denmark and abroad, and the following year began designing ceramics for the J. Walmann Pottery, which were inspired by the art of Japan and natural forms. Also influenced by both William Morris (1834-1896) and the British Arts &CraftsMovement, Bindesbøl developed a highly individual and expressionistic style that was characterized by bold shapes and bizarre patterns that often included abstracted cloud, wave and seaweed-like motifs. From the 1890s onwards, Bindesbøll was the leading Danish designer of his generation and his decorative Art Nouveau style had much influence on many younger designers, such as F. Hegel, Molger Kyster and even Georg Jensen. During the 1890s, Bindesbøll also worked for the Københavns Lervarefabrik and the Kaehler Keramik ceramics factories – the former producing his well-known earthenware dishes decorated with incised cloud-like motifs. His first silverware designs date from 1899; with their more delicate paisley-like motifs, which are reminiscent of those found on Indian textiles, these pieces display great finesse and stylistic innovation. His silver designs for A. Michelsen were subsequently shown at the 1900 Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale”. Throughout his career, Bindesbøll successfully translated his individualistic and forward-looking style into a number of diverse disciplines, including metalwork, leatherwork, jewellery, furniture, textiles, glass, graphics, lighting and stained glass. As one of the leading exponents of the Scandinavian Art Nouveau movement, Bindesbøll's designs were the antithesis of contemporaryindustrially-produced products. His adoption of abstracted decoration and simple forms, however, can be seen as anticipating the later emergence of modern design in Scandinavia. ![](images/3585fac4186c0a0aef78facd3ab4b2ead8ee366085dc87569473d071520b8c07.jpg) ![](images/9ab5913874cad20b5f6a9fe1d3e5cd85575a556555f99ab2d8c3f32484aa737a.jpg) ![](images/2b6d3014e12f2c74be9af17c491a9be51e486f66c652e1a663dbe4092b9dba0b.jpg)

Pietro Krohn, Heron service for Bing & Grøndahl, 1886-1887 ![](images/0687465b4dcbddbf6de97ba90dfebeb0756f1cf08c5cc74edb2d7eeb85aca12a.jpg) Effie Hegermann-Lindencrone, pierced porcelain bowl for Bing & Grøndahl, 1917 # bing & grøndahl (founded 1853 Copenhagen, Denmark) In 1833, Frederik Vilhelm Grøndahl began an as apprentice at the Royal Copenhagen factory and six years later was promoted to the position of designer/sculptor. Eventually he was approached in 1852 by two brothers, Jacob Herman Bing and Meyer Herman Bing. The Bings were established art and paper dealers in Copenhagen and wanted to set up the first privately owned porcelain factory in Denmark. The following year the business was established in Copenhagen under the name Bing & Grøndahl Porcelænsfabrik. Initially, the factory mainly produced large biscuit Neo-Classical porcelain figures and reliefs based on sculptures by the Danish artist Bertel Thorvaldsen (1770-1844), which were retailed through M.H. Bing's various galleries. Around 1880, J.H. Bing's sons Ludvig and Harald joined the factory as business and production managers respectively. Later in 1885, Harald Bing appointed the painter and illustrator Pietro Krohn(1840-1905)as the company's artistic director, which heralded a new progressive direction for Bing & Grøndahl. Three years later, Krohn designed the Heron service, which was remarkable for its austere style and asymmetrical patterning. The design of this service not only reflected the growing interest in Japanese subjects and Orientalism in general, it also predicted the Art Nouveau style. The majority of the products emanating from Bing & Grøndahl, such as those designed by F. A. Hallin, were either in the Beaux Arts style or were strongly influenced by the work of the Royal Copenhagen factory – indeed several artists were employed by both factories. In 1895 Jens F. Willumsen(1863–1958), who like his predecessor oversaw the production of “art” figures as well as more functional wares, succeeded Krohn as artistic director. During the 1920s and 1930s Bing & Grøndahl continued to produce art wares that included stoneware figures by Mogens Bøggild, animal statues by Knud Kyhn(1880-1969), refined classical tableware by, among others, Kay Bojesen, Ebbe Sadolin (b. 1900) and Hans Tegner, and other decorative pieces by Axel Salto, Kai Nielsen and Jean René Gauguin (1881–1961), son of the post-impressionist painter Paul Gauguin. Briefly between 1937 and 1938 Gunnar Nylund was artistic director of the company before returning to Rörstrand. After the Second World War, the company opened a new plant in Valby for the mass production ![](images/055aea3a853da06f8fff66b32c4d1fedda639c3406826fb20648db913d486f7b.jpg) ![](images/e22a06c830d1e8830b657edfd622883a13b5a088379c678efd767095545a4a79.jpg) Thorvald Bindesbøll, butterfly-pattern plate for Bing & Grøndahl, c. 1885 > Axel Salto, vases for Bing & Grøndahl, 1923-1925 & 1925 of tableware, while the original factory in Vestergade continued to produce more artistic wares. In 1947, Finn Juhl received the Eckersberg Medal for his interior design of the Bing & Grøndahl showroom in Copenhagen, which highlighted the company's commitment to design excellence. During the post-war years, Bing & Grøndahl continued producing its earlier designs (including its well-known blue and white porcelain figures) alongside more contemporary wares such as Gertrud Vasegaard's oriental-style stoneware pieces and her Chinese inspired porcelain dinner and tea service. In 1961, Henning Koppel began designing for the Bing & Grøndahl factory. According to him, the “perfect, pure material of this factory invited attempts to go beyond the limits”, with some of his designs being “based entirely on the characteristic of the porcelain”. Other designs by Koppel, such as his Form 24 service, were translations of his work in silver for Georg Jensen. During this period, the company continued to maintain its reputation for welldesigned tableware as well as figurative sculptures, and kept its long-established studio, that not only employed artists on a full-time basis, but also played host to visiting artists, such as Carl-HarryStålhane. In the late 1960s, Erik Magnussen also designed several important pieces for the factory, including a stoneware teapot(1963), the Form 25 Termo service(1965), and a porcelain teapot (1969) which had an extremely innovative double-walled form that was incised to provide an integrated handle. In contrast to Magnussen's restrained modern style, the company also produced humorous sculptures by Sten Lykke Madsen made of stoneware. Eventually the factory merged with Royal Copenhagen in 1987 and the original Vestergade plant was closed, but the company's prestigious studio was retained. In Denmark, Bing & Grøndahl was historically second only in importance to Royal Copenhagen, and as such it played a major role in establishing the Danish reputation for progressive yet high quality design in porcelain and stoneware. ![](images/a3bac5419acb6592ab51a6b0b391a98aed9d862e6b1d08b13e2fe9dbd3757355.jpg) ![](images/d70d2bfd922f77220c8ff39e173fccd6e57328f4e4168d9fdad8a5df5f6e63da.jpg)

![](images/4979c3ce60c9823cf0f06737ba99aa6c05e2a8f79d9a1d35bc56c731b15fb1f9.jpg) Hrafnkell Birgisson, graphic rendering of Homobile seating/ storage unit, 1999 # hrafnl birgion 6b. 1969 Rekjvk Iceland) ![](images/fb6182b3e73f12c3e1e32fa682d70dcbaa3c3a93d34bcd3acf0b9e28cb849c09.jpg) The Icelandic designer Hrafnkell Birgisson studied at the Reykjavik School of Art (1991–1992), the Icelandic College of Arts & Crafts (1992–1993), and the Polytechnic Hafnarfjördur (1993-1994). In 1995 he moved to Germany, where he studied product design at the Hochschule der Bildenden Künste (Academy of Art) inSaarbrücken under Andreas Brandolini(b. 1951), Harald Hullmann and François Burkhardt, and graduated in 2000. During thisfive-year period he also undertook a design studies course taught by Professor Achim Heine (b. 1955)at the Hochschule der Künste (Academy of Arts) in Berlin. Working within the fields of graphic design, furniture design, product design and interior design, Birgisson (who now lives in Berlin) has forged an international reputation for his idiosyncraticwork that often playfully subverts function.He has won numerous prizes, including the Derag Design Award (Munich) in 1997 for his Sleep & Go hotel pod. He also received a scholarship from the DAAD(Deutscher Akademischer Austauschdienst) in 1998. Birgisson's Homobile seating/storage unit (1999) reveals his interest in multi-functional design solutions, while hisHábolli teacup glasses(1998) exhibit the quirkiness that is so characteristic of much contemporary Icelandic design, from graphics to fashion. To date, Birgisson'smost interesting and potentially farthest-reaching design is his Sleep & Go (1997), which is an extremelyinnovative example of low-cost product architecture. Designed with industrial mass production in mind, the Sleep & Go could potentially be used as a form of low-cost easy-to-site emergency shelter. The design also predicts a less materialistic and more nomadic way of living. Birgisson's design solutions reflect the innovative nature of Icelandic design, while offeringtantalizing glimpses ofpossible future alternative lifestyles. ![](images/783e654b7efe7a76e6b704f3a027a8d98c58efb568efb7de3637bd9994435932.jpg) ![](images/083765687eb8acbc6e7548937be15cb7ba4bbf3ef652d2af1dc719ae8851c6eb.jpg) ∆Hrafnkell Birgisson, Sleep & Go hotel pod, 1997 – projection for 2020 ∇Hrafnkell Birgisson,working drawing of Sleep & Go hotel pod, 1997 – projection for 2020 ![](images/9d039129176f55d7b0bbca29149d8e8600d60184fd033b1c313897813df2d40f.jpg) ![](images/42bc47aaaf7797a0a847f1361ef3c2f16838576f8eee7b6a938744a983d388c4.jpg) Acton Bjørn, Facit PI typewriterfor Átvidabergs Industrier, 1958 – co-designed with Sigvard Bernadotte ## acton bjørn ## (b. 1910 Copenhagen – d.1992 Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/6904b45ef59763b720ff76ba2b6ba12f06740107b21b4ff272a3a2fe9cb398b9.jpg) A pioneer of Scandinavian industrial design, Acton Bjørn studied architecture and town planning prior to working as an industrial designer during the Second World War. He subsequently worked with the aircraft manufacturers Douglas and Boeing on the design of aeroplanes forSAs – an experience that gave him a valuable insightinto industrial design practice at one of the highest technical levels. In 1949 he met the designer Sigvard Bernadotte who shared his interests, and the following year they established one of the first Scandinavian industrial design consultancies under the name Sigvard Bernadotte & Acton Bjørn, Industridesign. This Copenhagen-based practice not onlydesigned metalwork and ceramics, but also industrial equipment and machines. Among their best known work was plastic kitchenware for Rosti (1950) and office equipment for Facit (1958). In 1966, Bjørn won a Danish ID Prize for his Beolit 500 transistor radio for Bang& Olufsen. Produced from 1965to1969, this design was one of the first radios to achieve a functional simplicity comparable to that of a telephone. The same year Bjørn established his own design practice, Acton Bjørn Tegnestue, in Copenhagen, which specialized in household products and appliances as well as office furniture and packaging. He also received much recognition for his corporate identity work, mainly executed for companies involved in banking andinsurance. Bjørn's product designs were classically Scandinavian in that they elegantly balanced practical function with simple sculptural forms. ![](images/99e0b21736c16cbf2b025350f9d88105f9488b2dfcf18fd6f4c095174d35d41f.jpg)

Jonas Bohlin, L/V-Collection pendant lamp (self-production), 1997 ![](images/cd9c9acd56a9f9fb64dfec4df0f58a9397317f8b520707d435c3d6187f0161a7.jpg) > Jonas Bohlin, Concrete chair (self-production), 1981 - re-issued by Källemo # jonas bohlin (6. 1953 Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/fcdf157536dd3accca5ac4d502a19951981ccded8892269ac9e6a81d4fd9bd3a.jpg) Jonas Bohlin worked as a civil engineer prior to studying interior design at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design), Stockholm(1976-1981). At his degree show in 1981 heshocked the Swedish design establishment with his Concrete chair – a tubular steel and concrete construction conceived more as an art piece than as usable furniture. The Concrete chair, which made reference to Bohlin's earlier life as an engineer working on bridge building projects, completely countered the tenets of Scandinavian good design and came to epitomize Swedish Post-Modernism. Bohlin subsequently worked as a freelance designer for Källemo, which produced theConcrete chair in 1982 in a limited edition. Källemo also made available his Concave chaise longue (1983), another Post-Modern design with a segmented form and highly graphic profile. Idiosyncratic designs such as thesewerewidely publicized throughout the 1980s and helped to establish Bohlinas one ofSweden's most avantgarde designers. Bohlin founded his own design practice 1983, and two years later established a Stockholm-based art and design gallery, Stockholm Mobile, exhibiting hisown work, whichplayed with form, function and emotion. In 1988 Bohlin became a professor at Beckman's School of Design, Stockholm, and was later appointed head of its FormCourse(1992–1996). The same year he was awarded the prestigious Georg Jensen Prize. During the 1980s and 1990s, Bohlin exhibited his numerous limited-edition designs in Scandinavia, Japan, America and Europe. One of his most notable projects known as “LIV"(the Swedish word for “life” and the Roman numeral for “54”), involved him and 53 friends of his rowing from Stockholm to Paris over a period of 116 days. This extraordinary exercise, marked by the publication of a book documenting the journey, inspired a design collection that included lighting,furniture, a vase and a rug. From 1991 to 1993, Bohlin was chairman of SIR(National Association of Swedish Interior Architects) and since then has remained one of the leading figures in the contemporary Swedish design community. On his approach to design Bohlin has stated: “I want my furniture to touch and please, to communicate with time and space, to be made by hand and heart, and to be considerate of mankindand nature." ![](images/bae053d5c5e8b16ba710861421d50294fd0a4aae29b0cdc8f1611d12034d5754.jpg) ![](images/8207319d9f7bb1d6644a0c6203f0da7af515919c181d37c8c7893b71d2280901.jpg) Installation at Jonas Bohlin Design, Stockholm, 2000 - showing L/V-Collection stools and pendant lamps (self-production), 1997 ![](images/2cf0f926fb1748f0f4fd7966b1f088bb14f5c7895f7ff7c5153d62d27efdfb04.jpg) ![](images/cb60e261b05c8910b454c653786f336915fbdcfe2d5bf84940afd1342d552e39.jpg) Kay Bojesen, Monkey toy, Kay Bojesen workshop, 1951 #

![](images/1d2f7c2f80ebe84101e06d257a2e9a1fcaf2bddc5bd0a3355586fc46b163a2e1.jpg) One of the most important pioneers of Danish design, Kay Bojesen initially trained as a silversmith at theGeorg Jensen workshop from 1907 to 1910. He continued his studies in 1911 at the Fachschulefür das Edelmetallgewerbe (Technical School for PreciousMetals), Schwäbisch-Gmünd, and subsequently worked for a short while as a craftsman in Paris. After returning toCopenhagen he established his own workshop there in 1913. His early work reflected the rich decorative influence of the Georg Jensen studio, but by 1931 he had adopted a modern style that emphasized simple forms, undecorated surfaces, and suitability for mechanized production. From 1930 to 1931, Bojesen designed ceramics for the Bing & Grøndahl factory. As a leading figure of Scandinavian Modernism, he was also thedriving force behind the opening of Den Permanente in Copenhagen in1931 – one of the first design centres to be established in Europe – which acted as a permanent showcase for the best products of contemporary Danish design. During the 1930s, Bojesen also designed numerous silver flatware and hollowware pieces, including his influential Grand Prix cutlery (1938), which was notable for its elegant pared down form and flowing line. Many of his silver designs were later translated into stainless steel and industrially produced by Motala Verkstad in Motala and the Universal Steel Company in Copenhagen. Bojesen was also one of the first modern designers to begin exploring the formal potential of the three-pronged fork. Apart from manufacturing his own designs, Bojesen's workshop also produced metalware by other designers, including Finn Juhl, Ole Wanscher and Magnus Stephensen. In 1938,the Danske Kunstindustrimuseum in Copenhagen staged a one-man show of Bojesen's work and he later exhibited at the 1940 (VII) Milan Triennale. During the 1940s and 1950s, Bojesen continued producing metalware items which brought him widespread recognition. He was awarded a Grand Prix at the 1951 (IX) Milan Triennale, and the following year was appointed silversmith to the King of Denmark. During this period he also receivedinternational acclaim for a number of landmark wooden designs, including his characterful monkey, rabbit, elephant, bear and puffin toys (which he had begun to make as early as 1935), and his functional yet sculptural teak salad bowls, suchas his spherical double ![](images/b5abb46cf5c8ce52f1bd1e01374ef1746d1f33bf67c68b86aacbab893adc6d73.jpg) bowl – a gold medal winner at the 1954 (X) Milan Trienale. Bojesen's workshop, now overseen by his son, continues to produce some of his best-known designs, which are still widely seen to exemplify the sensual yet practical style synonymous with Danish design. Kay Bojesen, cutlery in silver, stainless steel and ebony, Kay Bojesen's workshop, 1938 (from 1951 manufactured in stainless steel as Grand Prix cutlery) ∇ Kay Bojesen, teak double salad bowl with servers, Kay Bojesen's workshop, 1949 ∇∇ Kay Bojesen, silver serving dishes with ebony handles, Kay Bojesen's workshop, 1951 ![](images/c3feb505de28160dea23cd8eae56bbd80a301c27c9c9ffff603b13853756d511.jpg) OVERLEAF Kay Bojesen, wooden soldier, Kay Bojesen's work shop, c. 1954 Kay Bojesen, Puffin toy, Kay Bojesen's workshop, 1954 ![](images/6e4e0087a4d60be512edffb3df1cb19c6742c302b3770e331841c5130e4cac40.jpg) ![](images/440a942384dc915cffecb8a166e5cd0a9bc834ce8d85c76e58c4bca150fbf0ba.jpg) ![](images/36f1fb370575977e49f59898f6dd136efa41d2d07dacdf4938d7665be55279f6.jpg)

Vivianna Bülow-Hübe, silver and rutilated quartz necklace, 1954-1959 ![](images/794e2fb75582141800728da8824afbd2bd3e8f2c5bbce63946fc1a40ed8f9efa.jpg) Vivianna Bülow-Hübe, silver bangle watch, 1962- produced by Georg Jensen from1967 as model no. 326 # vivianna bülow-hübe . 1927 Malmö, Sweden) ![](images/549ce42ed4467ee681e4f5eafbe034983fbbb6cc3780ef5fb4415c236c216ffc.jpg) The Swedish jewellery designer Vivianna Torun Bülow-Hübe studied at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design) and the Kungliga Tekniska Högskolan (Royal Institute of Technology)in Stockholm (1945-1950). In 1951 she became the first female silversmith to establish her own studio in Sweden. Based in Stockholm, she initially concentrated on the design of wooden jewellery. The same year she received a commission for silver jewellery fromOrrefors and in 1954 was awarded a silver medal at the X Milan Triennale for her innovative designs in this medium.Believing that “A simple silver coil that highlights the line of the neck is more beautiful than all the diamonds in the world” and that “Diamonds are a girl's worst friend”, Bülow-Hübe often created plain ring-style necklaces incorporating pendants of pebbles and fossils as well as shimmering glass amulets. Bülow-Hübe moved to Paris in 1956 and two yearslater established a studio in Biot, France employing five assistants. While on the beach there collecting pebbles she met Pablo Picasso, who arranged for a solo exhibi tion of her work in Antibes in 1958. Duringthis period her designs, such asthe exquisite freeform Mobile necklace (1954–1959), became increasingly sculptural. In 1960 she was awarded a gold medal at the XII Milan Triennale and the coveted Lunning Prize. From 1967 she designed an extensive range of jewellery and watches for Georg Jensen in gold, silver and stainless steel. Typically, her designs for Georg Jensen, such as her well-known bangle-style wristwatch (1962), reflected the new sculptural confidence of the period. In 1968 she moved to Germany and established a design studio first in Wolfsburg, then in Wendhausen. While in Germany, Bülow-Hübe designed porcelain for Hutschenreuther and glassware for Löhnberg glassworks. She moved to Jakarta, Indonesia, in 1978, where she opened a studio and worked as a design consultant for a social foundation. In 1983, she was named an “Honorary Smith” by the Society of Contemporary Swedish Silver. Today, Bülow-Hübe continues to produce designs for Georg Jensen that are notable for their forward-looking, sensuous and sculptural qualities. ![](images/d4b63e41eeca795c77dabccb895d1464c08551b212ea23ce5a55d7967b917aad.jpg)

![](images/548ec39e045b54a4250decd315935e7a561321708d2ac62084c4efe70df2a3d0.jpg) Gunnar Cyrén, Nobel glassware range for Orrefors, 1991 ▶ Gunnar Cyrén, Pop goblets for Orrefors, 1965–1966 ## gunnar cyrén (6. 931 ävle, Sweden) ![](images/0547daf7b17446c89b264e0eb1bde19eced48f8f23819eb44c7d089302c2260c.jpg) Gunnar Cyrén studied metalwork at the Högre Konstindustriella Skolan (Higher College of Industrial Arts), and also at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design), Stockholm, receiving journeyman certificates in both goldsmithing and silversmithing in 1951 and 1956 respectively. He also briefly trained at the Cologne Werkschulen in 1954. After the completion of his studies, Cyrén began working in 1959 as a designer for the Orrefors glassworks. In contrast to the prevailing Scandinavian taste for rusticity in the early 1960s, Cyrén designed glassware that was distinguished by its precision, refinement and use of virtuoso craft skills. This approach to design is exemplified by his elegant overlaid glass bowl decorated with bear motifs (now in the Nationalmuseum, Stockholm). Cyrén's later iconoclastic Pop goblets (1965–1966) with their colourfully striped stems and opalescent bowls were similarly radical, yet perfectly encapsulated the joyful and youthful exuberance of the period. For Cyrén, design has always been a very private activity and his work often displays “a personal touch” that has on occasions been at odds with the constraints of industrial mass production. Designing either costly one-off pieces (using Graa/ technigues and decorative cutting) or glassware for small-scale serial production, Cyrén has concerned himself with obiects that not only function well, but also exhibit a "charming" personality. In 1966 Cyrén was awarded the Lunning Prize and during the 1970s began designing for Dansk International Designs and the Gefle Porslinsfabrik. In the early 1970s he also began designing glassware for industrial production and from the mid-1970s worked as a silversmith, at times combining both glass and silver in a single obiect. During the 1980s his glassware designs continued to display the restraint and elegance for which he had become so well known. In 1988 he received the Prince Eugen Medal and in 1991 he contributed glass tableware (with green, amber or gold coloured stems) to the specially commissioned Nobe/ service, which commemorated the 90th anniversary of the Nobel Foundation. Today, as “the innovative grand old man of glass”, Cyrén continues to design original and beautiful glassware for the Orrefors glassworks. ![](images/0b45d1e83886a6d3a91b22021212b71d3a368e8f1bd7128bbbbde384cfc8a3ee.jpg)

![](images/e96c546fac6806319bcc66222b82abb21e22da3933c331a687fafe82002548aa.jpg) Ewald Dahlskog, wine glasses for Kosta, 1927 # ewald dahlskog (Swedsh 1811950) Ewald Dahlskog studied at the Centraltryckeriet (Central Printing Office), Stockholm (1905–1908), and later at the Kungliga Tekniska Högskolan (Royal Institute of Technology). Stockholm (1908–1912), and the Kungliga Konsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts), Stockholm (1913–1917). Around 1920 he took several study trips abroad including visits to France, Italy and Tunisia, and fror 1924 to 1926 he lived in Paris as a freelance painter, illustrator and journalist, On his return to Sweden in 1926 he began producing designs for the Kosta glassworks, such as his stemmed cutglass bowl with highly stylized motifs of minotaurs and palm trees, which showed the influence of the Art Deco style. The following year he designed a range of wine glasses with inverted cone bowls and faceted stems that had a more modern aesthetic, as well as several free-blown vases and a decanter that were vernacular in style. Dahlskog also received widespread recognition for his marquetry panel designs, particularly for those he designed in 1926 for the Stockholms Konsterthus (Stockholm Concert Hall) which were adorned with stylized scenes of shepherds and soldiers. Three years later, Dahlskog began designing ceramics for Bobergs Fajansfabrik in Gefle, including his wellknown series of faïence flower vases and garden urns. The bold geometric forms of these ribbed vessels signified the increasing influence of the Modern Movement on Scandinavian design – having been exhibited at the landmark 1930 Stockholm Exhibition they were described as "functionalist” in the Swedish press. But while these moulded earthenware pots did reveal the formal influence of the Modern Movement, their chunky robustness owed more to the strong craft heritage in Scandinavia – a synthesis of the contemporary with the traditional that is so often at the heart of Scandinavian desian. Dahlskog continued designing for Bobergs Fajansfabrik until his death in 1950. ![](images/4af76628e22fa6e8b19b897c826f116a9bd5ac0a4ad2c2b758424844f92205bf.jpg)

![](images/29b6dbeab59a4b99af7e00b250b9ef221c763695c942f5049b18b152e2fce9c3.jpg) Björn Dahlström, BD-1 easy chair for CBI, 1994 # björn dahlström ( . 197 tckol, sweden) ![](images/edcb7f09fc899b4160140d1f2e1bfa84f6aeacaeabd09e0265c39a9190e96dcd.jpg) One of Sweden's leading furniture and industrial designers, Björn Dahlström beganhis career in the mid-1970s designinggraphics and animations for filmand television productions. After having been employed as an art director in an advertising agency in 1982, he founded his own graphic design company, Dahlström Design, and worked for a variety of clients including Ericsson, Scania and Atlas Copco. Eventually, Dahlström turned his attention to three-dimensional design – the RockingRabbit (1985) and streamlined Toycar (1996) for Playsam, and furniture for CBl in the early1990s, were all influenced by his earlier work in animation and graphic design. Dahlström's aesthetically pleasing product designs are often distinguished by their soft yet elemental forms and straightforward functional practicality. Believing that “the designer has to be part of the industrial process, taking the role of free-thinking catalyst”, Dahlström expanded his practice in the 1990s to encompass the design of a wide range of productsincluding a flashlight and soldering equipment for Primus, a pneumatic drill for Atlas Copco, toys and water-play systems for Aquaplay, electric handicap aids for Gewa, bicycles for Skeppshult, and a walking stick and plastic furniture for Magis. One of Dahlström's great skills lies in the way in which he is able to make even “hard” industrial design look and feel attractive. His Cobra MK1 pneumatic drill forAtlas Copco, forexample, which took four years to develop, not only set a new aesthetic precedent for such a product, but also maximized ease of handling by reducing vibrations to a minimum. Throughout the 1990s Dahlström won numerous awards for his product designs, including sixteen Excellent Swedish Design Awards and two Plus Design Awards. In 1999 he became Professor of Furniture Design at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design) in Stockholm and has since also taught at Beckman's School of Design, Stockholm. Dahlström's work, most notably his highly successful Tools cookware range (1998)for Hackman, embodies the high object-integrity that has epitomized the very best of Scandinavian design since the late 1920s. ![](images/3c7823d4ad99c3a6d5ddef3a1697adb26e5f24eda7a4d49ba09ff68fb1652862.jpg) ![](images/9180e2577d049b1176c4839a963d938e4a6f3fea05c9a5db9ed670aa08303777.jpg) 4 Björn Dahlström, BD Relax chaise-longue for CBI, 2000 ![](images/75db30170e18d2ee5510d609e8d663c74389b15b054efceb49669bac251d1ba6.jpg) Aga 2000 Specíal Edition cooking range for Aga-Rayburn, 2000 – based on the original standard model from the 1930s ## gustaf dalén ## (b. 1869 Stenstorp – d. 1937 Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/443d73ea431b2fc6cc9a533b8197f7ce20e34843d3f586a99aa3565538993616.jpg) In 1906Gustaf Dalén was appointed chief engineer at theSvenska AB Gas AccumulatorCompany, a supplier of acetylene gas. Here, Dalén undertook much research on gases and turbines, which resulted in the improvement of hot-air turbine engines and the de Laval steam turbine. Dalén became managing directorof the company in 1909, and subsequently invented Agamassan – a substance that absorbs acetylene allowing the gas to be concentrated without fear of explosion. In 1912 he won the Nobel Prize for Physics for his invention of the solventil, an automatic sun valve which uses sunlight to regulate a gas light-source so that it is turned off during daylight hours. This innovative design was later used internationally for the lighting of unmanned lighthouses and buoys. Tragically, Dalén was blinded in 1913 by an explosion that occurred during one of his experiments. While he was recuperating, he became aware of the problems his wife was encountering with her antiquated and highly temperamental cast-iron cooking range, which needed constant supervision to keepthe temperatures from fluctuating. By applying the concept of heat storage, Dalén invented a cooker that combined a small and efficient heat source, two large hotplates and two generous ovens into one robust and compact unit. Known as the Aga (taking its name from the initials of his company), Dalén's design achieved a high accumulationof heat that could be deliveredin precisely controlled quantities, with a minimum of fuel, within a wellinsulated environment. The heat generated was radiant, so that every cubic inch within each oven was utilized. The heat was also thermostatically controlled, which meant the elimination of all knobs and dials. Since the ovens and hotplates maintained different temperatures depending on their distance from the heat source, theAga could roast, bake, steam, simmer, fry, stew, grill, boil, broil and toast – all at once. Agas were first imported into the UK in 1929 and are now solely manufacturedbyAga-Rayburn in England. All theprincipal castings for Aga cookers are made at the historic Coalbrookdale Foundry in Shropshire – birthplace of the Industrial Revolution. The remarkable Aga revolutionized the design of cookers; even today it has many loyal users who still consider it the ultimateappliance. About 500,000 Agas are currently in use around the world, paying testimony to the sheer brilliance of Dalén's invention, which remains ![](images/6756645c1faa586e11a451296ce3e6e13d09d4d64409eb66295415c683219613.jpg) Gustaf Dalén, Aga Classic Special Edition cooking range for Aga-Rayburn, 2000 - based on the original standard model from the 1930s ∇ Archival photograph showing demonstration of Aga cooking range, 1930s ∇∇ Two Oven Aga cooking range for Aga-Rayburn, 1990s ![](images/26bbb0434b90bb8837f3b1f314ecd170baac0f2b40a216acda7e742db6aee946.jpg) an enduring icon of early Scandinavian industrial design. ![](images/e3f98054ff60bb1f347c7c3e5f605f8f2b8807a5b8d919c447cde29b91a383d1.jpg)

![](images/644737171dc00e2e7ebbea52d07139bc86b1bce5e86f87bc249d35c3d6aeb4e8.jpg) ▶Enamelled plate for David-Andersen, 1950s >Thorbjørn Lie-Jørgensen silver and ebony jug for David-Andersen, 1934 # david-andersen fonde 7 o oway) This renowned Norwegian goldsmith workshop bears the name of its founder, David Andersen, who established the business in Christiania(now Oslo) in 1876. By the turn of the century the David-Andersen workshop had begun to forge an international reputation for its exquisite ArtNouveau enamelled wares designed by, among others, GustavGaudernack and Thorolf Holmboe (1866-1935). The founder's son, Arthur David-Andersen (1875–1970), also designed silverware with swirling floral motifs in the Art Nouveau style for the family firm. Between 1901 and 1952, he wasthe artistic director of the workshop, and during this tenure he employed a number of leading designers including Harry Sørby, Johan Lund (1861–1939), Ludwig Wittmann (1877–1961) and JohanSirnes(1883-1966). In thelate 1920s Arthur's son, Ivar David-Andersen (b. 1903), became the thirdgeneration of thefamily to produce work for the firm. During the late 1920s and early 1930s, the workshop's output was increasingly influenced by the Art Deco style. Thorbjørn Lie-Jørgensen's(1900-1961)silver and ebony jug of 1934 reflects this stylistic shift with its bold undecorated form, although its softened geometry also anticipated thegentle organic forms that became so characteristic of Scandinavian design in the following decade. After the Second World War, Ivar David-Andersen took over as artistic director, while his father resumed his career as a designer, winning a gold medal at the 1954 (X) Milan Triennale. Since then, the workshop has continued to produce modern silverware designs alongside objects with more traditional flatware patterns, such as the Rococo-style Telemark. The company is also noted for the design and manufacture of contemporary metalware incorporating its famed and brilliantly coloured jeweller's emalje (enamels). Using a time-consuming technique that involves the building up of layers of enamel, the workshop has producedvases, dishes, bowls and spoons made of various metals – silver, aluminium, copper and stainless steel – that perpetuate the century-old Norwegian tradition of enamelling. Today, David-Andersen proudly claims to be Scandinavia's largest jewellery store, retailing not only its own designs (including delicately enamelled jewellery)but also those produced by other Scandinavian workshops. ![](images/f06676ea0408b637c5e6f973f926998672dc762e9e59e1bf1fceada8d2704a04.jpg)

![](images/5e9ce7fe135799add7d4fcb1ed76649b9a7300083d99e60046e93be274c05ba0.jpg) Nanna Ditzel, Model no. 111 silver armring, Model no. 107 silver bracelet and Model no. 333 silver brooch for Georg Jensen, 1956 # nanna ditzel (b. 1923 Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/7a58054c48734f20daf2bc8355c18f3e9fcf210f9241e023f15e16999c34878f.jpg) Nanna Ditzel (née Hauberg) studied cabinet making at theRichards School in Copenhagen alongside Grete Jalk and Kristian Vedel. She later trainedas a furniture designer under Kaare Klint at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Academy of Fine Arts)and also at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School for Applied Arts), Copenhagen, where she was taught by among others Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, Peter Hvidt, Aksel Bender Madsen(1916-2000), Peter Koch, Ejner Larsen (1917–1987), and the painter Victor Isbrand. While at the latter institution, she met her future husband, Jørgen Ditzel (1921–1961), who had earlier trained as an upholsterer. In 1944, they exhibited living room furniture together at the Cabinetmakers' Annual Exhibition in Copenhagen. These designs, which included a tea table with a removable tray designed for Louis G. Thiersen, received considerable attention. Two years later, the couple married and established their own design studio in Hellerup. Initially, they concentrated their efforts on solutions for small living spaces and Nanna explored the concept of using kitchen units as room dividers. In the early 1950s, she worked as a furniture designer in the architectural practice of Fritz Schlegel (1896–1965)while continuing to design and exhibit furniture with her husband. In 1952, they designed a range of children's plywood furniture for Knud Willadsen Møbelsnedkeri, and collaborated with Gunnar Aagaard Andersen (1919–1982)on the design of a stand for the Cabinetmakers'Exhibition. During this period, Nanna also designed silver jewellery for Georg Jensen with remarkably forward-looking organic forms. In 1954, she and her husband published a book entitled Danish Chairs and in 1956they were jointly awarded the Lunning Prize, which allowed them to travel extensively – Italy, Greece, Mexico andthe United States. From 1957, they designed a range of wicker furniture for R. Wengler that included the well-known hanging egg-shaped chair. They also designed a range of solid-wood children's furniture for Kolds Savværk, which comprisedtheir ubiquitous highchair (still used extensively throughoutScandinavia) and the simpleyet elegant Toadstools children's stool. This creative partnership was abruptly ended, however, by the untimely death of Jørgen in 1961.Nanna subsequently designed several playground environments and was asked by Søren Georg Jensen to design Nanna Ditzel, hanging eggshaped woven cane chair for R. Wengler, 1957 ∇ Period photograph showing egg-shapped woven cane chair hanging from a tree, c. 1957 ∇∇Nanna Ditzel, living room installation with Oregon Pine furniture executed by Poul Christiansen, 1962 ![](images/b933e70687cb3d4b4c35bb42af87db2578d83de2d672c996d3a9fce7fa0ffcfc.jpg) ![](images/f5b0f130db8ea87eb4bb7361e87951f79538547a3878b10972a8b1b0ca9be93b.jpg) jewellery that incorporatedsemi-precious stones, such as agates and tourmalines, for his family's silversmithy. Eventually Nanna married Kurt Heide in 1968 and spent fifteen years living in London. After his death, she returned to Copenhagen (1986) where she established a workshop, studio and home in one of the oldest parts ofthe city. Driven by her natural curiosity and “appetite for change”, Nanna Ditzel continues to execute innovative craft-based designs for furniture, metalware, tableware, jewellery and textiles. Her work, such as her butterfly-like Bench for Two Model no. 2600 (1989) for Fredericia Furniture, possesses a remarkable sense of lightness and texture as well as “motion and beauty”. ![](images/ccdd6c62efe54b4ee6e925dd6997913ac114355504ceacc8acd3b23ef55e5ce6.jpg) ![](images/1e51e6a0035d65b0c51b9d71ac87d8cbb4351f97fc6973c0774dc0338002b86f.jpg) Nanna Ditzel, child's highchair for Kold, 1955 Nanna Ditzel, Toadstools for Kold, 1962 – later produced by Krüger and today by Trip Trap ![](images/5b00a3554b9ac3bd58258e3d4519af42e59d58342f2f30906f591eec1f2f84b6.jpg) ![](images/20c519053f451a068dc8c74e2b35627ef745d97586dcdc286b9556c349b3d171.jpg) Nanna Ditzel, Trinidad chair for Fredericia Furniture, 1993 ▶Nanna Ditzel, Sommerfugle (Butterfly) chair for Fredericia Furniture, 1990 ∇ Nanna Ditzel, Bench for Two Model no. 2600 and table Model no. 2601 for Fredericia Furniture, 1989 ![](images/1ca87f77bfd4a9c9e0db521e8d4b53385366588088aa5d1880e4efd94505e9de.jpg) ![](images/c56c382a34d1775d01c04b96986ca120af34e5f0a5c3011ebe1216e28cb08f17.jpg)

![](images/85b6875fb59dbe827315cd6b59aac8b3042d0a0d5befa11b5bccd7a67a4455ef.jpg) Tias Eckhoff, Maya cutlery for Norsk Stålpress, 1961 # tias eckhof(6 126 lstr lee Norway) ![](images/e77af74a026759cadbf6ff8fcc6acaef336262e93d6fc805886e05b5403ec63b.jpg) The renowned Norwegian designer Tias Eckhoff initially trained in Denmark with Lillemor Clement and Inger Folmer-Larsen, and then later apprenticed under Nathalie Krebs. (1895–1978) at the Saxbo pottery studio in Copenhagen from 1947 to 1948. He also studied at the Statens Håndverks- og Kunstindustriskole (National College of Art and Design) in Oslo, graduating in 1949. The same year he began working as a designer for Porsarund Porselænsfabrik, a ceramics factory that was attempting to modernize its product line by re-organizing its design department and recruitina a number of young gifted designers. One of Eckhoff's first successes for Porsarund was his well-known Det Riflede (The Fluted One) tableware range designed in 1952. This white porcelain service recalled the soft forms pioneered by earlier Scandinavian designers such as Wilhelm Kåge in the 1930s and reflected Eckhoff's goal “of arriving at a beautiful, simple and functional solution”. He also designed the fireproof Glohane (1955) and the Regent (1962) tableware ranges for Porsgrund, as well as several other utilitarian services intended for institutional use. Eckhoff was Porsgrund's artistic director between 1953 and 1960, and in this position he had an enormous influence on the creative direction of the factory. In 1951 he won two prizes for his design of decorative and utility glassware in a competition held by Hadeland, which revealed his talent and scope for working within the contexts of both artistic and industrial design. Beyond his work in ceramics and glassware, Eckhoff also gained an international reputation for the excellence of his metalware designs, In 1953 his silver Cypress cutlery (1952–1953) won the Inter-Scandinavian Design Competition sponsored by Georg Jensen to celebrate the company's 50th anniversary. This elegant desian led to him winning the coveted Lunning Prize the same vear. Later, Eckhoff designed highly polished stainless steel cutlery for Dansk Knivfabrik (including the Fuga and Opus ranges) and for Norsk Stålpress (now known as Norstaal). His designs for Norsk Stålpress included Maya (1961), Una (1973), Tiki (1974), and Chaco (1990). Eckhoff also designed metalware for Halden Aluminumvarefabrik and architectural hardware, such as door-locks and mounts, for Trio-Ving, Using the same single-piece injection-moulded plastic seat-shell, he designed the Type 350 and Circle ![](images/f4e39dde57cf784a544f971f3c5f36751055fd76281c852043113cf885cf832a.jpg) armchairs (both 1983) for the Oslo-based furniture company Ana-Tomi. Eckhoff's rational and almost scientific approach to design was tempered by an acute artistic sensibility that ensured that his designs were both functional and beautiful. Over his career he won numerous awards, including five gold medals at the Milan Triennale 1954, 1957 and 1960, and three Good Design medals from the Norwegian Design Council (1962, 1965, 1966). As the most prominent Norwegian designer of the modern era, Eckhoff almost single-handedly helped focus international attention on his country's design output – thus ensuring that Norway was not completely overshadowed by Sweden, Denmark and Finland. △ Tias Eckhoff, Regent service for Porsgrund, 1961

![](images/f7bc069c0f394fe472e7661b906dfbf12651e15ac5e0e12a5a3fad201efabe6f.jpg) Kurt Ekholm, AH glazed faience tableware for Arabia, 1935 Kurt Ekholm, Sinivalko (White-Blue) tableware for Arabia, 1936 # kurt ekholm(ini s ltr e ith o0-195) ![](images/51b62a53c8939c9ab788ac3716d49b5effbc65f0e15341521817dfcb26629874.jpg) Born in Finland, Kurt Ekholm studied at theKungliga Tekniska Högskolan(Royal Institute of Technology), Stockholm. While training in Sweden he gained an insight into the moral and social philosophic basis of modern design and was greatly influenced by the progressive “funktionalmen”(functional)movement there. On his return to Helsinki in 1932 he joined the Arabia ceramics factory and that same year set up its prestigious art studio.
