Around 1934 he designed a number of stoneware vases with simple forms and mustard yellow or celadon green glazes. More importantly, Ekholm also designed the practical, attractive and inexpensive AH service (1935). This warm yellow faïence range of tableware was followed a year later by his Sinivalko (White-Blue) service, which was undoubtedly inspired byWilhelmKåge's revolutionaryPraktika service (1933) for Gustavsberg. With its softened practical forms and simple banded decoration, the stackable and inexpensive Sinivalko service was similarly utilitarian and aesthetically austere in nature. Like Praktika, the design of the Sinivalko service was primarily motivated by social concerns and in particular the idea of “good design for all”. In 1949 Ekholm moved to Sweden and worked for a year as a designer at the Rörstrand ceramics factory. He was subsequently appointed director of the Konstindustriskolan (School of Industrial Arts), Gothenburg, in 1950 and two years later obtained Swedish citizenship. Ekholm'spractical wares, which were highly suited to industrial mass production, not only exemplified the democratizing ideals of Swedish design, but also reflected the continuous transfer of ideas from one Scandinavian country to another. ![](images/37c37ecba7dddb484c5766bdfe5c46a5641ba71ff902c900ee10907d3872aea4.jpg) Axel Wenner-Gren, Model V vacuum cleaner, 1921-the first Electrolux model to be fitted with runners ![](images/fcfa60ea6f5858827edc7b6ec0c45d20a361211e73b7d9dc81a07ed9e7ed86fb.jpg) ∇Axel Wenner-Gren, General Manager of Electrolux ## electrolux (founded 1919 Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/0308d25d4e6910c7362ebf7aaddda84192c7884f5520a1c6c164cf443c89ee3f.jpg) ![](images/60f06f6698f83b5f03e301ad6f82f3403e3985dbacaa5eb8203ff488ac22ae50.jpg) The Electrolux Group is the world's largest producer of powered household appliances, with over 55 million products – including refrigerators, cookers, washing machines, vacuum cleaners and lawn mowers – being sold worldwide each year. The ancestry of Electrolux can be traced back to the AB Lux company, which was founded in 1901. In the same year the company launched a kerosene lamp designed specifically for outdoor use, which was known as the Lux lamp. Later in 1912, Axel Wenner-Gren begana collaboration with the company that resulted in his invention of one ofthe world's first household vacuum cleaners, the Lux 1. The same year this revolutionary design was launched by AB Lux, the noisy car-like vacuum cleaners that trundled along the streets plyingtheir business from one house to another were rendered virtually obsolete over night. In 1915, the company introduced the first of its revolutionary cylinder vacuum cleaners, which came with a range of tools that could be individually attached to the end of a flexible hose. Four years later, Lux merged with another Stockholm-based company, Elektromekanista, to form Elektrolux (later changed to Electrolux) and Wenner-Gren was appointed general manager of the new entity. In 1921,the easy-to-useMode/V vacuum cleaner was launched with its sleigh-like runners designed by Wenner-Gren – an innovationthat featured on Electrolux cleaners well into the 1960s. The following year, Swedish engineering students Baltzar von Platen and Carl Munters invented a cooling machine which converted heat to cold by applying the absorption process and which could be powered by electricity, kerosene or gas. This highly innovative refrigerator was subsequentlymanufactured in limited quantities by their company, AB Arctic. In 1925, Electrolux purchased the company, increased production of the refrigerator, and over the next decade grew rapidly, establishing manufacturing plants in France, Britain and America. In the late1930s, Electrolux recognized the competitive advantage offeredby high-style design and commissioned Raymond Loewy(1893-1986) to design several streamlined products, including a cylinder vacuum cleaner, afloor polisher, and the sleek L3000 refrigerator. 1940 saw the launch of Alvar Lenning's revolutionary labour-saving streamlined Assistent (Assistant) foodprocessor, which could mix, grind, grate, squeeze and chop. Later in the 1940s and early 1950s, Swedish designers SixtenSason and Ralph Lysell (1907–1987) produced other streamlined designs for Electrolux, including Sason's famous bullet-shaped Model 248 vacuum cleaner(1943). In 1963, Electrolux established its own in-house design department under the leadership of Hugo Lindström. Over the following decades, Electroluxincreased its share of the white goods and household appliance markets with various company acquisitions, including Eureka, Husgvarna, Zanussi and White Consolidated, which owned brands such as Frigidaire, Kelvinator and White Westinghouse. One of Electrolux's design directors, Michael Green, notes: “In today's marketplace, appliance manufacturers that don't make a serious commitment to design very quickly turn into commodity producers ... this company includes design in the Values and Vision statement." Like many other Scandinavian manufacturers, Electrolux has been more concerned with offering innovative value-added design solutions than with attempting to predict fashion trends. For nearly a hundred years its products have eloquently addressed the Scandinavian desire for “practical beauty” – a strategythat remains key to the company's phenomenal success. ![](images/f55305cb3d0efee5118801ea9e8f143a3bbfaa70147cf55881297bc796285754.jpg) English advertisement for theModelV vacuum cleaner, C. 1921 ∇ Oxygen vacuum cleaner, Electrolux, 1999 ∇Electrolux vacuum cleaner advertising car, 1920s ![](images/2989be4f76a17ab1843b8e84f2ed6176f939371921aaf6769f5332eeb2c2a731.jpg) ![](images/b3ee445724cddd6a28c7f3f6a55c4d37d4012a3e1ea44334eb01eb8d121e2d8f.jpg) Display of the model D10 dishwasher, the first “Round Jar” model, Electrolux, 1959 ![](images/d47706f181df25ecb99a033ec5466316b225083a536f4341197de49dfcd90774.jpg) ![](images/31b63f0c12fc0562597612ec436bfb7f02b8d1a86521d6ea8cb8d52c05c3ccf3.jpg) △Alvar Lenning, Assistent (Assistant) food processor for Electrolux, 1940 ![](images/b6e8ff54ad19cf1e8f616053719cbe9fcc2da13a87040e59172b5108a71f307b.jpg) △>Model L1 refrigerator - Electrolux's first air-cooled refrigerator, produced 1931-1937 4 Carl Otto, City Box chest freezer for Electrolux, 1956

![](images/f6d0d7b1a8817f6d4fd74ad60814c60a10136158d313623d567f96c4e534155d.jpg) ▶Ergonomi Design Gruppen, welding equipment for Hörnell International, 2000 # ergonomi design gruppen (founded 1979 Stockholm, Sweden) Widely recognized for its work in the area of design for special needs, Ergonomi Design Gruppen (EDG) evolved from two Swedish design groups that had been founded in the late 1960s. In 1979, Designgruppen and Ergonomi Design officially amalgamated to form the 14-member Ergonomi Design Gruppen, based in Bromma.The group is dedicated to the research and development of safe, reliable and efficient design solutions based on ergonomic principles for the working environment and handicapped people. It studies usersystem problems bybuilding full-scale models to evaluate designs in experimental situations, and collaborates with medical experts as well as patients to hone its solutions. Ergonomi's most notable design for (dis)ability is the Eat/Drinkprogramme of cutlery, drinking vessels and plates (1980), which was manufactured by RSFU Rehab – a private company that was initially set up as a grant-aided foundation to advise on and supply various medical aids. The Eat/Drink range was designed so that itcould be used by people who had only single-hand function. The programme's development and manufacturing costs were subsidized by grants from various insurance companies and public institutions, so that itcould be sold as inexpensively as possible. Indeed, had it not been for this private and public funding, the programme would never have got off the ground as no independent manufacturer could be found that was prepared to produce this landmark realneed design. Apart from RSFU, Ergonomi Design Gruppen has also been commissioned by other state-funded bodies such as the Institutefor the Handicappedand the Work Environment Fund to research and develop products for particular disadvantaged sections of society. The group has also designeda number of award-winning products for industrial manufacturers, including the Genotropin Pen for Pharmacia & Upjohn, an ergonomic coffee pot for SAS, the Ergo range of hand tools for Sandvik, a baby carrier for Baby-Björn, and an ergonomically-resolved stapler for Esco. Among the founders of the group, Maria Benktzon (b. 1946)andSven-Eric Juhlin (b.1940) both undertook research in 1972 into muscular ability and its relationship to the actions of gripping and holding, and have since specialized in design for disability. Ergonomi Design Gruppen is guided by the belief that, “Design is not only appearance. ![](images/da5ac4b73d47650b1e8846405d1c3d111df918fe98efb9dc652ef782ee706598.jpg) ![](images/b2ff964a92822a0a8d9eb0f79cfdcc460893d23aa6fc885c0916c15fa75d5975.jpg) It is form, function and economy. Conscientious product development can increase sales, reduce production costs, open new markets and improve quality profile. Design becomes a strategic tool for success." Ergonomi Design Gruppen is also renowned for machines, tools and protective gear designed to reduce the risk of repetitive strain injury and accidents. Central to its work is the idea of social inclusiveness – a philosophy that is at the core of Scandinavian and in particular Swedish society, where there is a progressive approach towards the provision of services and products for various disadvantaged groups of people. Like the advanced welfare state found in Sweden, the origins of this ethical approach to design can be ultimately traced to the Christian doctrine of Evangelical Lutheranism, which stresses reason and moral rectitude. It is this fundamental underlying morality – so clearly embodied in the work of Ergonomi Design Gruppen – that is such a characteristic feature of Scandinavian design in general. △△Ergonomi Design Gruppen, bow-saw for Bahco, 2000 ΔErgonomi Design Gruppen, gripping tongs for Etac, 1985

![](images/37cf4c1fcadcae1f5f0168a6eaad0d06b9fe04a663608d04803ab945b8cfa9e2.jpg) Ericsson promotional photograph, c. 1956 - showing the Ericofon designed by Hugo Blomberg, Ralph Lysell & Gösta Thames in 1954 ▶Carl-Arne Breger, Ericofon 700 telephone for Ericsson, 1976 – designed to celebrate the 100th Anniversary of the company # ericsson (founded 17 tockholm, weden) ![](images/0dcb1c2d57b16e9e02d38589b3aee56f1f2273dc2cbdba3bfdf30436c4fddc81.jpg) A world-leading manufacturer of telecommunications equipment, Ericsson's origins can be traced to the last quarter of the 19th century when Lars Magnus Ericsson (1846-1926) founded the company in Stockholm. Initially the business concentrated on the repair of telegraph equipment, butsoon its founder recognized that there was a real need for improvements to be made to existing telephone equipment. By 1878, the company was developing telephones that were basedon an earlier design by Alexander Graham Bell (1847– 1922). In the early 1890s Ericsson began manufacturing telephones of its own design, and in 1892 launched its first major product, a desk instrument with a hand-held microphone. Soon after this the company began exporting telephones throughout Europe, and its success led to the building of several overseas factories, including one in Mexico. In 1909 Ericsson launched the first cradle telephone, the Model88, which was enormously influential throughout Europe. Seeking to update this landmark design, the company commissioned the artist Jean Heiberg(1884-1976) and the Norwegian engineer Johan ChristianBjerknes in 1930 to design a telephone with a Bakelite casing to replace existing metal types. Developed using plaster prototypes, the resulting DHB 1001 telephone was less angular than earlier models, and its coherent relationship between the handset and the main instrument body established the standard for telephone design until the late 1950s. Although it was not the first to use Bakelite in its construction, the DHB1001 telephonewas highly influential and inspired Henry Dreyfuss'(1904- 1972)later plastic model 300 (1930–1933) for Bell. Between 1940and1954, HugoBlomberg (1897–1994), Ralph Lysell(1907–1987), and Gösta Thames (b. 1916) designed and developed another groundbreaking model for Ericsson, the Ericofon (launched in 1956). This unusual andforward-looking telephone had a completely unified form that integrated the earpiece, mouthpiece and dial. The design of the Ericofon, which incorporated new lightweight materials such as plastic, rubber and nylon,was made possible bythe increasing miniaturization of technology. Fun yet stylish, it was the most popular one-piece telephone for over three decades. In the early 1970s an up-datedpush-button version of the Ericofon was introduced known as the Model 700. This ![](images/cfacf8d618709831f312506882bb9653d5c6d3822a83f3aaaa68ca6d03e2a4ee.jpg) eri 1ccss74 ![](images/4f53af1ac7a22583849f2df4fb9312cfbe63ada273ca9dc9ce67640dd2f0ba71.jpg) △ Ericsson Model no. 88 telephone, 1909 △ Jean Heiberg, DBH 1001 telephone for Ericsson, put into production in 1932 Ericsson promotional photograph showing the Ericofon telephone in use, C. 1956 Hugo Blomberg, Ralph Lysell & Gösta Thames, Ericofon telephone, 1954 - launched in 1956 ![](images/603c2efcec1b415dd781fbe226c59b6238405eaa5e706c809e486ef4f425239f.jpg) ![](images/14f7ecc854e97123729e5f6e4325c3ed89b96fd0d27e41e43b996572b3444bb3.jpg) ![](images/5bc2a627d7f94326f8c1b373d4fdf5d4c1a9d8f5b58e48d043927b4fe4fd3899.jpg) Ericsson Model no. R380e mobile phone, 2002 modish design with its sleek yet angular form displayed the bold and graphic outline that is so characteristic of much Scandinavian industrial design. During the 1980s and 1990s Ericsson became one of the leading manufacturers of mobile telephones, producing well-designed yet at times rather conservative products. Throughout this period the company was the world leaderin mobile telephone systems, connecting nearly 40 percent of the world's mobile callers and was at the forefront of the WAP (Wireless Application Protocol) revolution – the bridge between mobile communication and the Internet. As one of the leading players in the telecommunications industry, however, the company was severely hit by the stock market turmoil in the electronics sector during the early2000s. By2002 Ericsson's stock price had plunged to such an extent that today the very survival of thisSwedishhousehold name remains unclear. ![](images/0b87c8c5e0ba8c120d994720db2e6826b075cacf087d643c1eaac50b1a9d7f54.jpg) ![](images/75139229218a6eba48f6adf9a1e493c54f778baead8241bcf690107142d27a15.jpg) 4 Ericsson Mobile Com-1 communicator (concept design), 2002 ![](images/6daabb0706cd034e029abed457b631b2140fe8441651f3ab791b80a6bb980764.jpg) ![](images/8a1356e92e27da68c8db200aba530cb5d7f0f125613a12eafb868da01bf878b3.jpg) Thomas Eriksson, Progetto 9208 medicine cabinets for Cappellini, 1992 # thmas eriksn19 kdsik eden) ![](images/01af873c52bb4ef8f8d84d86545e557f0ce0e4c9de471c950ea5e0633f1689eb.jpg) The Swedish architect and furniture designer Thomas Eriksson studiedarchitecture in Stockholm from 1981 to 1985. He began working as a freelance architect in 1988 and between 1992 and 1993exhibited a number of furniture designs as part of Cappellini's Progetto Oggetto collection in Milan, New York and Cologne. These pieces were notable for their often symbolic, simple elemental forms, such as his E-shaped Progetto 9304 occasional table, his ladder-like Progetto9209 wall-mountedcoat-rack, and hiscross-shaped enamelled steel Progetto9208 medicine cabinet. Eriksson's later furniture for IKEA, most notably the PS television table (1995)and PSclock/cupboard (1995), had similarly pared-down forms. Apart from his furniture designs, Eriksson has also designed various domestic products and architectural fittings. In 1995, he was awarded the Swedish Design Award and three years later was commissioned bySAs to design its Euroclass Lounges in Milan and London. He has also designedSAS airport lounges inStockholm, Copenhagen and Newark (New York) in a contemporary yet understatedNordic style utilizing blond woods and simple, unadorned, functional forms. Eriksson'sinterior design clientele also includes Absolut, H&M and Virgin, while Asplundand CBI have also manufactured some of his furniture and product designs. In 1998, Eriksson founded the design company Stockholm Design Lab with Björn Kusoffsky (b. 1945) and Göran Lagerström (b. 1965) with the concept and goal of it becoming an “important platform for the creation of quality design that matters and makes a difference." The philosophy of Stockholm Design Lab is“rooted in Scandinavian tradition and based on the fundamental ideas of simplicity, clarity, openness and innovation”. Throughout the early2000s Eriksson has continued to pioneer a fresh interpretationof Swedish Modernism. His pared-down work with its clean Nordic lines epitomizes the new and dynamic spirit that has recently emerged in Scandinavian design. ∇ Thomas Eriksson, Progetto 9209 coat rack for Cappellini, 1992 ∇ Thomas Eriksson, PS television table for IKEA, 1995 ![](images/8219c58c8bdc691d7898c0ff14034c977f94d4ecc3363893c9e5fa3e6dbe4243.jpg) ![](images/0a61a995ade16b89340f6782cafda48dd387db8a5f6e9c9b6804318e46c9dbdd.jpg) ![](images/8243f6cc75b5e73adc564f1ce09ff22ae6599743452f7fd164ce810364b80860.jpg)

![](images/4262ed8ff062589d008356adb27549db76e51852f4368ac4ab26b9d67fe9f138.jpg) Vuokko Eskolin-Nurmesniemi, Jokapoika (Every Boy) shirts for Marimekko, 1957 # vuokko eskolin-nurmesniemi ![](images/5574d1097091fed8e25131177b6901fa55feed82b735d0099cd3ec274f73e2d0.jpg) (b. 1930 Helsinki, Finland) ![](images/0c216e09442bf0179826a2e90046b42105179f4c6570c3020bb572e518f87c7c.jpg) Vuokko Eskolin-Nurmesniemi revolutionized Finnish design during the post-war years with her boldly patterned and coloured textiles. She began her training at just 16years of age in the ceramics department of the Taideteollinen Oppilaitos (Institute of Industrial Arts) in Helsinki. After completing her studies in 1952, she worked briefly as a designer forthe Wärtsilä-Arabia ceramics factory, and in 1953married the Finnish furniture and interior designer Antti Nurmesniemi. The same year, the founders of Marimekko, Viljo and Armi Ratia (1912-1979), “hand-picked”her to become the company's new artistic director. During the immediate post-war years there were shortages ofall kinds in Finland and the lack of new clothes meant that women's dresses, which tended to be made of dark and drab coloured textiles, were endlessly adapted in the cause of “make do and mend”. Around this time, however, theRatias other firm Printex had begun producing colourful and vibrant furnishing textiles designed by Maija Isola that were printed on inexpensive cotton sheeting. The concept for Marimekko was to use similar screen-printed textiles for clothing, and Vuokko Eskolin-Nurmesniemi was entrustedwith creating the first line of youthful, fun and off-thepeg garments, which were the very antithesis of the expensive and rather staid collections emanating from the well-known Paris fashion houses. Eskolin-Nurmesniemi's textiles, such as Tibet and Ram, were patterned with large geometric motifs, and often used two overlapping colours so as to create a third colour – a technique that became a key feature of Marimekko fabrics. The design of Eskolin-Nurmesniemi's clothes was as radical as the patterning ofher textiles. In her search for greater universality of appeal she eliminated traditional features such as tucks, darts and button-holes and replaced them with zippers and touch-and-close fasteners such as Velcro and press-studs (up until then only ever used on men's clothing) so as to make her garments more user-friendly and less physically restrictive. The first Marimekko show was held in Stockholm in 1957and Eskolin-Nurmesniemi'sground-breaking designs were widely acclaimed by critics. The bright colours she used were especially appreciated in the United States and her clothes soon became highly fashionable there after Jacqueline Kennedy purchased a number of them. One of ![](images/361f1512a8e145acc6f998716b6085dc9f7b050632a9bd9545ac32636df4aa68.jpg) ![](images/3d9364a694af0baa242b82256f54d514e6eac5c3499606e08d811e087a332ec6.jpg) Eskolin-Nurmesniemi's best known designs, the Jokapoika shirt(1957) with its small yet colourful piccolo stripes is still in production after 45 years – a remarkable feat given the fickle nature of the world of fashion. Her clothes and textiles were favoured in particular by designers, architects and other creatives who preferred her unconventional look, which reliedon structural essentialism rather than on gimmickry or showiness. In 1964, Eskolin-Nurmesniemi founded her own Helsinki-based company, Vuokko, and continued to create loose-fitting clothing that predicted the future work of designers such as Issey Miyake (b. 1938), who regards her as his “design mother". She has received numerous awards including the Lunning Prize (1964), the Finnish State Design Prize (1969), the Prince Eugen Medal (1986), andthe Kaj Franck Prize(1997). In 1977 her printed cottons and jacquards were exhibited at NewYork's Metropolitan Museum of Art, and in 1988 she was made an Honorary Royal Designer in London. Throughout her career VuokkoEskolin-Nurmesniemi has created trends rather than followed them. As one of the most successful Finnish designers of all time, her clothes and textiles helped define the unmistakable character of Finnish design. 4△Vuokko Eskolin-Nurmesniemi, Hood dress for Vuokko, 1966 △Vuokko Eskolin-Nurmesniemi, Pyörre (Whirl) dress for Vuokko, 1965 ![](images/76731f7eefb60d491dbadbfd4c481e45c70ec97e32f909464bbb05c1e8e79305.jpg) ## fiskarS (found 4 i ar, Finland) ## FISKARS In the early 17th century Peter Thorwöste moved from Stockholm to Turku and became an agent for a prominent merchant. Having accumulated much wealth as a result of his dealings in Turku, Thorwöste subsequently loaned some money to the owner of the local Antskog ironworks for its expansion. The loan could not be repaid and so by default Thorwöste obtained full proprietorship of the works in 1647. He subsequently gained permission to extract iron ore from the Ojamo mine and in 1649 was officially allowed to manufacture cast iron and forged items (except cannon). The same year he was also granted permission to establish a blast furnace and a bar hammer at Fiskars. This new ironworks was highly productive, being able to produce over 1,000 kg of iron a day: The Fiskars ironworks manufactured all kinds of products such as nails, wire, knives, tools, and reinforced wheels, which were transported to Stockholm and the provinces in the south of Finland. During the mid-18th century, the ironworks began producing small amounts of copper that was rendered into utensils by Fiskars' smiths. In 1822 the ironworks was acquired by John Jacob Julin, who during the 1830s established at Fiskars the first fine forging workshop and the first machine workshop in Finland. By now the ironworks was producing various household utensils, including cooking pots, frying pans, high-quality tableware, kitchen knives, scissors, and the famous puukko hunting knives. By the turn of the century Fiskars was manufacturing some forty different models of horsedrawn plough (in total over one million ploughs were produced at the ironworks between 1860 and 1900). During the First World War the ironworks was modernized and its product range expanded, but its further growth was impeded by the global depression of the 1930s. The post-war period saw Fiskars focus its attention on the developing American market. To this end the company produced a stylish, export-led range of flatware, known as Triennale (1957), which was designed by Bertel Gardberg and reflected the growing internationalism of Finnish design during this period, In 1967 the company launched its famous orangehandled O-series scissors (1963), which had been developed by the engineer and wood-carver Olof Bäckström (b. 1922). The prototype for the ergonomically conceived ABS handles of these scissors was carved by him from solid wood.

![](images/4b680e23ba5c3bcae342c5980640e46d33d7eaa6dcb9ef7a08c6598dd6351830.jpg) ![](images/a17c20b873bd9c722fe63f2592e6959fa211d0560bac83de5fa70daba0ff9fc3.jpg) ![](images/3b38493f36bca8ca16550a86818b33c37087d89c6f36bc6b85d89c73210a1b6e.jpg) Apparat zum Futterkochen ![](images/4519c46746852db62e26d96a1d0109762059335dec7a54dff5e3a2126ae7179c.jpg) Bäckschnaschine. Pol ia eite Haenke ![](images/474a98977aa44959b8c16c0508b8f95cb4f44ebe66bb768b77dc55bb88c79e8b.jpg) Kartoffelwaschmaschine. ![](images/525d67ba95dd8d47a384258b926b4a444bbe8499307b2a2158a6e086fc13ab15.jpg) Das Depôt Köserner Plug für 2 Plerde # Fiskars'schen mechanischen Fabrik in Finnland. Drill-Plag Sandee- wad MIarwtalistcsismen-Ehe No. 1 in Rign. ![](images/88a844cff595f546b81e273b5898ce6996f7edf560f8076726a6cae7764d3eb8.jpg) landwirthschaftliche Maschinen, Daumpfuasehinen. Loeomobilen ete. and lat sae Verlanf and en Aaatke turitie: ![](images/e0cecc291fc45586862be15539487c1bd41a1ab53f713f6e4b99d299f29b9679.jpg) ![](images/405e4b130386408f990ef62b788a0fa606fbd39b0bfa0d519b834fa383436c2f.jpg) Licensed worldwide, the scissors were also manufactured as pinking shears and in a left-handed version. Over the succeeding years this benchmark ergonomic design, now known as the Classic, has been developed into a family of eighteen scissors. More recently the Fiskars design team under the guidance of Olavi Lindén (b. 1946) has developed a range of garden tools that has received numerous design prizes. As the oldest existing industrial company in Finland, Fiskars has become one of Finland's most popular visitor attractions, and holds various design-related exhibitions during the summer months. Fiskars' products, which are celebrated around the world, reflect the company's “commitment to quality and systematic application of ergonomic principles, which result in products that are easy, safe and pleasurable to use". ![](images/1c1936aab22c9ad0db585f8331f0c19943235b7cfcdb70b8b2a0f1cfde9b34d4.jpg) Δ Fiskars catalogue, late 1800s Photograph showing the hand-finishing of blades at Fiskars cutlery mill, c. 1950 △Fiskars catalogue showing Bistro cutlery and Triennale cutlery – both designed by Bertel Gardberg in 1957, C. 1960s ![](images/60c541d86fbb47e6c7534d9532ca27d65a7d7575f73a6b531be2b8cbc6d182a1.jpg) ![](images/92145f905d03f999627ca50c05a7338014519ac78ca99abe08e8172f10cc9d17.jpg) Price list showing various Fiskars scissors, 1967 ▶> Advertisement forO-series scissors, c. 1967 ![](images/6e204f539dfdb3a8fa6a6df6c21d91cebc9daf4fd6b92495895db721f9064130.jpg) ![](images/83a662e84c121dbab519bb990163ccc4e8417c5a6f6ba7c230e0c81cf9f2572c.jpg) Handvänlig formgivning Fiskars sax har utformats sà att den ligger ypperligt i handen. Ni klipper lättare och behändigare än med nâgon annan sax. Specialsax för vänsterhänta Fiskara har tänkt på Er som är vänsterhánt. Vi gör en egen sax enkom tör Er, lätt att känna igen pá den röda färgen. Rostfritt stál Fiskars saxar titiverkas av prima kromstál med hög kolhait. Häller sig skarpa tänge. ![](images/16bd2f921a29e002de2021c6f6dc109e5db6d7e9691258157ed893cf712aa68c.jpg)

![](images/33356630eb53c1a3e37f4f7f8ce69cc49ff67a89cb52a5c1995c6829b50555ff.jpg) Kay Fisker, silver tea service for A. Michelsen, 1929 Kay Fisker, silver cocktail shakers for A. Michelsen, 1928-1929 & 1927 # kay fisker(Danish 193-1965) ![](images/fffa38bd12b7256e2aa9ecd00990f5a64fb9707feaa9643babc00a63d88b21b5.jpg) Kay Fisker worked for the Danish architect and designer Anton Rosen from1912 to 1916 before studying architecture at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) inCopenhagen. Aftergraduating in 1920, Fisker established his own architecture practice and in 1921 was awarded a gold medal in Ghent for his work. Between 1919and1927 Fisker was also the editor of the journal Arkitekten, and from around1925 he began designing silverware for A. Michelsen in Copenhagen. As a memberof the younger generation of avant-garde designers in Denmark, Fisker set out to modernize Danish design by developing forms that were strongly geometric, such as those of his cocktail shakers from the late 1920s. Form and decoration were often utterly inseparable in his designs, as his bonbonnière for A. Michelsen(1926)or his beech wingback chair from the 1930s demonstrate. Fisker also designed book covers and exhibited his work at the seminal1925“Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes” in Paris. In 1928, he was awarded the prestigious Eckersberg Medal and later in 1947 he received the C.F. Hansen Medal. He became a professor at the Kunstakademiets Arkitektskole (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts, school of architecture), Copenhagen, in 1936 andheld this position until 1963. His design work was the subject of several one-man shows held in Charlottenborg (1934 and 1953), Paris (1949), London (1950), and Århus (1953). Like many other Danish designers, Fisker's skill lay in his ability to update traditional forms by striking a delicate balance between historicism and outright Modernism. As one of the most influential architect-designers working in Denmark during the 1920s and 1930s, Fisker mastered a wide range of materials so as to produce not only luxury goods, such as his exquisite silver cocktail shakers, but also more mundane yet useful products, most notably his cast-iron stove that has an understated beauty derivedsolely from functional purity. ![](images/64c31c88012d004b50f5c3d926f502ce0c16c815fca7d1cd0852e8b2d87cd5b7.jpg) Kay Fisker, interior of the Danish pavillion at the 1925 “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes" in Paris ∇4Kay Fisker, cast-iron stove for Støbejern, c. 1920 ∇ KayFisker, wingback chair (unknown maker), 1930s ▶ Kay Fisker, wine pitcher for A. Michelsen, 1926 ![](images/0d660ff89fdbd86cbe28db2a98079f5368ae219bfba21377680fa3c6189d42c4.jpg) ![](images/29f2bf38b016bed2f4c888258504d3a87d993de12d6c2ada06436239e674f049.jpg) ![](images/41947ce26ad7d5caa6e13b4107859b8dc1d9744290d940718476a01b7c652c31.jpg)

![](images/377655dbe7cf38b1726f6537091c95b53cd93a7cbb2e26c0223c392c387f18eb.jpg) > Kaj Franck, Kartio vases for littala, 1958 # kaj franck (b. 1911 Viipuri, Finland – d. 1989 Santorini, Greece) ![](images/3d0915035d3a6793c66782f91ac10fe8b5d03c7a10a16f58d66e6512df22884b.jpg) One of Finland's most celebrated designers of glass and ceramics, Kaj Franck initially studied furniture design under Arttu Brummer (1891– 1951) at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (Institute of Industrial Arts), Helsinki, graduating in 1932. He subsequently worked as a catalogue illustrator for TaitoOy (1933) and the Riihimäki glassworks (1934). Between 1934 and 1938 hewas employed as a decorator and interior designer by Te-Ma Oy Tema and later designed printed textiles for the United Wool Factory in Hyvinkää in 1938. The following year, Franck was conscripted into the military where he met many fellow countrymen from less privileged backgrounds – an experience that profoundly impacted on his views of society. In 1945, he began working for the Arabia ceramics factory and a year later, designed a range of utilitarian tableware for the Väestöliition (Family Welfare Association). During the 1950s, he designed several ranges of everyday modern crockery and kitchenware that were intended for multipurpose use and that used geometric rather than fashionable streamlined forms. In an article published in 1949, Franck wrote: “New conditions of life and a new lifestyle have forced us to modernize home fittings ... (however) ... You can see hardlyany change in everyday porcelain ware ... There is no need for compromising on the need for appropriate utility items. Durable, solid, waterproof, easy to wash ... our times will build its design idiom on the conditions of life and forms which belong to us." Franck realizedthese ideals inhisglazed-faïence Kiltarange (1952), which was devised not as a traditional-style dinner service but rather as individual pieces with a shared aesthetic. In 1955, Franck received an Asla grant to study design teaching in America, and during his time in the USA he researched eating habits, which had a direct bearing on his subsequent design work. From1954, Franck also designed limited-production wares, such as his Lumipallo (Snowball)series, until he left Arabia in 1973. He also designed pressedglassware for littala between 1946 and 1950 that included the practical Kartio range of water jugs and drinking glasses. In 1950, frustrated by the lack of experimentation at littala, Franck left the company and a year later was appointed design director of the Nuutajärvi-Notsjö glassworks, which had recently been acquired by theWärtsilä group. The Nuutajärvi ![](images/d43e25e4dc484c0053115f4d383c440b636867ce02e3e2aed7d47cf761fa06cc.jpg) △ Kaj Franck, Lilli glasses for littala, 1956 factory had already established a reputation for the excellence of its studio glass, and while working there Franck was able to explore the creative potential of glass by experimenting with different techniques and pigments. In 1952, he recruited two Italian glassblowers who were able to bring with them the expertise of the Murano glassworks, and following this some of Franck's designs bore the unmistakable influence of Venetian glass. Franck's blown art glass for Nuutajärvi varied tremendously in style, from delicately engraved decanters torustic-style glasses with encased bubbles and boldly coloured bands, to “spun”glass withswirling stripes. One of Franck's last projects was the redesign of the Kilta range for Arabia. The resulting Teema range (1977–1980)ofnineteen pieces based on elemental geometric forms (the circle, square and cone) reflected his belief that, “the only possible way to solve the problems of utility ware was to be radical and social”. Franck's career is indicative of the almost unique working practice of Scandinavian designers, who often design functional democratic productsintended for mass production in parallel with exclusive one-off or limited edition art-based pieces. ![](images/bb49e63ebe13fc4af63971d331e4a1ec173aede1400f9004697c60e5633c31d1.jpg) ![](images/b4064237e0e5986b245f8c8e82cd697e4465cfc01ac247281f6ccfe25ada6253.jpg) ![](images/23abe381a271fad56f713c5a0a3ca9cf4984b96d7c9e72f82bfe55fd4e640478.jpg) ![](images/59d1a543f6f777a99e3140829578aae7f54ce577f6f53defd6aa5fa5351b7b92.jpg) ![](images/d25c0e8a671615535ac4e3f249320a977d53c934f28bcbd56f65c3da027be9bd.jpg) 4Kaj Franck, Teema tableware range for Arabia, 1977–1980 - based on Franck's earlier Kilta range designed in 1948 & 1951-1952 ∇ Kaj Franck, Teema tableware for Arabia, 1977–1980 ![](images/209d96be12f48a4da36666330bf9e8052bba9b3f54f09a176781876e167a7cea.jpg) ![](images/cda4f3a054ba2258cf464362fe9a67912005f00ac2db57c88b91ac74dbe74d5a.jpg) Josef Frank, Hawaii textile for Svenskt Tenn, 1943-1944 # jos k(b. 1885 ate, stria . 67 totoi, Sweden) ![](images/63edadb95a0bd6e05cdbf5a71b994aa1d350ffa5c00cc3204a00f2ddc9a4b5a8.jpg) Austrian-born designer Josef Franktrained as an architect at the Technische Hochschule (Polytechnic) in Vienna (1903–1910). From 1919 to 1927 he was a professor at the Kunstgewerbeschule (School of Arts and Crafts) there. Between 1925 and 1934, he also ran a home furnishings business Haus& Garten with Oskar Wlach (1881-1963). As one of the most prominent architects in Austria, Frank designed for the Wiener Werkstätte and participatedin theDeutscher Werkbund's landmark 1927“Die Wohnung” exhibition in Stuttgart. His specially designed flat-roofed reinforced-concrete double house was extremely modern, but his furniture from the same period was Neo-Classical in tone and completely at odds with the Modernist functionalist aesthetic. In 1933 Frank began designing for the Swedish interior design company Svenskt Tenn. Two years later, he moved to Stockholm, became the firm's chief designer and adopted Swedish nationality in 1938. He designed numerous textiles and furniture for the company inspired by the 19th century British Arts & Crafts Movement and by turn-of-the-century Viennese design. Although he rejected functionalist ideology and viewed personal taste and comfort as the basis of domestic design, Frank's work was essentially modern, being distinguished by a purity of form. Hisfurniture was remarkable for its harmonious proportions and high quality craftsmanship, aswell as its practicality and comfort. From 1942 to1944, he taught at theNew School for Social Research in New York, and as a theorist wrote several books, including Architektur als Symbol (1930)and Accidentism (1958). He returnedto Stockholm in 1946and remained there until his death in 1967. His work enjoyed a revival in the 1980s and a number of his designs have remained in production with Svenskt Tenn. Like Carl Malmsten and Bruno Mathsson, Josef Frank is regardedas one of the three classicists of Swedish design. As an early pioneer of “the Scandinavian style”, his contribution was immense, while his belief that, “the home does not have to be planned out in detail, just put together with pieces itsinhabitantslove”epitomized theScandinavian distrust of dogmatic universalism such as that promoted at the Bauhaus. ![](images/59d779c84170fb3f68426647eeb34d419567d9867f486da8f62ecd17df40b62d.jpg) 4 Josef Frank, Model no. B2483 table lamp for Svenskt Tenn, 1936 4Josef Frank, Model no. 881 cabinet-on-stand for Svenskt Tenn, c. 1938 ∇ Josef Frank, interior with mahogany chest-of-drawers and Poisons textile – both for Svenskt Tenn, c. 1943 ![](images/559986fb0251961aef195c73fe074355e3d2b8e859f99583374df23947c35c9a.jpg) ![](images/8e5a2fe6aa7f73208da52d32e304fa264d64784d8497b075cb48c42bd5d550d7.jpg) ![](images/cd6fdc484069cadfc65b45f336a3b1cd81c9a1c1667a5838f94f910803341a64.jpg) ![](images/c890292838b0b064c8bfab735d502fbbe32d9b022dc27fb4eea61f33bb116fc4.jpg) ![](images/e6103aff873f8e93b04733e8188fe557296c5c3d203b423708e8e31ed0e07a5b.jpg) Josef Frank, Dixieland textile for Svenskt Tenn, 1943-1944 4 Josef Frank, Tehran textile for Svenskt Tenn, c. 1943-1945 ∇Josef Frank, Celotocaulis textile for Svenskt Tenn, C. 1930 ![](images/99675785bb78772aa304217604e51117553d27c9cc6cc2ec6705430de1ce6968.jpg)

Berndt Friberg, stoneware vases and bowl forGustavsberg with "hare's fur" glaze, 1964, c. 1961, 1959, 1978& 1963 ![](images/3237e57907a8b4cb210d220c32ffa6e97d91bbd5ac7627e94567cb3f093549c4.jpg) > Berndt Friberg, stoneware vase for Gustavsberg, 1954 # berndt fribergSwedish 1999-1981) The Swedish ceramicist Berndt Friberg apprenticed as a thrower at the Höganäs-Billesholms stoneware factory from 1915 to 1918. He also studied at the technical school in Höganäs and from 1918 trained at various ceramics workshops in Denmark and Sweden. In 1934 he joined the Gustavsberg ceramics factory, where for the next decade heworked as a thrower for Wilhelm Kåge. In 1944 he became a member of the Gustavsberg Studio and subsequently designed his own ceramics for the company until his death in 1981. During this period he produced numerous one-off stoneware vessels that were distinguished by their delicately coloured – soft greens, pale ochres, dusky blues – Oriental-style semimatt “hare's fur” glazes. The forms were influenced by those of earlier Far Eastern wares. Although inspired by Oriental antecedents, Friberg's work was typically Scandinavian in its refinement – colour, texture and form were carefully balanced in order to realize objects that had a compelling natural beauty. His hand-thrown ceramic designs produced at the Gustavsberg Studio were awarded three gold medals at the 1947(VIII), 1951 (IX) and 1954(X) Milan Triennale and the Gregor Paulsson Trophy in 1960. His work was also shown on numerous occasions at Nordiska Kompaniet as well as other exhibitions held in Stockholm, Gothenburg, Karlstad, Höganäs, Malmö, and Växjö. Like his mentor Wilhelm Kåge, Friberg produced stoneware pottery that exemplified the superlative quality of Scandinavian craftsmanship. With his sensitive handling of materials and profound understanding of their formal and aesthetic potential, Friberg set an important benchmark in the design and composition of studio ceramics. ![](images/ce9ee96990d606243c1233aeea7ad0c91a0077ee39def19e8c618778cca44fcc.jpg)

![](images/a92f337b72b08fd08c2e5040864a84c7bdd00933326f9a39cd26eacd4351c0c1.jpg) Arne Jacobsen, Modei no. 4130 Grand Prix chair for Fritz Hansen, 1955 # fritz hansen (founded 1872 Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/cc7ca46c0aaf117f6547a20c1fe97de111650b19a7e38933dd20d8b98668e0db.jpg) ![](images/937b476f7c44e42877ea85fb79bd54ca07520e3622071a188f7834c4d34ced38.jpg) In 1872, Fritz Hansen established a cabinet-making workshop in Copenhagen that specialized in woodturning and the manufacture of wooden and iron frames for upholstered furniture. In 1915, Fritz Hansen's son, Christian, introduced various industrial processes to enable the mass production of furniture. These new manufacturing techniques, which included wood bending processes that were similar to those developed earlier by Michael Thonet in Austria, allowed significant expansion of the company during the 1920s. In 1932, Christian's sons, Fritz and Søren, joined the firm and began modernizing its product line with designs such as Søren Hansen's DAN chair (1930) – a subtly updated Thonet-style cafe chair. One year later the company became involved in a legal dispute with Lorenz-Thonet over a chromed tubularmetal cantilever chair designed by the architect Mogens Lassen (1907–1987). This was one of many legal wrangles within the furniture industry over the ownership of the intellectual rights to the cantilever format. During the 1930s, Fritz Hansen also produced a large number of other Moderniststyle designs in bentwood and tubular metal by architects such as Heilmann Sevaldsen, Fritz Schlegel (1896-1965)and Kaare Klint. In the late 1940s, the firm began producing furniture designed byHans Wegner, for example his Chinese Chair No. 1 (1944), which was inspired by an antique Chinese chair illustrated in Ole Wanscher's book, Types of Furniture (1932). Like other designs by Wegner, this chair was not a reproduction but an evolution of an existing type – a high-quality modern reworking thatenhanced the chair's rationale for volume production. Other notable furniture that FritzHansen manufactured in the late 1940s included a beech armchair by Ove Boldt (a reinterpretation of the traditional Windsor chair), an upholstered beech chair designed by Søren Hansen (which anticipated the sculptural forms ofArne Jacobsen), and a beech sofa with collapsible side elements by Børge Mogensen (a modern reworking of an antique type). Fritz Hansen also developed and manufactured the landmark moulded-plywood AX chair (1950) designed by Peter Hvidt and Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, an accomplishment that signified an important shift towards a more Modern idiom for the company. In 1951/52 Arne Jacobsen designed the stackable Ant chair for Fritz Hansen, which with its continu- ![](images/edf1197d18397adbae562fd2b5a98e4b190d69428408e5ef1b879d70e2481273.jpg) ![](images/9aefbba29634d59882837acc64816de5dbcc208ca9a04016eaf04197a0d6d305.jpg) ous moulded-plywood seat section and tubular metal leg construction was a masterpiece of simplification. With its pinched waist and curvaceous form, the Ant chair went on to become iconic of “The New Look" in the 1950s. This revolutionary design was followed in 1955 by the even more successful Series 7 range of chairs, which had a similar moulded-plywood, tubular metal construction that was eminently suited to high-volume industrial production. Later in the 1950s, Fritz Hansen began production of Arne Jacobsen's seminal Swan and Egg chairs (1958). These hugely popular designs not only predicted the space-age forms of the later 1960s, they were also among the first seat furniture to fully exploit the potential of latex foam as an upholstering material. Furniture designers who employed this newmaterial benefited enormously from the freedom it granted them – latex foam-upholstered chairs did not require any springing so their forms could become much more sculptural while at the same time more visually unified. A decade after Fritz Hansen launched these chairs it commissioned Piet Hein and Bruno Mathsson to develop a group of tables that complemented Jacobsen's earlier Ant and Series 7 chairs. The form used for this range of tables (1968) was based on Hein's mathematical concept of the “superellipse” – a shape in-between a rectangle and an oval. In 1974, Fritz Hansen also began production of Verner Panton's extraordinary 1.2.3. series of chairs, which comprised twenty different models. Having remained a family concern for four generations, the firm was eventually acquired by the Skandinavisk Holding Company and its offices were moved from Copenhagen to Allerød. Today, Fritz Hansen continues to produce its classic designs by Arne Jacobsen and Poul Kjærholm alongside its more contemporary collections by, among others, Alfred Homann and Pelikan Design. Throughout its long and industrious history, Fritz Hansen has been one of the most prominent furniture manufacturing companies in Scandinavia, and as such it has contributed greatly to the promotion and acceptance of the simple yet elegant Scandinavian style throughout the world. ΔArne Jacobsen, variants of the Ant and Series 7 seating ranges for Fritz Hansen, 1952-1968 ΔMogens Lassen, wicker and tubular steel chair for Fritz Hansen, 1933 Elmar Moltke Nielsen & Knud Friis, FM chair for Fritz Hansen, 1985 ∇ Arne Jacobsen, Model no. 3107 chairs for Fritz Hansen, 1955 – later made available in a wide range of colour finishes Poul Kjærholm, PK9 chair for Fritz Hansen, 1960 ![](images/178378fbf3e61305c94d4fa21f590341e7ede2660977f7f02f1342437d338e8a.jpg) ![](images/8bd9bae1a9d6277ed8d5ad3242cc4aa12b486baac7f2fc540c39596e112e9ffd.jpg) ![](images/ff35f4af4ecfe3a2df6191fddab037206baaf201c04128d07a3849785114b405.jpg)

▶ Niels Gammelgaard, Moment sofa for IKEA, 1985 ![](images/7faef6f06583dfeb20f86f2bbf5d327c9beb0c776722ac368e787fb48e842d38.jpg) Niels Gammelgaard, Nevil chair for IKEA, 1994 # niels gammelgaard (b. 1944 Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/5f4cba37b26508e8141a7de8f4429e4dacc4b295d78e643c4609074c9a67f795.jpg) Niels Gammelgaard studied industrial design under Erik Herløw at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) in Copenhagen, graduating in 1970. In 1969, he cofounded Box 25 Architects, which undertook the design of Sofiegården College, Christianshavn in 1973, and the Kvarterlygten (Streetlight) for Louis Poulsen in 1976. After meeting Ingvar Kamprad, the founder of IKEA, Gammelgaard began designing products for the company in 1975. Since then, his furniture designs for IKEA, such as the Ted folding chair (1977) and Moment sofa (1985), have not only won numerous prizes, including eight Ütmarkt Awards and two Red Dot Awards, they have also been among the company's best-selling products. In 1978, Gammelgaard co-founded with Lars Mathiesen (b. 1950) Pelikan Design a Copenhagen-based industrial design practice, which over the past 24 years has designed and seen into production literally hundreds of products ranging from small pencils to children's tricycles to hospital beds. Pelikan Design is best known, however, for the design of contract furniture – offices, waiting halls, rest areas – and can name many of the most prestigious Danish furniture manufacturers among its clients, including Fritz Hansen, Bent Krogh, Fredericia, and Erik Jørgensen. Gammelgaard and Mathiesen view furniture as a fundamentally industrial product and so hightech plastics tend to feature strongly in their repertoire of materials. Among the largest commissions Pelikan Design has received was the interior design of the Copenhagen city and suburban railway (1995), which won a Brunel Award in 1996. This project involved a radical space concept whereby asymmetrical bench seats were used to encourage passengers to move toward the windows in order to decrease congestion in the aisles. More recently, Pelikan Design was awarded the Danish Design Council's Annual Award in 1998 for the Wing screen wall (1993) and a Neocon Silver Award in 2000 for the /Q furniture series (1996). With its abiding mission to establish longlasting relationships between its products and users, Pelikan Design remains at the very forefront of Scandinavian design practice. ![](images/00069549f3d9986d14e445d73901287d9ac04c29997299e2c4a1cbcc51942a1b.jpg)

Bertel Gardberg, Canton stainless steel and teak pan for Hackman, 1957 Bertel Gardberg, silver and teak chocolate pot (handmade), 1955 ![](images/a293477d0693aa8dff156cac59559fdb55482af388435101826a595c42b25e67.jpg) # bertel gardberg(Fiih b,118) ![](images/1404af1caaec1db071ffe9d9e7a91c5a2a6b2269986f9550f576c8dfcffb627e.jpg) The Finnish metalware and jewellery designer Bertel Gardberg studied at the Goldsmith's School, Helsinki, before training at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (Institute of Industrial Arts), Helsinki from 1938 to 1941. In1949 he established his own studio in Helsinki and subsequently designed and produced his own silverware products, such as the cuboid teak and silver tea caddy (1955) and his silver and palisander Mocha service (1957). He also designed objects for industrial production that were manufactured by Fiskars and Hackman, among others. For the latter, his product designs include the stainless steelCarelia cutlery (1957)and the Canton cooking pan (1957) – both of which possess a remarkable aesthetic refinement that testifies to Gardberg's careful attention todetail andskilful handling of materials. The perfectionism and timelessness of products such as these earned Gardberg widespread recognition and numerous awards, including gold and silver medals at the Milan Triennale of 1954 (X), 1957(XI)and 1960(XII), andthe Lunning Prize in 1961. Between 1966 and 1971 Gardberg lived in Dublin, where he was artistic director of the Kilkenny Design Workshop(1966-1968) and technical director of the Rionor company in Kilkenny (1968-1971). During this period, he attempted to revitalize Ireland's decorative arts by promoting the role of craftsmanship in local workshops. After returning to his native Finland, Gardberg established a silver and woodworking studio in Pohja in 1973. There he continued to produce exquisite jewellery and small sculptures set with a combination of precious and semi-precious stones, as well as ecclesiastical silver that was produced by Noormarkun Kotiteollisuus. Gardberg also designed other domestic products for industrial production such as his pewter tobacco jars, which were produced by Hopeatehdas. Known as the father of Finnish Arts & Crafts, Gardberg believed that, “It's not just brains that think, as every craftsman knows. The hands think, too, when they work with various materials. And between the hands and the brain lies the human heart, and love for the work. You have to respect the qualities of the material." Like many other Scandinavian designers, both past and present, Gardberg elegantly demonstrated through the aesthetic and functional integrity of his work that the “crafts are the imagination of industry”. ![](images/0910a5465f896eef12619ad47a64ca428d1c29d3086c6268bd8ccca78e78c9c4.jpg)

Simon Gate, Parispokalen (Paris Cup) for Orrefors, 1925- exhibitedin the Swedish Pavilion at the Paris 1925 “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriel Modernes" ▶>Simon Gate, Viking pitcher and glasses for Orrefors, 1917 ![](images/e080c6e5a01e6c4058ee621dfc0f35ceb989bb8c183c5b174e59c60aaf1d8859.jpg) # simon gat.1 n) ![](images/ca8ec765a4d7d805b2a3eddfa3b097fede68c0eba1f05cc86bc08991c2af256b.jpg) One of the most innovative Swedish glass designers of the 20th century, Simon Gate initially studied painting at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design) and Tekniska Skolan (School ofTechnology), Stockholm, from 1902 to 1909, and then from around 1909 at the Kungliga Konsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts), Stockholm. In 1913, Johan Ekman purchased the Orrefors glassworks and subsequently began looking for a designer to help develop the company's products along more artistic lines. Already an accomplished figurative artist, in 1916 Simon Gate became the first “artist-designer” to join the glassworks. His product designs fell into two categories – simple functional wares that employed updated traditional forms which were produced by Sandvik, Orrefors' sister company, and finely engraved pictorial pieces with Baroque inspired decoration which epitomized the “Swedish Grace” decorative style. One of Gate's most celebrated designs was the Parispokalen (Paris Cup) that was produced specifically for the Swedish Pavilion at the 1925 “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes” in Paris, where it was awarded a Grand Prix. This large showpiece vase measuring 75 cm in height was adorned with intricate classical motifs, which required around 450 hours to engrave. On seeing this design, as well as others by Gate and Edward Hald at the exhibition, the French critic Léon Deshair declared that the two designers “pensent en verre” (thought in glass). Certainly Gate and Hald's work from this period though quite different in style from one another, marked a crucial turning point for Swedish glass and opened up new markets for Orrefors. Apart from his delicately engraved pieces, Gate also designed undecorated glassware such as the Slottglass (Castle glass) of 1923 – a green glass goblet with a fluted form that predicted the later and more sculptural Scandinavian style. From 1916 onwards, Gate collaborated with the master glassblower Knut Bergkvist and developed a new technique known as Graa/ after the Holy Grail, which involved casing clear glass blanks with thin layers of colouredglass. Patterns were then cut, etched or engraved into the objects to expose the underlying clear glass. These pieces were then reheated and coated with a layer of clear glass, and finally blown into their required form. By using this laborious and expensive method, decorative patterns could be encased to dramatic effect within glass bodies. During the 1930s, Gate's designs became increasingly simplified in both form and decoration. Often he would use broadly cut geometric motifs that gave his objects a jewel-like faceted quality. Around the same period, Gate also designed bulbous thick-walled vases which accentuated the inherent optical and reflective qualities of glass and provided a strong sense of mass. Throughout his career Gate experimented profusely with glass and developed numerous innovative techniques that were used by later designers at Orrefors. Like so many other Scandinavian designers, Gate perfected his craft by developing studio art pieces that were subsequently translated into designs suitable for industrial mass production. As one of the fathers of Swedish art glass, Gate had an enormous influence on subsequent generations of Scandinavian glass-designers, and through the sheer virtuosity of his work helped focus international attention on Scandinavian design during the inter-war years. ![](images/e493804981164277819e64677774d3284bb7dedf9c535a89f097b67f7f5a8a8e.jpg) ![](images/af89cee211c3202ce7fe9ce2f0582ede65bff5964a56b088c10468da38e56c9e.jpg) △Simon Gate, Thousand Windows cut-glass vase and bowl for Orrefors, c. 1930 Simon Gate, Triton vase for Orrefors, 1916-1917 ![](images/737f1215fcf4c2ed6c0557ef2fbacbcdf57324b274b46f0fa6fbed400aafc3a9.jpg)

Gustav Gaudernack, design for enamelled “plique à jour" compote with peonies for David-Andersen, 1904 ▶ Gustav Gaudernack, design for enamelled "plique à jour” Dragonfly bowl for David-Andersen, 1908 ▶∇Gustav Gaudernack, Dragonfly bowl for David-Andersen, 1909 OVERLEAF 4Gustav Gaudernack, Design for two enamelled "plique à jour" floral goblets for David- Andersen, c. 1904 Gustav Gaudernack, Design for enamelled "plique à jour" Dragonfly and Water Lily bowl, c. 1908 ![](images/a8f6cc44c4d673d7558c7179e560b023da91d1355e44c575daaaa2fe42d84d1c.jpg) # gustav gaudernack ## (active in Norway, b. 1865 Bohemia – d. 1914) Born in Bohemia and trained at the Ceramics Vocational School in Tetschen(1885-1887)and at the Kunstgewerbeschule (School of Arts and Crafts) in Vienna (1888–1891), Gustav Gaudernack emigrated to Norway in 1891, where he became one of the main exponents of the Scandinavian Art Nouveau style. The same year he arrived in Norway he began designing glassware for the Christiania Glasmagasin in Oslo. From 1892 to 1910 he designed metalware embellished with decorative enameling for the David-Andersen company in Oslo, such as his exquisite Dragonfly bowl (1908). Gaudernack's designs epitomized the Art Nouveau style with their radiant enameling and sinuous and stylized forms derived from both flora and fauna. He received widespread acclaim for the skilful execution and imaginative style of his delicate yet sumptuous designs, which rivalled any work emanating from the main centres of Art Nouveau such as Brussels or Nancy. In acknowledgement of this, Gaudernack was awarded a silver medal at the 1900 Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale” and (with David-Andersen) a Grand Prize at the 1904 “Louisiana Purchase Exposition” in St. Louis. In 1910, he established his own workshop in Oslo and continued to design and produce objects using silver, filigree and enamel. Two yearslater, he became the first principal instructor for goldsmithing and enamelling at the Statens Håndverks- og Kunstindustriskole (National College of Art and Design). The work of Gustav Gaudernack reflects the considerable impact that the Art Nouveau style had on Scandinavian design during the early 20th century, especiallyin Norway, where swirling naturalistic motifs were adopted for furniture and textiles as well as for posters and food packaging. ![](images/dc960f8594305081bfa4a3546a9b73992b633175bea37fdcff01e02e2a9e4d9b.jpg) ![](images/7e0cf1db44924d2c716ec838e1a166033652c940e0b6b060c075b46f76c1c2fd.jpg) ![](images/2efc548507ab08460c16192263a043c2d8a8d27f671bac6e4b81c7828beb15f1.jpg) ![](images/8882da0119d374e75bbf3b202b5f36079419f060024754b1392130c4f11d8349.jpg)

ElsaGullberg, Fish textile hanging for Elsa Gullberg Textilier, c. 1950 ▶ Arthur Percy, Spaljé (Trellis-work) screen-printed rayon textile for Elsa Gullberg Textilier, 1936 – Gullberg's company also produced other designers'textiles ![](images/751f128bf4b691d04e1f89e5b55706589d8378003ac28cde4af9ad12b8fd8a67.jpg) # elsa gullbergswedis 8819984) The Swedish textile designer Elsa Gullberg learnt the art of weaving at her family home in Scania. As a young woman she also practised carpentry, metalwork and printing prior to and during her studies at the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design)in Stockholm. From around 1910 she began designing textiles that were intended for either traditional craft production or industrial manufacture. Aware of the design reforms advocated by the German Werkbund and well grounded in the tenets of functionalism, Gullberg was director of the testing and furniture design departments at the Svenska Slöjdföreningen (Swedish Society of Craft and Industrial Design) from 1907 to 1917. She later went on to head the Society's intermediary bureau from 1917 to 1924, where she gained first-hand experience of largescale industrial production methods. She eventually established her own textile company known as AB Elsa Gullberg Textilier in 1927, which produced knotted hangings and carpets with rhythmic folkstyle patterns. Around this time she also began experimenting with the weaving of synthetic silklike materials, such as rayon, which led to her designing a checked upholstering textile (1930) for Nordiska Kompaniet. In 1955, Gullberg handed over the running of her company to her daughter, Elsa-Maria Gullberg, but continued to design textiles and carpets, some of which were suitable for domestic weaving techniques. During her career she also produced a number of specially commissioned textiles, including those for the Stockholm Concert Hall, Gothenburg Town Hall, Malmö City Theatre, and King's College Chapel in Cambridge. Like fellow textile designer Märta Måås-Fjetterström (1873–1941), Elsa Gullberg played a major role in the revitalization of hand-weaving that took place in Sweden during the inter-war years; but more significantly she was also an important pioneer of textile design for industrial production. Whether produced by hand or in a factory, Gullberg's brightly coloured textiles reflected her unquestionable skill and knowledge of design and manufacture. Her company also produced a wide range of modern mix-and-match upholstering textiles, as well as tapestry hangings by other designers, such as Birgitta Graf, Arthur Carlsson Percy (1886– 1976)andEinar Forseth(1892–1988).

Daniel Johan Carlsson, flintware teapot and covered dish with Viking Revival style decoration for Gustavsberg, 1877 ▶Wilhelm Kåge, green glazedvase with silveroverlay decoration for Gustavsberg, 1930 ∇ The Gustavsberg workshop, 1896 ![](images/e4f80ff28980b233f72400ee20a9f736099f428450939952d2f9206b3f5358ca.jpg) # gustavsberg (founded 1825, Gustavsberg, Sweden) ![](images/172b6e26055e9a36a0931babf41f959d4cbeeb614a41a4b045a43f2196fb5411.jpg) The Gustavsberg ceramics factorywasfounded in 1825 at Gustavsberg, on the island of Värmdö, on a former brickworks site. Initially the factory produced designs that were hand-painted with simple floral patterns but by the late 1830s the company had adopted various new techniques for ceramics manufacture from England, including transfer printing. At this stage Gustavsberg was also producing bone china using imported clay from Cornwall, and employed British workers skilled in china making. During this period, the patterns used often closely followed those of British wares. Reputedly, one of the owners of the factory had a double-walled suitcase in which he smuggled copper-plates between England and Sweden. Nevertheless, the factory soon became celebrated for its wares and achieved commercial success when its Parian figurines (also based on British antecedents) were awarded a gold medal at the 1867 Paris “Exposition Universelle”. In the mid-1860s, Gustavsberg broadened its production of both “art” wares and tableware, but duringthe 1870s it came under increasing attack for the “bad taste” of its products. The management responded to this criticism by issuing ceramics that were truly Swedish in nature, and by commissioning August Malmström(1829-1901) and Magnus Isäus (1841–1890) to produce designs in the Viking style. By 1900, under the directorship of Wilhelm Odelberg, the factory had 1,000 employees and was manufacturing delicately painted ceramics designed by Gunnar Wennerberg in the ArtNouveau style. The factory's output later came under the influence of the Svenska Slöjdföreningen (Swedish Societyof Craft and Industrial Design), founded in 1845, an organization that was later inspired by the Deutscher Werkbundand simi larly promoted an alliance between art and industry. Its rallying cry for the production of Vackrare Vardagsvara (MoreBeautiful Everyday Objects) was taken up byWilhelm Kåge, who had been appointed the artistic director of Gustavsbergin 1917. Under Kåge's leadership during the 1920s and 1930s, the factory's output became increasingly influenced by the Modern Movement. Kåge's stackable open-stock Praktika ware (1933), for example, was utterly utilitarian and epitomized the functionalist approach to design that was being promoted by the Continental avant-garde. In 1937, Kåge designed a less austere and more popular service known as Soft Shapes, which predicted ![](images/6d01169e69eab3462958d7789aa7ea4b06ee7f7a421fbe05ef89d2ce5933e438.jpg) ![](images/a3826f010d2b727ba0461c01c2173ac11bd731d2be733072be924dbada0eb7a5.jpg) △Wilhelm Kåge, various stoneware vessels and plate for Gustavsberg, late 1950s Δ> Berndt Friberg, large stoneware vase for Gustavsberg, 1959 ▶ Carl-Arne Breger, stoneware water jug for Gustavsberg, 1957-1958 ![](images/9960f4603b1d708a39ccffb9c43f73fc48458e428a64112de3c35d80c88987f6.jpg) the organic forms that were to become so characteristic of later Scandinavian design. The same year, Gustavsberg was sold to the Kooperativa Förbundet (Swedish Co-Operative Union and Wholesale Society) and the factory was modernized. Two years later, it began production of sanitary wares, and in 1945started manufacturing products in plastics. In 1949, Stig Lindberg took over from Wilhelm Kåge as artistic director. During his tenure, Gustavsberg produced both industrial products and more limited artistic wares. During the1970s, Sven-Eric Juhlin (b. 1940) – who founded Ergonomi Design Gruppen – and Jan Landqvist designed sanitary wares and plastic products for the company that were primarily intended for institutional use. In 1980, Karin Björquist (b. 1927) succeeded Lindberg as the factory's artistic director and in 1987 the company was sold to the Finnish company Wärtsilä. Later, Gustavsberg was divided into three small companies that continued to produce ceramics in its old factory. Since 2000, the company has been part of the Villeroy & Boch group and today specializes in the design and manufacture of sanitary ware. ![](images/3c9857643042eae15d2c40d6c2974266b61538859c20bf1bba7a3ba3ebceab38.jpg) ## 312110987654321 44Per-Olof Landgren, plastic measuring jug for Gustavsberg, 1977 ![](images/6227f66963849300a4d32407e04fce1cdd733a582e7a2a8af0512cdd697f69a8.jpg) Gunnar Larsson, Kulan ashtray for Gustavsberg, 1970 – later produced by Eusto-Idealplast ∇ Sven Erik Juhlin, plastic mugs for Gustavsberg, 1972 ![](images/5c1af07baeb5102e1f6d73f99a283c8bb4dcf0bfe7e4a524d21eed629f72af7e.jpg) ![](images/d54dc90e9c435c83cad6359417cc3dbc2958f1ffa79a2a77fb1fb5cb9ae28dee.jpg) ## hackman founded o pur,ianand) ![](images/0746fafddb1c90369a4c6c0167b30317bc9feb2ca0d92853b83b4167d4ec86f3.jpg) In 1790, the merchant Johan Friedrich Hackman established a trading company in Viipuri thaf sold wooden products, including goods made from sawn timber. This wood was lumbered from forests owned by the company and processed in its sawmills. When Johan Friedrich Hackman died in 1807 his widow consolidated the company. At the same time, his son introduced a number of industriallyproduced products that were made and retailed by the company. By the early 1860s, Hackman was using steam-driven saws to increase its productivity, and the company's subsequent expansion led to the acquisition of the Havi Candle Factory in 1866 and the founding of a cutlery factory, close to Viipuri, in 1876. This new, larger enterprise undertook the manufacture of a wide range of wares, including kitchen knives, hunting knives, scissors, skates and forks. Hackman later exhibited at the 1878 Paris “Exposition Universelle", where it was awarded a gold medal for its kitchenware and cutlery designs, In 1891, the company's cutlery factory was relocated close to its sawmill in Sorsakoski. After a fire at the sawmill in 1898, the factory expanded its product-line to include handcrafted tools, a variety of household utensils, and a number of products made of sheet metal, In 1902, Hackman began using a nickel-steel alloy for its knife and fork handles – a new material that had been developed in France in 1885 and was remarkable for its strength. Later in 1923, Hackman began manufacturing products in another newly developed material – stainless steel. Like other Scandinavian manufacturers, Hackman was highly instrumental in the promotion of this revolutionary material through its production of elegant yet popular designs. During the early 1950s, Hackman introduced its first stainless steel saucepans (which were initially made by hand) and started commissioning well-known designers such as Kaj Franck to contribute to its product-line. The company subsequently received widespread recognition when it launched Bertel Gardberg's Canton cooking pan and Carelia cutlery in 1957 – designs that exemplified Hackman's pursuit of design and manufacturing excellence, Over the following years, Hackman continued to specialize in the production of stainless steel household wares, which on occasion featured handles made of exotic hard woods or plastics. One of the most iconic designs produced by Hackman was Tapio Wirkkala's ## stainless steel and nylon-handled Puukko knife (1961), which was inspired by traditional Sami hunting knives. During the 1960s and 1970s, Nanny Still McKinney and Ilmari Tapiovaara also produced designs for the company. In 1969, Hackman & Co became part of a joint stock company and later in 1989, joined an amalgamated trust group known as Designor Oy (which today also owns the Arabia, littala and Rörstrand brands). Having subsequently become the leading design house in Finland, the company reaffirmed its commitment to avant-garde design by holding a large design competition in 1990, with the winning entries being put into production. During the late 1990s and early 2000s, Hackman commissioned a number of leading designers, including Björn Dahlström, Harri Koskinen, Carina Seth-Andersson (b. 1965), Antonio Citterio (b. 1950) and Ross Lovegrove (b. 1958), to create a new and exciting range of cutlery, utensils and cookware known as the Tools collection. The manufacture of functional innovative and simple products such as these has ensured Hackman's position at the forefront of a major international revival of interest in Finnish design. Δ Stefan Lindfors, Tools salad bowl and servers for Hackman, 1998 ΔΔ Bjömn Dahlström, Tools sauté pan änd grill pan for Hackman, 1998 4 Renzo Piano Workshop, Tools cutlery for Hackman, 1998 Δ Tapio Wirkkala, stainless steel and nylon Puukko knife for Hackman, 1961 – based on traditional Lapp hunting knives and produced up to the 1970s

![](images/b313539c9747eebe818f6f2ff7efc9a348976c64bfbb1d3b418da851930de5a6.jpg) Gro Bergslien, apple shaped glass paperweight in opaque glass for Hadeland, 1976 # hadeland (founded 1762 Jevnaker, Norway) ![](images/b8a7d6c53fefd8cc3bba70343daba8b73e97c7bfff0168108e2373194c6f2241.jpg) In 1762, the Danish-Norwegian state under the rule of Christian IV began building a glassworks on a site it owned in Jevnaker. Three years later the Hadeland Glassworks fired up its ovens for the first time and started producing colourful cobalt blue bottles, pharmaceutical glassware, and floats for fishing nets. Hadeland was an enlightened employer who granted its workforce various social rights and advantages, including the provision ofschooling for the workers' children. The glassworks was sold in 1824 and in 1842 Hadeland was amalgamated with two other glassworks to form a privately owned company. After the introduction of transparent colourless glass in 1852, Hadeland began producing tableware products, including drinking glasses, bowls, dishes and vases – both plain and undecorated. In 1898, the glassworks became part of the Christiania Glasmagasin company, which had (and stil has) a large shop on the main square in Oslo (or as it was known then, Christiania). In response to government pressure on factories to increase the design quality of their products, in 1929 Hadeland appointed Sverre Petterson (1884-1958) as its first artistic director. Initially, Petterson's designs were light and graceful, but by the mid-1930s they became heavier and more architectural, thus reflecting the influence of the Art Deco style. Under his guidance, the glassworks hired thetalented designer Willy Johansson (a third-generation glass blower) in 1936. At Hadeland, Johansson learnt sandblasting techniques from an in-house apprenticeship with the sculptor Ståle Kyllingstad, as well as engraving methods at the Christiania Glasmagasin. From 1947, Johansson headed the Hadeland design team and in 1954 he received a Diplôme d'Honneur at the X Milan Triennale for his glassware, which was distinguished by simple elemental forms. During the 1950s, Hadeland also produced glassware designed by Herman Bongard (b. 1921)and Arne Jon Jutrem (b. 1929), which also possessed an unadorned solidity that emphasized the intrinsic qualities of the glass. Since the 1950s, Hadeland has produced both pressed and blownglassware, ranging from theutilitarian to the artistic (such as Gro Bergslien's apple forms of 1976). In 1988 Arne Jon Jutrem became the artistic director of Hadeland's design department and during his tenure the glassworks has produced distinctive sculptural glassware designed by, among others, Maud Bugge and Lena Hansson. Although perhaps lacking the sheer brio of Finnish glassware, Hadeland's output has nonetheless been central to Norwegian design. Today, the glassworks remains responsible for around eighty percent of the country's total glass production. ![](images/7e4e303ef7a8d6aa491a0cb54bf9abc0a13090b8b7b1306eb702792aa853460a.jpg) IngerMagnus, Glide carafes with shot glass stoppers for Hadeland, 1990 ∇ Hadeland glassware from the Nostetangen collection, based on 19th centurymodels ![](images/a5206a9ec1ac3a20936c34f3c3f771dda507bd20a00eabd7a91e00a169b58884.jpg)

![](images/c3071833fc0da2a7e29434d789db55d0f4c97d30f732a85895da2fa6ca011d77.jpg) ▶Edward Hald, Bollspelande Flickor (Girls Playing Ball) vessel for Orrefors, 1919 # edward hald ## (b. 1883 Stockholm – d. 1980Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/809e4dc1246db4f3596e83edaeb9322dfb902ec3deb6f876e8620980411304c6.jpg) The Swedish glass and ceramic designer Edward Hald trained in England and Germany from 1903 to 1904 before furthering his studies at the Technische Akademie (Polytechnic) in Dresden from 1904 to 1906. At this latter institute Hald was undoubtedly inspired by the reforming activities of the Dresdner Werkstätten für Handwerkskunst (Dresden Workshops for Arts and Crafts, founded in 1898) and its advocacy of well-designed products for everyday use. In 1907, Hald went on to study painting at the Artists' Studio School in Copenhagen, and from 1908 to 1912 trained under Henri Matisse(1869–1954), whose Fauvist style had a major influence on the decoration of his later designs. Between 1917and 1927 Hald designed a number of ceramic products for Rörstrand, including the earthenware Halda service(1919)with blue rustic-style transferprinted decoration. This tableware, which epitomized the elegant “Swedish Grace” style, was illustrated in Gregor Paulsson's influential 1919 publication, Vackrare Vardagsvara (Good Everyday Goods). In 1917, Hald also began designing modernistic glassware for Orrefors. Like his contemporary, Simon Gate, Hald executed a number of engraved narrative pieces for Orrefors, even if his style was influenced more by trends in contemporary fine art than by historic classical styles. His Bollspelande Flickor (Girls Playing Ball) vessel of 1919, for example, was adorned with simplified expressionistic motifs that drew stylistic references from Henri Matisse's art. In response to the Svenska Slöjdföreningen's (Swedish Society of Craft and Industrial Design) rallying cry for beautiful yet functional wares for everyday living, Hald also designed more utilitarian glassware, which was shown to much acclaim at the 1917 “Home Exhibition” held at the Liljevalchs galleryin Stockholm and was produced by Sandvik (which became an Orrefors subsidiary in 1918) from1920 to 1930. These moulded products made of muted coloured soda glass were notable for their softened traditional forms and simple ribbed or fluted decoration. In the late 1920s, Hald began devising even more rational pressed glass designs that were suitable for large-scale mass production. It was his virtuoso engraved “art” glass pieces, however, such as his exuberant Fyrverkeriskålen (Fireworks) bowl of 1921, that brought Hald the most recognition at the time and led to him being awarded a Grand Prix at the 1925 “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes” in Paris. While continuing to design for Orrefors and Rörstrand, Hald was also the artistic director of the Karlskrona porcelainfactory from 1924 to 1933. He became managing director of Orrefors in 1933, and although the glassworks experienced cuts in exports and shortages of raw materials during the Second World War, it continued to undertake both technical and artistic experimentation. During this period, Hald developed the Cut Graal and Fish Graa/ cased-glass techniques, while the forms he adopted became softer and simpler. In 1945, Hald was awarded the Prince Eugen Medal for his glasswaredesigns, but two years later he decided to resume painting, while simultaneously remaining a freelance designer for Orrefors until the late 1970s. As one of the leading proponents of Modernism in Sweden, Hald's great skill lay in his ability to impart the high-object integrity of his exclusive craft-based pieces to product designs that were intended for industrial mass production. ![](images/d2161d8dfaed6e73d13d0cd504ed01ba9802f912a9b6ae848ed44d12220bca22.jpg) △ Edward Hald, Fish Graal vases for Orrefors, 1937 Frida Hansen, design for Poppy tapestry, c. 1898 ![](images/f6085d491bfb52b9de746a898922b495494cfa592ab69def6612154ad821bd0c.jpg) ▶ Frida Hansen, Summer Night's Dream tapestry hanging, c. 1899 ## frida hansen ## (b. 1855 Hillevåg-by-Stavanger – d. 1931 Oslo, Norway) The Norwegian textile designer Frida Hansen studied painting and then later established an embroideryworkshop inStavanger in 1882. During this period she became increasingly familiar with the Norwegian folk tradition of tapestry weaving – since the 18th century “peasant” weavers had produced tapestries depicting relatively primitive biblical scenes. These textiles, whose powerful artistic expression made up for all that they lacked in technical finesse, were generally viewed in Norway as national art treasures. Inspired by these antecedents, Hansen began learning weaving techniques on a standing loom at Kjerstina Hauglum in Sogn, and in 1889 completed her first woven tapestry. A year later she established her own textile studio, and then in 1892 moved to Oslo (then known as Christiania) where in 1899 she founded a weaving company under the name of Det Norske Billedvæveri (DNB). Initially she drew motifs from the ancient Norwegian sagas, but after a trip to Paris around 1885 she embraced the Art Nouveau style. From this period onwards, her tapestries were often adorned with “Mackintosh-style” roses and other highly stylized flowers with strong saturated colours and bold swirling outlines. At the DNB workshop, Hansen not only revived the use of natural dyes and old weaving techniques, but also pioneered special new techniques including her famous “transparent weaving”, which brought her widespread acclaim. This innovative process involved leaving some areas of the warp exposed so as to create a transparent background that contrasted dramatically with the richly coloured woven motifs. During the 1890s, Hansen collaborated with fellow Norwegian designer Gerhard Munthe (who designed several tapestries for DNB), and in 1899 she wrote a book entitled Husflid og Kunstindustri i Norge. She exhibited her work at the 1893 “World's Columbian Exposition” in Chicago and also at the 1900 Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale”, where she was awarded a gold medal.
