By the turn of the century, Frida Hansen was not only the most important Norwegian weaver, she had also become one of thegreatest advocates of the naturalistic Art Nouveau style in Scandinavia. ![](images/878ca14b0bcf6de84309217edb3244302cca57c96f8581e0693fb94ed64fab7c.jpg)

![](images/bfe39ed0f831b500cf555159fd8d11a5e7434d50286f1853308856c402c9fc48.jpg) Søren Hansen, DAN chair for Fritz Hansen, c. 1930 # soren hansen (anish 1951-197) After the First World War, the Danish manufacturer Fritz Hansen began experimenting with the production of bentwood furniture using techniques that had been pioneered at the turn-of-the-century by the Austrian designer and industrialist, Michael Thonet(1796-1871). Eventually, Søren Hansen(a descendent of the company's founder) and Fritz Schlegel (1896–1965)came up with some designs for the company, which after several attempts were successfully put into production. As Karl Mang noted in his publication, History of Modern Furniture (1979), Søren Hansen's bentwood DAN chair of 1930 was, “One of the few truly new ideas for the bentwood process, which was stagnating in terms of formal process." The chair's continuous bent-wood element, which incorporated both the back rest and rear legs was not only a technical masterpiece, but also provided the design with an elegant line that emphasized its structural essentialism. Both Hansen and Schlegel went on to design tubular metal Modernist-style furniture for FritzHansen, which was similar to that emanating from Germany at the time and which was shown at the 1932“Den Permanente” exhibition. The same year, Søren Hansen officially joined the family firm and designed a number of chairs for the company, including a painted beech upholstered armchair (c. 1949) thatwas notable for its curvaceous sculptural form. Like other leading Danish designers, Hansen's considerable skill and understanding of construction enabled him to re-invent and modernize existing furniture types, while balancing function and aesthetics so as to achieve optimum solutions for industrial manufacture. Production of a bentwood element for the DAN chair, c. 1930 ![](images/5a835d35094dc2f78ce8cafafb10a631f1d46293a9c9daac1f28ca8f0a6d5d62.jpg) ![](images/4045ba1164eaf5f0b2264cf05ec57cc290623109ddd29f7511c62199f088e73f.jpg) ![](images/739a5eecdb84eedf7c49f641429cf78727dafb5026681b07abd9559add3316df.jpg) 4ΔSøren Hansen, stacking stools for Fritz Hansen, 1953

![](images/ac3107e5b17d697d830c3e4ca7a5ad12c9c9514f338a7c7e94aa703de7f25281.jpg) Sixten Sason, Hasselblad 1600F launched in 1948 – the world's first single-lens reflex camera in 6x6 format with interchangeable lenses, film cartridges and view-finders ∇Victor Hasselblad # hasselblad (founded 1941Gothenburg,Sweden) ![](images/3944f806f83c325557e899986e4d0a8946e4696e814bf53c183bf3b81a0a765e.jpg) ![](images/4ac4e66c6b5628acc9f64342ed72214dc6ba4df5551c94a2eae0145b425e617e.jpg) Renowned throughout the world, Hasselblad cameras have been used by a plethora of famous photographers, from Ansel Adams to Irving Penn, to create some of the mosticonic images of all time. The origins of Victor Hasselblad AB can be traced back to an earlier wholesale and distribution company, which was established in 1841 byVictor Hasselblad's(1906-1978)grandfather. This firm sold all kinds of products – musical instruments, knives, perfume, toys, oil lamps, buttons, soap, crinolines, pens, and water closets – and by the 1870s was retailing lanternes magiques imported from France and various photographic and stereoscopic pictures. Later, the company began importing photographic equipment and in 1893 it issued the first Swedish camera catalogue, which not only included the Kodak No. 1 and No. 5 cameras, but also a camera marketed under the name of HasselbladSwedish Express. The company eventually became the official agent for Kodak in Sweden and its founder's son, Victor, became a keen amateur photographer. In turn, his son Victor also developed an interest in photography, which he combined with his love of ornithology to become a pioneer bird photographer. In 1925, he undertook a period of study at Kodak in Rochester, New York, and returned with a somewhat cumbersome Graflex, which was then the best camera available for bird photography. Although he later used a more compact 35mm Leica, he was still not entirely satisfied with the results. In 1933, he published a book entitled, Flyttfågelstråk (The Passage of Migratory Birds) that was illustrated with his own photographs, but by this time he was already thinking about creating an entirely new camera. During the SecondWorld War, a German bomber containing a reconnaissance camera was shot down over Swedish territory. When theSwedish Air Force took the camera to Victor Hasselblad to see if he could produce a replica, he reputedly said, “No, but I can make a better one!" The resulting handheld military aerial camera, the HK7, was the first single-lens reflex camera in 6x6 format with exchangeable film cartridges, and was manufactured for the Swedish Air Force from 1941 to 1945. This undertaking proved an invaluable experience, particularly in terms of what Hasselblad learnt about the construction of shutters for use in very cold temperatures, the benefits of motorized camera operation and interchangeable cassette systems, and the value of high-resolution optics that could be speedily interchanged. Even before the hostilities of war had ended, Victor Hasselblad began adapting the HK7 for civilian use. To this end he had the foresight to recruit the leading Swedish industrial designer Sixten Sason to work on the project. Using wooden prototypes, Sason developed a highly compact housing for the camera that had a simplified and easy-to-use arrangement of operational buttons. The resulting 1600F, which shared many of the features of the earlier HK7 camera, was launched in New York in 1948 where it met with huge enthusiasm – U.S. Camera magazine commented, “With few reservations we have found this to be the dream camera." The 1600F was an ergonomic masterpiece and became the blueprint for all subsequent Hassel blad cameras. In 1962, when NASA engineers were sketching what they thought would be the ideal space-camera, the astronaut Walter Schirra remarked, “That looks just like my Hasselblad!" A Hasselblad 500C was duly purchased and modified by NASA engineers to fit their exacting specifications. Since then, Hasselblad cameras ![](images/d2ac0cf7972a9b0d9ed03d9011d3d72b481862ebf1b66494c7c8672cbdd60304.jpg) ![](images/bc1f5243852ff66434e0a90613a7d597c418a1e1a9dfcaaab950d00af1b608d7.jpg) have been used on all American space missions, including the first moon landing in 1969. Combining elegant no-nonsense styling and superlative function with high-quality robust construction, the Hasselblad camera system is among the most famous Swedish products of all time. △Hasselblad HK7handheld aerial camera manufactured for the Swedish Air Force, produced from 1941 to 1945 4Hasselblad 205TCC camera, 1991 hadd all38 ![](images/bf4ef40212d0d76ebd86d0b0485079b3ee4ccce768f173d5c09d96bb8d7acc6d.jpg) Edwin E. Aldrin with the Hasselblad EDC (Electronic Data Camera) lunar camera 1969 – Hasselblad cameras have been included on every NASA manned space mission since 1962 Hasselblad 503CW camera – equipped with winder CW and view-finder PME90, 1996 ![](images/1f5b194f2ea396e9bdfb6fe54c16187500b1d9cc26c905c3b420b2ff41f13896.jpg) ![](images/03fa11a6465a771b5d0c90051dd53480efe0eac9374c745324e8eef815fef789.jpg) Piet Hein, Model no. 1147A Superegg silver box for Georg Jensen, 1966 ![](images/52325d8f8839a1c2f496c892750b97b254434296c91fa6991dcb1427515e3236.jpg) ▶> Piet Hein, leather and steel bar stool for Fritz Hansen, 1961 ## piet hein (b. 1905 Copenhagen – d. 1996 Funen, Denmark) ![](images/ed04c1c696918978d7c920ecdc16a8a693d6992968b78e53a492bf9eea6ed646.jpg) The Danish mathematician, author, inventor and designer Piet Hein studied at the Copenhagen University, Copenhagen, and the Kungliga Kunsthögskolan (Royal University College of Fine Arts), Stockholm. Throughout Scandinavia, he is best known for his thousands of epigrammatic poems, which he referred to as “grooks” and published under the pseudonym, “Kumbel”. He also wrote extensively on the relationship between the arts and sciences. While working as a consultant on a town-planning project in 1959 that encompassed the design of Sergels Square in Stockholm, he devised the concept of the “superellipse” as a way of solving traffic problems. Halfway between an oval and a rectangle, the “superellipse” form was repeated in his product, lighting and furniture designs, such asthe Superegg box (1966) for Georg Jensen and theSuperellipse table (1964), which he co-designed with Bruno Mathsson for Fritz Hansen. For his achievements as an inventor and his work in the sciences Hein was awarded the Alexander Graham Bell Prize in 1968, and an honorary doctorate from Yale University in 1972. His pioneering work as a designer was also recognized when he was awarded the prestigious ID Prize and the German “Die gute Industrieform” Award in 1971. Hein's product designs can be seen as an outcome of the continuous search for the “ideal” form – a defining characteristic of much Scandinavian design. Unlike most other practitioners, however, Hein used mathematics as the basis of his solutions rather than historic precedent. He was a wry commentator on design, who summarized his definition of good design in a poem entitled Form and Content: “Most design is sheer disaster/hiding what one doesn't master. True design asks one thing of us/to uncover what it covers." ![](images/769af3b67e074c99aa470cb62628ff8532c93bb522e59ed460b5af0343c10cee.jpg)

![](images/1a17ef69d7a47757986cdfe3ffc90714c768484ab541a4c1510775f30cd44ed1.jpg) Poul Henningsen, PH table lamp for Louis Poulsen, 1927 # poul henningsen ![](images/0f704eccfb9bdbd79c9f208593ccdaf6858f28e2d9d6bbaae6863aebbdcb4c7d.jpg) ## (b. 1894 Ordrup – d. 1967 Ordrup, Denmark) One of the greatest lighting designers of all time, Poul Henningsen studied building techniques at the Tekniske Højskole (School of Technology), Copenhagen, from 1911 to 1914, and then architecture at the Polyteknisk Læreanstalt (Polytechnic) from 1914 to 1917. While studying architecture he designed his first chandelier as part of an interior design project. After leaving the Polyteknisk, having not sat his final exam, Henningsen worked as a reviewer for the art journal Vor Tid and as co-editor of the art periodical Klingen from 1917 to 1921. He was also a journalist for the newspapers Politiken and Extra Bladet from 1921 to 1925. During this early part of his career he experimented with different materials such as copper and glass, and designed a number of light fixtures including a chandelier for the Carlsberg brewery in 1919. Three years later he exhibited a spherical light fitting with reflective planar rings at the “Artists' Autumn Exhibition”, and designed the reflective Slotsholm street light forCopenhagen's municipal lighting department. As a left-wing radical who throughout his life agitated for social improvement, Henningsen used his column in Politiken as a platform for presenting his views on design – on the need for better lighting he wrote: “The aim is, by working scientifically, to make lighting cleaner, more economical, and more beautiful." In 1924, he developed a series of multi-shaded table and hanging lights that were designed to reduce the dazzling glare of the modern electric bulb. The sameyear, he began designing lights for Louis Poulsen and the first lampfrom his subsequent PH series was exhibited to great acclaim at the 1925 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes”, where he was awarded a gold medal. Henningsen's revolutionary PH series of hanging, wall, and table lamps were the result of many years of scientific study and fine-tuning. By using light diffusion graphs and reflection sketches he was able to develop a system of lights with alternately shaped and positioned shades that could direct light downward, provide good ambient light, and minimize the intensity of the reflected light and bulb – all at the same time. The logarithmic spiral configuration of the elegantly curved PH shades was reputedly inspired by a cup, bowl and plate stacked on top of one another. The PH lamps, which were designed for mass production and are still manufactured ![](images/255a6acb4636bdfc36a026eeda2eb8c6873412e9ed57b46366dc7cbc1564aa92.jpg) by Louis Poulsen, were widely exported throughout Europe, North and South America, Africa and Asia. Prior to the Second World War, they were especially popular in Germany, where they were featured in Das Neue Frankfurt magazine and Licht und Beleuchtung, the Deutsche Werkbund's architects' reference book on lighting.In the early 1930s Henningsen also designed furniture such as his Slangestolen (Snake chair, 1932), which was constructed of a single length of bent tubular metal, for the Copenhagen-based manufacturers V.A. Høffding. Throughout much of his life, Henningsen denounced what he saw as the artistic pretensions of Scandinavian design and urged for a more utilitarian approach that would bring good design to the masses. Unlike the majority of Modern Movement designers, however, Henningsen regarded traditional forms and materials as eminently suitable for the manufacture of more democratic products. On his death in 1967, he left over one hundred lighting designs, some of which have been issued posthumously. ![](images/2b7d2b7b487ad465ea25792feef6521308ffb70079ee8157d36c2ab63bc5613d.jpg) ΔPoul Henningsen, tubular steel and red leather Slangestolen (Snake chair) for V.A. Høffding, 1932 △ Tubular steel furniture designed by Poul Henningsen for V. A. Høffding on display in Fredericia, 1932 ![](images/be49a446c9859788605d7a4e3574e887625f7a67ae671a68c8d85a9f455b20da.jpg) ![](images/7ef622c8b80e9fd99a723429e1171508c4834cdcc373856c28a59bbe67ef79bb.jpg) ![](images/141bbb817e425c6a87d18e263fe9b0c5fb749c224cb82abd9d3f800bd45b468e.jpg) ![](images/4f8265ac2c3031e6cc883eb9061b115f4b97543ab92bacca537229e0a53d1429.jpg)

Erik Herløw, silver cocktail shaker for A. Michelsen, c. 1950 ![](images/bea1d5bada43818fbb886f0d03ea659837638f6b34b5091a67605d0a1ec4d1d9.jpg) > Erik Herløw, aluminium water pitcher with bakelite handle for Dansk Aluminium Industri, 1952 # erik herløw (b. 1913 Helingsør – d. 1991 Helingsør, Denmark) ![](images/367b3caa6597932c15954a161599a4f24258f5bb2a5c9f4a0463a15a5cdfcc7a.jpg) Erik Herløw studied architecture at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) in Copenhagen from 1937 to 1944. He established his own practice in Copenhagen in 1945, and over the course of his career undertook the design of numerous architectural projects, including several state-funded exhibitions on Danish design, the United States Embassy in Copenhagen in 1952(with Jørgen Juul Møller), an oil refinery for Shell, and various factories. Herløw is also well known for his industrial design work. From 1942 to 1949 he designed for the silversmith A. Michelsen and later designed metalware products for Georg Jensen, Norsk Stålpress, and Dansk Aluminium Industri. For the latter company he re-designed (1952-1953) a standard aluminium and bakelite coffee pot, which the firm was already manufacturing, into a sleek and streamlined product that used significantly fewer components in its construction. Between 1952and 1954 Herløw was director of design instruction at the Guldmedehojskolen (College for Gold- and Silversmiths), Copenhagen, and from 1955 to 1979 he served as artistic adviser to the Royal Copenhagen porcelain factory. He also co-founded the Danish Industrial Design Society, was professor of industrial design at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) from 1959 to 1979, and during the late 1970s undertook the interior design of the SAS A-300 Airbus. Herløw was a major force in the evolution of Danish industrial design, who believed that the role of the designer included working closely with production specialists so as to have an influence on all aspects of the design process, from initial feasibility studies to the final manufactured product. In this way, products could be assessed and closely evaluated during their development so as to ensure that the best possible solution was achieved. Summarizing his design philosophy he wrote, “The physical design of a product will reach the optimum of its usefulness where there is an interrelation between economy, construction, material, function, aesthetics, ethics, anduse." ![](images/d2353ae04ecde2bb778b8c8362e97a242e1d7b44daa4522161485ee3a08d4da8.jpg) ![](images/7eb0b6bc0a6118dac3e57e7b745c6aebc2cb3b9712b5189296263f1f8ff24ffa.jpg) 4 Erik Herløw, aluminium and bakelite coffee pot for Dansk Aluminium Industri, 1956 – version of his earlier “redesigned" coffee pot from 1952-1953 ∇ Erik Herløw, silver mocha service for A. Michelsen, c. 1950 ![](images/bfc18010c4c27d3961beaef92fd95e7f00ef867266731754902c21e6749b9763.jpg) ![](images/80fbb99172025e67fb4f460fa16c489d7486c1b412ef1245c32cdd464d68629e.jpg) Orla Juul Nielsen, Tranquebar wine glasses for Holmegaard, 1925 #

![](images/8d1fe24fd18492c82c9347ebaa16b22a576334256cc95a5b0056487c0768cf24.jpg) At the beginning of the 19th century, the progressive thinker Count Christian Danneskiold-Samsøe decided to use the peat that occurred in abundance on his large estate as fuel for a new glassworks. Having died before he could see this project through to fruition, his widow, the Countess Henriette Danneskiold-Samsøe, finally managed to establish the glassworks at Holmegaard Marsh in 1825. The first furnace for the factory was built by a Norwegianglassmaker, Christian Wendt, who had previously worked for the Hadeland glassworks. Initially, the factory manufactured cheap dark green bottles, known as bouteilles, which were used for beer or schnapps. After several years, however, the Countess bravely decided to up-grade and broaden the company's product line. To this end, she hired glassmakers from Bohemia and Southern Germany, who in 1835 began producing European-style traditional domestic stemware and “art” glass. In response to increasing competition from Swedish glass manufacturers, in the early 20th century Holmegaard recruited its first well-established designer as a permanent member of staff. Orla Juul Nielsen was employed by the company as part of a co-operative enterprise with Royal Copenhagen to produce glassware for its porcelain dinner services. It was noted in a company letter at the time that, “the factory has found an artist who shares our view of remaining down to earth, primarily in the shape of the drinking glass and the glass bowl, which attempt to come as close to the essential form as the nature of the material will allow, avoiding everything with a hint of over-refinement or artistic lightness, but in its pure simplicity striving for the grand effect." Certainly, the idea that beauty could be achieved through simplicity was not only at the cutting-edge of design thinking at the time, but when put into practice by Holmegaard it led to an important new identity for Danish glassware. In 1924, the architect Jacob Bang joined the design office at Holmegaard and four years later he was appointed the firm's artistic director. Bang subsequently designed a variety of glassware within a modern idiom. His well-known Primula and Viola ranges were widely acclaimed and revealed his talent for producing designs that were simple and practical yet pleasurable to the eye and hand. While Bang's work was highly rational, being intended for the most part for industrial ![](images/291eff8c9733d895a75ec13ddd7678794f2c0f532790d7ed59c9a35476d260b1.jpg) manufacture, it was not devoid of the human touch. Unlike the machine aesthetic that was being promoted at the Bauhaus, Bang's product designs projected a softer and more emotionally seductive aesthetic, which predicted the Scandinavian approach to Modernism that flourished from the 1930s onwards. Bang's work for Holmegaard was also a demonstration of his belief in democratic design – he preferred “not expensive drinking goblets for the Rockefellers, but beer glasses for Mr. Hansen of Denmark”. Nevertheless, Bang diddesign on occasion exclusive“art” glass pieces, including crystal glasses, bowls and cocktail shakers that were delicately engraved and cut with Art-Deco-style motifs. In 1942 he was succeeded by Per Lütken, who remained Holmegaard's artistic director until his death in 1998. During his 56 year tenure, Lütken not only introduced the latest developments in “art” glass, but also attempted to “renew the classical wine glass" by continuing to adopt the traditional cold cracked-off way of producing such products. His best-known design, the Provence bowl (1955), demonstrates theway in which Lütken was able to revolutionize traditional glassmaking through ![](images/7c27918acb1c5233201f532ffc2c820b59f60995b0ac8611bb0db655d654ff06.jpg) ∆ Per Lütken, Winston water jug and glass (green), 1957 & Jacob Bang, opaline glassware range, 1960 – both for Holmegaard Jacob Bang, carafe for Holmegaard, c. 1932 ∇ Michael Bang, Saturn Platina glassware range for Holmegaard, c. 2000 ![](images/7cdb513c73b00f8148dcff9b91542f59d9c18d546699f9c7b37666448157f0e3.jpg) pioneering forms. Products like this epitomized the widely perceived good taste of Danish design during the 1950s and brought Holmegaard much international recognition. Believing that design should “delight our common daily lives”, Lütken perpetuated the Holmegaard ethos of simplicity, classical proportion, and high-quality craftsmanship through his work. In 1968 Jacob Bang's son, Michael, joined the glassworks and subsequently designed a number of successful glassware ranges, such as the elegant Fontaine wine glasses (1987) with bubble encased stems. He hasalso designed “art” glass and several lights, including the Paraso/ table lamp (1970). Other designers who have worked for Holmegaard since the 1980s include Torben Jørgensen (b. 1945), Anja Kjær (b.1956), Arje Griegst(b. 1938), Ann-Sofi Romme (b. 1957), Allan Scharff (b. 1945) and Peter Svarrer (b. 1957). Combining the traditional simplicity of Danish design with new and experimental forms, these individuals have all helped pioneer the sensuous functionalism that has come to epitomize Scandinavian design in the early 21st century. ![](images/7806b27e48c4eebe761f99a18e4f50e4aa286eebae75b2f2c8384c832e24c24f.jpg) ∇Bent Severin, Princess glassware range for Holmegaard, 1957 ∇ From left to right: Darryle Hinz, Neptunchampagne glass, 1991; Arje Griegst, Xanadu goblet, 1983; Torben Jørgensen, goblet with white filigree, 1980; Michael Bang, Fontaine wine glass, 1987; Michael Bang, Monte Carlo cocktail glass, 1984 – all for Holmegaard ∇∇ From left to right: blue vase by Bent Severin, blue bowl by Henning Koppel, blue vase by Nanna & Jørgen Ditzel, white vase by Henning Koppel & white vase by Nanna & Jørgen Ditzel – all for Holmegaard, 1958-1960 ![](images/a531476176d2472d090ec0783f964d8f1e1185b1a968375ad4dbdae299a83175.jpg) ![](images/4713d0e377abe0945ec408ffe3b965486b18585d6905949bf7cc7197cc7b532f.jpg)

![](images/ec900cfc268c41216f059071f6162477bda371b21a5c224c8873a77443dd6d35.jpg) Knud Holscher, d-line door handle, 1972 ▶Knud Holscher, d-line staircasesystem, 1999 # knud holscher (b. 1930 Rødby, Denmark) ![](images/c3301063d596216c25b5ecd1044503136a471d745d82d8fcc8afad4f62e506b7.jpg) The internationally renowned industrial designer Knud Holscher studied architecture at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts), Copenhagen, between 1949 and 1957.From 1959 to 1964 he worked for Arne Jacobsen, initially as a draftsman and then later as an associate participating in one of Jacobsen's most important building projects, St Catherine's College, Oxford. In 1964, Holscher established a design practice in Copenhagen in partnership with the British product designer and architect Alan Tye (b. 1933). Together, they co-designed the Modric range of sanitary fittings, which was “the first comprehensive range of ironmongery manufactured in Britain to an integrated design system”. The Modric range subsequently received a British Design Award and the British Aluminium Industry's Prize in 1966. The following year, Holscher became a partner in the Krohn & Hartvig Rasmussen architecture practice. In 1970, he won another British Design Award for an office signage system and two years later, designed his highly successful d-line range of stainless steel door furniture, which has won numerous awards and has grown to become the world's most extensive range of architectural ironmongery and bathroom fittings. The d-line range with its soft-edged functionalism is a powerfui manifestation of Holscher's “striving for a humanist interpretation of the rational and the presumptive”. In 1975, Holscher designed stainless steel salad servers and tongs for Georg Jensen within a similar vocabulary of form. Today, Knud Holscher Industrial Design is one of the leading Scandinavian design consultancies and numbers among its clients JCDecaux, Erco, Philips, Fritz Hansen, and Facit. The practice has also won numerous competitions for street furniture and signage systems. Holscher's classically Scandinavian humanist approach to industrial design synthesizes the “rational with the irrational” so as to “provide an answer to problems that are so complex that their formulation is found only in, and with, their solution”. Holscher has also designed numerous public and private buildings, including the Royal Danish Theatre in Copenhagen(1978), Næstved Town Hall (1981), and Copenhagen Airport (1983), and withothers has published a book entitled Culture in a Crisis Time (1980). ![](images/4f80d41b859b8ce74f9077d3cb343d254f818ddce5a2b5aa39d67662028474e3.jpg)

FriedI Holzer-Kjellberg, FK-Service tableware for Arabia, 1953 ![](images/189e034226ce958adfd32ff277d3fe4a488643c71f57c2afb9d7fe1a745c8130.jpg) # friedl holzer-kjellberg (active in Finland, b. 1905 Loeben, Austria – d. 1993) Born in Austria, Friedl(Elfriede) Holzer-Kjellberg studied at the Kunstgewerbeschule (School of Arts and Crafts) in Graz. After her formal academic training she moved to Helsinki and in 1924 began working as a designer for Arabia, for whom she was eventually appointed artistic director in 1948. Her ceramic designs for the company were awarded a gold medal at the 1935 Bruxelles “Exposition Universelle et Internationale" and a silver medal at the 1937 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts et Techniques dans la Vie Moderne”. She experimented for ten years before perfecting her famous rice-pattern technique, which involved the meticulous cutting of rice-like patterns into an extremely thin porcelain body so as to leave delicate “open” incisions. These were then covered with a colourless glaze, which produced areas of subtle translucence. Holzer-Kjellberg's exquisite and extremely delicate rice-pattern series reveals the influence of the art of Japan, which was widely felt across the whole of Scandinavian ceramic design in the 20th century. Comprising numerous bowls and a tea service, when first launchedin the early 1940s the rice-pattern tableware was an instant success. It remained in production with Arabia until c. 1974, but due to popular demand was re-introduced in 1986. Apart from her porcelain products, Holzer-Kjellberg also designed a number of one-off stoneware pieces. Having won a gold medal at the 1954 (X)Milan Triennale and the Pro Finlandia Medal in 1966, she continued designing for Arabia until 1970. ![](images/16f330477085e353925736bb4184219f46fca53948ac857c1e1b8679582742b2.jpg)

![](images/5edef4d5dee9be922a866825dca0ac847de53250b5b536725160b8f2637f768f.jpg) Alfred Homann, Ensemble chairs for Fritz Hansen, 1992 # alfrend homan 9 oengn Denrk) ![](images/7a3def7f78a6e3557e7b902164727a6b4bb1690e04829bd6da0ebac1c271d2c0.jpg) One of Denmark's most celebrated and hardworking architects and industrial designers, Alfred Homann graduated in architecture from the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) in 1976. From 1972 to 1979 he was employed in the design practice of Professor Vilhelm Wohlert (b. 1920), who like others of his generation approached architecture holistically, becoming intricately involved in all aspects of the work from the construction process to the details, inventory, and lighting, so as to ensure the highest level of quality. While working in Wohlert's office Homann designed light fittings for the first time – an area of design for which he would later become particularly well known. By the time Homann established his own architecture and industrial design practice in 1978, the holistic approach had become central to his work. Both his Homann wall lamp (1981) and Strata light series (1988), for example, were originally designed site-specific for a major building renovation he undertook in Nyhavn. Whether designing an outdoor lamp or a piece of furniture, Homann seeks “simplicity, clarity and logic”, and believes that the best products are those that are initially designed for a specific context and then later converted into standard manufactured products (Louis Poulsen subsequently put both the Homann and Strata lights into production). In 1992, Homann won widespread recognition – including Germany's Red Dot Award – for his multi-purpose Ensemble chair designed for Fritz Hansen.Theundulating single-form seat shell of this chair, which is injection-moulded in a specially developed recyclable polymer, incorporates an innovative construction technique that produces air pockets in the shell so as to reduce the amount of material used and lessen overall weight. TheEnsemble's seat shell not only affords good lumbar support and resilience, its surface provides a pleasurable tactility. As an efficient stacking chair designed specifically for the contract market, the Ensemble is appropriate for a number of uses including cafeterias, conference rooms, offices and waiting areas. Like his lighting products for Louis Poulsen, Homann's furniture for Fritz Hansen, Rudd International USA, and Labofa benefits from refined detailing and high-quality engineering – attributes that are synonymous with Danish industrial design. Apart from his product design work, Homann has also undertaken numer- ![](images/568e0bc21972e2bc7dcf212609fd469cd7cc596df471f8dfcc75cd71e5c7204c.jpg) ![](images/93c0baf982d9db418e3376284c964c86df99d5084bfe1635c737466588a8211e.jpg) ous architectural projects (both new-build and renovations) for private homes as well as housing projects and public buildings, most notably for DSB(The Danish State Railway). ![](images/74af2585778f0b9b30c24b13cf84aa478fad8159aba7b10e50b7172ea1889a99.jpg) △Alfred Homann, Nyhavn pendant lamp for Louis Poulsen, 1986 △Alfred Homann, Nyhavn parc light for Louis Poulsen, 1984 4 Alfred Homann, Homann bollard for Louis Poulsen, 1992

![](images/1a15e676cf670251d823162e8778fa9c5a7ddcac288bf8839ab237bc95d44fb7.jpg) Göran Hongell, Aarne glassware range for Karhula, 1949-1950 - now produced by littala # göran hongell (b. 1902 Helsinki, Finland – d. 1973) ![](images/43a36a759851526ac78fb8649115117b226c91923428fa0013f8baddd07f6843.jpg) The son of a master builder, Göran Hongell studied decorative painting at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design), Helsinki, from 1919 to 1922. At that point in time “decorative painting” studies were the forerunner of more formal industrial design courses and were intended to equip students with the necessary skills to become either craftsmen in their own right, artistic directors working in factories, workshops and studios, or art teachers.During this period in Finland there was a general concern to forge a new and distinctive national style (as a resultof the country's newly-found independence) and to improve the aesthetic quality of industrially-manufactured products. In response to this climate of reform in design, Hongell became a member of Ornamo (the Finnish Association of Decorative Artists) in 1924. Having undoubtedly read an article in the 1927 Ormano yearbook written by Arttu Brummer (1891–1951), which praised the Svenska Slöjdföreningen (Swedish Society of Craft and Industrial Design)and its promotion of “Beautiful Things for Everyday Use”, Hongell and the decorative painter Gunnar Forsström established a design practice that initially specialised in the design of murals and stained glass. Following this, in 1928 they submitted designs to the Riihimäki glassworks' competition for “useful and tasteful types of glass for everyday use” and won fourth prize. In 1932, Hongell was awarded third prize in a similar glassware competition held by the Karhula glassworks for his Congorilla vase, which was decorated with simple marine-lifemotifs, and the same year he exhibited a series of black moulded designs with silver enamelling at the Finnish Society of Craft and Design exhibition. Also in 1932 Hongell began working as a part-time artistic adviser to Karhulaand the factorysubsequently began manufacturing his unusual decorative black glass designs with classical motifs, which were exhibited at the 1933 (V)Milan Triennale. During the early and mid-1930s Hongell designed a number of cut and engraved glass pieces for Karhula that played off the optical qualities of the material. It was not until 1937, however, that he began designing pressed-glass products that were suited to large-scale industrial mass production. During the 1940s and 1950s, his glassware became increasingly utilitarian, with simplified forms and plain undecorated surfaces. ![](images/97de9ba7b1180932b07fea178b51439dedd783602546bcbd594b5d0e93207c83.jpg) Among his best-known designs from this period were his “sea-green”glass vases, which were designed specifically for export, and his Aarne glasses(1949–1950), which continue to be produced bylittala – having been described in Hufvudstadsbladet in 1951 as, “a compromise between the popular stemmed glasses and the cheaper unstemmed ones. They feel good in the hand and possess a 'festive' quality.”" In 1957, Hongell retired and a year after his death in 1973, a retrospective exhibition of his work was held at the Karhula Glass Museum. Although less wellknown than later designers such as Timo Sarpaneva or Tapio Wirkkala, Hongell was one of the great pioneers of industrial glass design in Finland and was one of the first Scandinavian designers to produce high-quality everyday glassware that was not only extremely functional but also possessed a real sense of style. ![](images/a22a1c05867f819ce9a7f52780360dc937d6437d61fd0d9a2597b4fb53919aae.jpg) △Göran Hongell, blue crystal vase for Karhula, 1930s 4American advertisement for Karhula showing a cut crystal bowl by Göran Hongell late 1930s A SELECTION OF OUTSTANDING VASES AND BOWLS, HAND BLOWN AND HAND ENGRAYED BY KARHULA'S WORLD FAMED CRAFTSMEN. Bowl as illustrated in clear crystal or seagreen Ferne Irwin INTERIOR DESIGN 1519 NORTH MAIN SANTA ANA, CALIF. Saara Hopea, Pantteri vases for Nuutajärvi, 1954 ![](images/d2b72a3ca6e527b8f0275280c65fc9b702502f44e402e40ccae36473f61378ef.jpg) ![](images/346f6127efab8053ed56ed3de54ef0b45ce783c7e50037e4f0a0aeaaefd197c2.jpg) ![](images/13d570544037580346b87d0c3526f52d997440adf60b8e50720df95ab7d4ea63.jpg) # saareae hopea (Finish 256 d.1984) ![](images/e5e4576556dde3ed2fc0a26b94280099fe39bc4a2139ded5e8c9173c8da6d1b3.jpg) The Finnish ceramicist, silverware and glass designer, Saara Hopea, trained as an interior designer at the Konstindustriella Läroverket (College of Industrial Art) in Helsinki. After her studies she worked as a freelance interior designer from 1946 to 1949. In 1952she joined the Arabia ceramics factory and for seven years worked as an assistant to Kaj Franck. She showed her work alongside Franck's in a special exhibition held in Helsinki in 1954, and also participated in a number of other landmark exhibitions including the 1955 Helsingborg “H55” exhibition. At Arabia she designed several unusual ceramic pieces, including the Astarte hors-d'oeuvres tray, which was named after the many-breasted Phoenician fertility goddess and made up of nine “joined” bowls. In 1954 (X) and 1957 (XI) she was awarded silver medals for her “practical pottery” at the Milan Triennale. During the 1950s, she also designed glassware for Nuutajärvi – some of which was suitable for industrial production, such as her moulded pitcher and stacking tumblers, which won a silver medal at the 1954(X) Milan Triennale. Her higher volume designs were generally distinguished by clean undecorated forms and a rainbowof muted colours (the latter being a renowned characteristic of her glassworks), while her other work, such as her exquisite spotted Pantteri vases, fell into the category of “art glass”. Hopea also designed silverware for her family's business, Ossian Hopea, in Porvoo. In 1960, she married the American Oppi Untracht, and the same year emigrated to the United States where she worked as a freelance silver designer for, among others, Potter and Mellen Inc. Her husband taught her enamelling techniques and she subsequently produced numerous copper bowls and plates adorned with sweeping geometric patterns made by overlapping coloured enamels. Returning to her native Finland in 1967, she continued to produce enamelled metalware designs until 1980. Saara Hopea's work not only reveals her unquestionable mastery of materials, form and manufacturing techniques, it also stands in testimony to the significant role women have played in the history of Scandinavian design – a contribution that has facilitated the development of a more humanistic approach to design, especially with those products intended for industrial mass production. ![](images/8ad66aecae75d32cfb8cb6c0d41070cbdb9f1af2d26c74561cba785631ba4052.jpg) ∇ Saara Hopea, Model no. 1718 glasses for Nuutajärvi, c. 1951 ∇∇Saara Hopea, Model no. 1618 pitcher with Model no. 1718 glasses for Nuutajärvi, c. 1951

![](images/5d5f1341e20de9059a202c78d4734eb89616fee4680df98e0c7a49abb0b3413f.jpg) Johan Huldt & Jan Dranger, Stuns chair for Innovator Design, 1973 # johan huldt & jan dranger (b. 1942Stockholm, Sweden) During the 1960s there was a commonly-held belief that group action was a more effective means of producing a result than going it alone – the espousal of this conviction resulted most dramatically in the anti-Vietnam war protests and student riots of 1968. The idea of organizing direct group action was also embraced by many young designers, especially in Sweden, a country that already had a long history of design collectives and manufacturingco-operatives. In the late 1960s, fourstudents from the Konstfackskolan (University College of Arts, Crafts and Design) and Tekniska Skolan (Institute of Technology) in Stockholm, Johan Huldt, Jan Dranger, Martin Eiserman and Jan Ahlin, formed an alliance known as Dux4yra. Initially, they began developing experimental furniture made of plastics and paper. Following this, the group was invited to the factory of the Swedish furniture manufacturer Dux to conduct further experiments with tube bending machines. It seemed that bent tubular metal was the material best suited to their objectives: the realization of functional, affordable democratic furniture that comprised a “do-it-yourself” element. As Kerstin Wickman noted, “The American do-it-yourself' idea (b. 1941Stockholm, Sweden) had now taken hold and the intention was that it should be as easy to buy furniture as to buy food in the self-service store. This was the tone of the discussions during these years."After graduating in 1968, two of the group members – Johan Huldt and Jan Dranger – founded their own manufacturing company the following year. Known as Innovator Design, the venture came to international prominence when it exhibited the Stuns chair at the Cologne Furniture Fair in 1973. This highly functional tubular metal easy chair with a canvas sling and polyurethane upholstered cushions was startling for its simplicity and prefigured the new international style of High Tech. As a piece of knockdown furniture, the chair was sold in unassembled component form in a cardboard box so that it could be bought literally off the shelf. Interestingly, Johan Huldt grew up in his childhood home with an early example of Swedish knockdown design, the Trivia furniture range (1943) designed by Elias Svedberg and Erik Wørts, the latter of whom became one of the pioneers of flatpack furniture at IKEA duringthe1950s. Huldt and Dranger's furniture, which was made available in five different colours – yellow, orange, green, 4 Johan Huldt & Jan Dranger, Tech trolley for Innovator Design, late 1970s ![](images/f604bc30c47647c920a6882e215b934cceed2ed29425338e9b51649e4a9b7a42.jpg) ∇ Johan Huldt & Jan Dranger, Slim chair for Innovator Design, 1982 ![](images/bb2611fa33cda236f9e79672d4d690d1f0e771c08d3d61704d43417d7d664a55.jpg) brown and black – and with chrome finishes, was especially well received in France, where it was retailed by Habitat and Roche Bobois. Colourful, cheap and easy-to-assemble, their Innovator range of chairs, tables and trolleys projected a youthful image that was unashamedly industrial. While countering the natural wood aesthetic so closely associated with Scandinavian design, Innovator Design furniture could be seen as a re-interpretation of 1930s Swedish Modernism, which was itself associated with experimental applications of industrial materials such as steel andglass. Huldt and Dranger's landmark furniture designs signalled a new internationalism in Scandinavian design and were retailed anonymously so as to counter the cult of designer-names that had gained enormous currency in the 1950s and 1960s. The furniture became so popular during the late 1970s and early 1980s that eventually Innovator Design production plants were established in four different countries and IKEA began retailing pieces from the range. In tribute to his far-reaching contribution to Scandinavian furniture design, Huldt was made director of the Swedish Furniture Research Unit (1974-1976) and in 1983 was appointedchairman of the National Association of Swedish Interior Architects. He has also consulted to the United Nations and in 1994 was made director of Svenska Form – the Swedish design council. Having subsequently suffered much copyright infringement with Innovator furniture, Huldt became a leading campaigner against design plagiarism. Since the mid-1980s, Jan Dranger has collaborated with IKEA and has concentrated on sustainable design. In 1999 he received an Ecology Design Award (IF Hanover) for his inflatable softAir seating. Manufactured by News Design DFE, the softAir range includes a couch that uses 83% less resources than an upholstered version and takes 10% less space to transport and warehouse. Inexpensive, durable, and environmentally sound, this highly innovative furniture is available through IKEA. ![](images/6a02664cbda52f8c39900199dc432870bfb6bfde72868191dfe751cb2a960432.jpg) Model no. 90 chainsaw for Husqvarna, 1959 ## husqvarna founde 689 ueaen,Sweden) ![](images/8c4aef2baffe89962c0e41dc808718d59c3d5b65d1412d38d3fd260dfbb73bfc.jpg) One of the highest profile Swedish manufacturing companies and one of the world's oldest brands, Husqvarna has a history that dates back over three hundred years. In 1689, King Charles XI of Sweden ordered the construction of an arms factory on the banks of the Husqvarna River, some six kilometres east of Jönköping. Initially, the factory produced musket barrels using waterpower harnessed from the Husqvarna River. The name Husqvarna means, “house-mill”(hus = house & kvarn = mill) and can itself be dated back to as early as 1529. During the late 19th century, the company – like the famous Remington Armory in the United States – diversified its product-line to include the manufacture of domestic appliances. Husqvarna's first sewing machine, known as the North Star, was launched in 1872 and two years later the Husqvarna foundry was established in order to manufacture a startling array of products, from wood-burning kitchen ranges to labour-saving household appliances such as machines for grinding corn and mincing meat. Husqvarna began manufacturing bicycles in 1896, and this led the company to become not only the largest producer of bicycles in Sweden but also from around 1903 a manufacturer of motorcycles. It was not until 1914, however, that the first Husqvarna motorcycle was actually launched. Like other Husqvarna products, the motorbikes were prized for their high build quality and for the value-for-money they offered – both central tenets of Swedish design. In the early 1950s, the company sought the design expertise of Sixten Sason, one of Scandinavia's first indus trial design consultants and an early advocate of productstreamlining.Sason was responsiblefor the design of a number of Husqvarna appliances, including a drill and sewing machine that were notable for their streamlined aesthetic and compact yet logical layout. During themid-to-late 1950s, Husqvarna engines were incorporated into new products such as lawnmowers (from 1956) and chainsaws (from 1959). The first chainsaw was known as the Mode/ 90 and heralded the firm's legendary status as a manufacturer of exceptional forestry equipment. A decade later, this reputation was sealed with the introduction of the Mode/ 180 – the world's first chainsaw to have a totally integrated vibration-dampening system that made it both safer and easier to use than existing models. Since then Husqvarna has pioneered ![](images/9e16ef3f67babee6a2d9c344a6e8ab3461db6f23030ace1258f5714a3b53fe4c.jpg) Carl-Arne Breger, microwave oven for Husqvarna, 1969 ∇Gunnar Leijonberg, automatic lawnmower, 1999 many user-friendly features for its award-winning chainsaws, which are especially favoured by professionalusersfortheir excellent power-to-weight ratio. In 1977 Husqvarna was purchased by the powerful Electrolux Group. The Husqvarna motorcycle brand was sold in 1996 to the Italian manufacturer MVAgusta/Cagiva, and the sewing machine brand was sold in 1997 to the Swedish Viking company. Today, forestry, park and garden productstogether with white goods manufacture remain Husqvarna's core business. ![](images/3037436c7fd40aeb99d892ad6f8aaf2e70f747af8d3dea5f5100fa0576a1da62.jpg)

![](images/5a8d0119df7ac89981103fbcc6c298226b316329b6edf372c47f1aa9487bd682.jpg) Peter Hvidt & Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, AX table for Fritz Hansen, c. 1950 ▶ Peter Hvidt & Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, teak and papercord armchair for Søborg Møbelfabrik, c. 1956 ∇Peter Hvidt # peter hvidt . 9 enhage - d 6 ehagen, Demrk) orla mølgaard-nielsen (b. 1907 Ålborg – d. 1993 Hellerup, Denmark) ![](images/a66b531c172f0d9bbaedec8b9753b90ae6091c523059288ac22f5c4d2802e5ca.jpg) Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen apprenticed at the Nielsen Brother's workshop in Iborg and subsequently studied cabinetmaking at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School of Arts & Crafts), Copenhagen. He later trained under Kaare Klint in the furnishing and interior design department of the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts), Copenhagen. As one of Klint's students, Mølgaard-Nielsen was grounded in the relatively new science of anthropometrics – the systematic collection and correlation of measurements of the human body – and taught how to apply this data to his furniture designs so as to achieve better performance and more comfortable solutions. After completing his academic studies, he worked for a number of Danish architectural firms, including those headed by Kaare Klint, Helweg Moller, Arne Jacobsen, and Palle Suenson (1904–1987). Between 1944 and 1975, he worked in partnership with Peter Hvidt, who had similarly studied cabinetmaking at the Kunsthåndværkerskolen, Copenhagen (graduated in 1940 and established his own design practice in 1942) One of the earliest fruits of this collaboration was the Portex chair (1944) – a teak design with an innovative construction that was one of the first modern stacking chairs produced in Denmark, and one of the first Danish furniture products conceived for industrial manufacture. Manufactured by Foreningen for Møbelexport, the space-saving Portex chair was available with or without arms and with an optional upholstered seat cushion. Hvidt and Mølgaard-Nielsen also collaborated with the well-known Danish furniture manufacturing company Fritz Hansen, and this association led to the pioneering of a completely novel form of furniture construction known as “lamella-gluing” in which layers of beech wood were laminated onto a mahogany core – a technique similar to that used for wooden tennis rackets. The AX chair (1947) was the first Danish furniture product to employ this method of construction. Initially, the chair had a quilted leather-upholstered sling seat/ back that was attached to its beech-veneered mahogany core frame, but the design was later modified so that it was better suited to industrial mass production – from 1950 it was manufactured with a teak and laminated beech frame with separate moulded teak-faced plywood seat and back elements. Although most often seen as an ![](images/820d5615ff455a1c53b53332a10cb85517f7c066f721c81d4b40a1314e0abe1c.jpg) ![](images/84a13fa8a2ef947eae07298c7a248b6ba033ec9da4799bb08a4c9b10cf6b70a4.jpg) ![](images/d1306eb6f777e88a6a68878fa5051a078d33674ddd67e9e04335bec021c37826.jpg) △ Peter Hvidt & Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, unassembled AX chair elements for Fritz Hansen, c. 1950 Δ Peter Hvidt & Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, AX chair for Fritz Hansen, 1947 – this early version had a padded leather sling seat/back section ▶> Peter Hvidt & Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen, AX chair for Fritz Hansen, 1950 – the mass-produced version armchair, the AX chair was also available without arms and with a single armrest. Tables were also produced to match the range of chairs. Importantly, the AX series could be sold in knockdown form, which made the different pieces easy to transport and relatively inexpensive to produce. As a result, the AX armchair became one of the best-selling chairs in Scandinavia during the 1950s. It was also awarded a number of prizes, including diplomas of honour at the 1951 (IX) and 1954 (X)Milan Triennale exhibitions, and a Good Design Award from the 1951 “Good Design” exhibition in America. During the 1950s, Hvidt and Mølgaard-Nielsen also designed a number of “Teak Style” chairs for France & Son and for Søborg Møbelfabrik, which revealed the influence of Finn Juhl's sculptural furniture. Later in 1961 they developed the X lounge chair for Fritz Hansen, which featured a woven cane seat construction and utilized the lamella-gluing process. The design of this chair was adapted in 1977 to facilitate a quilted seat section and to enable it to be linked into benchlike configurations for waiting areas. From 1970, Hvidt and Mølgaard-Nielsen also collaborated withthe designer Hans Kristensen. Through their high-quality, democratic, and technically innovative furniture designs, such as their hugely successful AX chair, Peter Hvidt and Orla Mølgaard-Nielsen helped disseminate around the world the casual yet modern Scandinavian look that became so popular and influential during the 1950s and 1960s. ![](images/c3f21bcdd5cd70f81e113ca9fa1475d7ff74865420f51ed92fd0707aa4fcff14.jpg) Alvar Aalto, Savoy vase for Karhula, 1936 – later produced in cobalt blue glass by littala >▶ Timo Sarpaneva, Lansettí I1 (Lancet /I) vase for littala, 1952 ## ittala foud 1a Fiad) ![](images/1aba593b9195449f854a153a8c10ec43cb5816a203c3284986ca3269d95596ae.jpg) The littala glassworks has historically contributed much to the identity of Finnish design and through its contemporary ranges of glassware continues to promote the design excellence that has become so closely associated with the country's manufacturing output. In 1881, the Swedish master glassblower Per Magnus Abrahamsson founded the littala glassworks near Kalvola. Although the company's first glassblowers were recruited from Sweden, its early glassware, which mainly consisted of simple undecorated designs for household use, revealed influences from elsewhere in Europe. After a difficult beginning, the glassworks eventually managed to gain a reputation for its high quality products, some of which were engraved by Alfred Gustafsson with patriotic motifs that reflected Finland's struggle during the early years of the 20th century for cultural and political autonomy. Having encountered financial difficulties during the First World War, the company's main shareholder was forced to sell his shares to the Karhula glassworks in 1917, which at the time formed part of the Ahlström group. Although it lost its commercial independence, littala benefited from the additional investment that came with being part of a larger industrial group. Ahlström subsequently instigated numerous improvements at the factory, and the Karhula glassworks continued to play an important role within the group's activities, Even though products from both glassworks bore the name “Karhulalittala", by the 1930s littala was mainly concentrating on the production of blown-glass rather than on pressed glass – limited-production “art” glass pieces rather than mass produced utilitarian glassware. Initially, Aino Aalto's range of functional Aalto glassware (1932) and Alvar Aalto's famous Savoy vase (1936) were manufactured by Karhula and were only later produced by littala, In 1946, littala organized a competition in an effort to develop a range of contemporary art glass, which was won jointly by Tapio Wirkkala and Kaj Franck. The unconventional nature of both these designers' work heralded a new direction for littala and a new era in Finnish glass design. Objects such as Wirkkala's famous Kantarelli vase (1946), which was inspired by the heaped piles of chanterelle mushrooms sold in the main market in Helsinki. revealed the new sculptural confidence that was emerging in Scandinavian design at the time. In 1950, Franck left littala, and the same year Timo ![](images/162067f926fa0e23540716c5c8c4edfb306c29e3f3709ff152f48006afca5b5b.jpg) ![](images/1ca59c0056977a347c26f689f6968b4b2e1ee3020be2bd58bccbfae208cbec24.jpg) △ Annaleena Hakatie, Ballo candleholders for littala, 1995 Sarpaneva joined the glasswork's design team. Like Wirkkala, Sarpaneva pioneered many new forms and techniques that brought the company international recognition, especially at the Milan Triennale exhibitions, and helpedfocusattention on Finnish design in general. During the 1950s, Sarpaneva designed several pieces of highly sculptural art glass that employed his innovative steamblowing technique. He also designed the company'sfirst modern household glassware collection in the mid-1950s. Known as the i-collection, this product range was the result of two yearsintensive research and attempted to bridge the utility and art glass markets. With its delicatesmoky colours and simple forms, thei-collection possessed a sophisticated yet elemental beauty. During the 1960s, littala produced a number of “frosted” glassware ranges, notably Sarpaneva's landmark Finlandia vases (c. 1964) and Wirkkala'sbestselling Ultima Thule range(1968), which exerted considerable influence on mainstream glassware design in the 1960s and 1970s. In 1971, the companybuilt a new factory for the production of light fittings and established a glass museum, butduring the early 1970s littala along with other Finnish glassworks suffered badly from the global recession and from the proliferation of cheap foreign copies of their designer collections. By the mid-1970s, however, there was growing demand for smooth-surfaced glassware, and littala designers produced a number of ranges usinginnovative production techniques, such as those used for Sarpaneva's Archipelago range(1978), which captured different sized bubbles within the glass. The Kaveri wine goblets (1979) designed by Jorma Vennola (b. 1943) even combined clear glass bowl with interchangeable plastic stems. During the late 1980s and early 1990s, two new designers joined the littala design team, Tiina Nordström (b. 1957)and MarkkuSalo (b. 1954), who have since executed a number of notable designs for both mass-produced and one-off glass objects. Tiina Nordström's Major votive lantern vase(1994) reflects her goal of finding a concrete expression for glass, which she describes as the “immaterial material”. In 1990, littala was purchased by the Hackman Group and with a new international outlook the company commissioned work from non-Finnish designers such as Konstantin Grcic (b. 1965) and Marc Newson (b. 1963).Since ![](images/98644b9c82c5b79db7c015e3f82ecb15787eed8b36a77b03fe5f2051cf23be21.jpg) 1996, the highly talented Finnish designer Harri Koskinen has also worked in-house for littala and has developed a number of notable products, including the At/as vase/candleholder (1996) and the large-scale Relations storm candleholder (1999), both of which demonstrate the innovation and artistry that have for so long been littala's greatest attributes. In order to meet changing lifestyle demands and to spread itsbeliefs about how to optimize the functionality of our homes, in 2002 littala's parent company launched a range of products for cooking and socializing that was brought together from its four Scandinavian brands – Arabia, Hackman, littala and Rörstrand – into one new common brand, littala. 4 Timo Sarpaneva, Marce/ vase for littala, 1990 ∇Markku Salo, Nappi candleholder for littala, 1998 ∇∇ Markku Salo, Marius glassware range for littala, 1985 OVERLEAF 4 Tiina Nordström, Major votive lantern/vase for littala, 1994 Tiina Nordström, Tuisku vases from the Pro Arte Collection for littala, 1995 ![](images/00379e49fb27d55dc141ebc5434e5a9d8fe4b05aa7aa784942991f55a78fb0ab.jpg) ![](images/06ed58fa9e594aa4252c1f270833a9ee38feb1d2030af01e53b6177a09b52c03.jpg) ![](images/326f1a5822c58a9d1ea0a5b4d98f841e4fae934b274acecf28cfa3819d396c3c.jpg) ![](images/2a55fe3fa3adf0ba6f6e44fe3c3aa30ae38a715da78227f7484e65ccc2d16be4.jpg) ![](images/9a9f9dfdd1eab6d1f27e71417b6d64e3fa0926342e867414a1c473a35a28482a.jpg) First IKEA store, 1958 ∇ Ingvar Kamprad, founder of IKEA ## ikea (founded 1943 Älmhult, Sweden) ![](images/984eeb54ae8a72f4d06336c8ccd4c7844d94401692aae79325b1c9bfe1a4290d.jpg) ![](images/89d8d2880da1d9ad587536daf0290e6817049bb139c1a13f96b49778b20db70a.jpg) No other company has promoted the concept of Scandinavian design more widely than IKEA, which for over 50 years has manufactured and retailed highly democratic products based on the trinity of “form, function and affordability”. From its outset, IKEA espoused a particularly Swedish philosophy born out of the socialist belief that “people who are not well off should be given the same opportunities as people who are” – which in IKEA's case has meant the possession of welldesigned yet inexpensive home furnishings. The origins of this extraordinary and now major international company can be traced to 1943, when the 17-year-old Ingvar Kamprad (b. 1926) established IKEA with a £150 loan from his father. Founded in the town of Älmhult, a few miles from Kamprad's home village, IKEA was situated in the Småland region, which had historically been one of the poorest areas in Sweden. The company originally sold a variety of products through mail order, from cattle soap to stockings. Later, during the early 1950s, IKEA began retailing furniture at factory prices through mail-order catalogues. This move was met with such resistance from the established furniture trade that IKEA was not allowed to exhibit at the large furniture trade fair held in Stockholm, and suppliers threatened to boycott the company. Kamprad observed that “most nicely designed products were very, very expensive”, yet he desired to manufacture well-designed practical products that the majority of people could afford. With this social mission to democratize design, Kamprad realized that there were three essential priorities – aesthetics, function and suitability for mass production. During the late 1950s, he recruited his first designers, Gillis Lundgren, Bengt Ruda and Erik Wørts who, while working within the modern idiom, were designing furniture for self-assembly – a revolutionary development in domestic furniture design. The Rega/ bookshelf (1959) designed by Lundgren was one of the first IKEA products sold in flatpack form. Another concept for the home IKEA's designers were developing at the time was “modular thinking”, which involved the design of modular components and interlinking systems rather than individual one-off products. This led to the company's co-ordination of its product range and promotion of an integrated home furnishing look – an early and important example of lifestyle retailing. The advent of particleboard in the 1960s heralded ![](images/3ca029a6c57281c37e1de3d58d47ed16e58d1798867444c3b213323fa3916564.jpg) 4Karin Mobring, Diana chair for IKEA, 1972 ∇ Jan Dranger, Tärnaby chair for IKEA, 1983 ∇∇ Cover of the first IKEA catalogue, 1951 ![](images/e1642690ad08b569f80e57016c3cac06675ddab7b7372c607521aec235a16d12.jpg) the arrival of really low-cost furniture and this influenced IKEA's product line immensely. An inexpensive yet relatively hard-wearing material, particleboard was used extensively by IKEA for all kinds of self-assembly furniture, as it still is today. While the 1970s were a period of general insecurity IKEA prospered and expanded thanks to its policy of producing inexpensive functional furniture with a youthful appeal. During this time the company developed several products made of plastic, including the Telegono lamp (1970) designed by the avant-garde Italian designer Vico Magistretti (b. 1920). Throughout its history, however, IKEA has mainly concentrated on producing interpretations of well-known classic designs – from the ubiquitous Thonet café chair to the plastic Polyprop stacking chair by Robin Day (b. 1915). The 1970s were also the decade of stripped pine and brightly coloured textiles, and IKEA developed this informal look within its product range specifically for younger consumers. The following decade, IKEA began producing stylish yet practical furniture designed by, among others, Niels Gammelgaard – whose Moment table (1987) won the Excellent Swedish Design Award. Other award- ![](images/9e84e9bebdeb7739ce5eafa3c0dfb388815fb17ad91dab6ad5430becb68e6c07.jpg) ea 2 ![](images/4b7aabf85f5c38440efe2230345e3bb1e89aebc01853379712fd98ed8e2d8d0b.jpg) Gillis Lundgren, Tajt chair for IKEA, 1973 – could be converted into a single bed ∇Tord Björklund, Bogen table/trolley for IKEA, 1986 ∇Knut Hagberg & Marianne Hagberg, Puzzel children's furniture range for IKEA, 1988 – recipient of an Excellent Swedish Design Award in 1988 > Olle Gjerlöv-Knudsen & Torben Lind, Skopa chair for IKEA, 1974 ![](images/795a6fa342183587d46e20f12b6221cbee44e96990fe2ab9b93de55b1980f472.jpg) ![](images/d62897d3df47ca3f661e71d3aeff2d93bdf7ee757f6d62c5076812257e7b4940.jpg) ![](images/0bfc9ccd37bdf1b7c2b481210089b4fdbcacab06e2bedd1825d5eb2c672dd4bb.jpg) kea 4 ![](images/0d40f94b249fcb1c2959a47b53e70b3a89dcff60eccbfef30b1eae781aa9afd4.jpg) ![](images/1bf3a3be67d9f46c45bf43bd535a343f75c07333acdd5a39d3b90ed0c2d343d3.jpg) △Noboru Nakamura, Poem chair for IKEA, 1977 – although designed by a Japanese designer this chair is “so strong, so blond, so typically Scandinavian that it remained in the IKEA range for years" △> Cover of British IKEA catalogue, 1989 winning designs included the Puzze/ range of children's furniture (1988) designed by Knut and Marianne Hagberg, which combined primary colours and simple forms and became a bestseller. Throughout the 1990s, IKEA continued to expand its international operations and popularize its version of Scandinavian Modernism. Today, the IKEA Group has 143 stores in22countries with a further 20 stores owned and operated by franchisees in 13 countries/territories. More than 2,000 suppliers in over 50 countries manufacture its 12,000 products. In 2001, the company's turnover was 11.3 billion Euro, while more than255 million people visited its stores worldwide. Despite these impressive figures, Kamprad is not content with just producing home furnishing and plans to diversify his product line into small pre-fabricated flats. As the design theorist Victor Papanek wrote, “Onething is certain: IKEA will continue in the forefront – ecologically, socially and culturally – of making things that work, possess beauty and are affordable." ![](images/4c36b4eb549d74eb6a446222b3aa57ae5bb1bcf280faeccf7b44464ab181aff1.jpg) 4Thomas Sandell, PS-Serie wheeled storage unit for IKEA, 1995 ∇Morten Kjelstrup&Allan Østgaard, Mammut child's chair for IKEA, 1993 ∇> Page from British 2002 IKEA catalogue showing bestselling Billy shelving system which was designed in-house and first launched in 1978 ![](images/e567b221d31e7160ae63a3da78209cf98b73b3aaa10fefd88d44c970207e64cb.jpg) ![](images/2e1ad3fb7c8d15b6f86c244c2ec97c308a65c8032a4b2e9b68ad05c1868cbb22.jpg) Clean design lines, high level versatility and low level prices have kept BILLY at the top of the sales list for over twenty years. Just look what you get for yout money. Two different heights, two widths, beech, birch or new medium brown ash veneer and a range of finishes. Low doors or gloss high doors, shelf inserts and heighr extension units. For something novel, this year we've introduced decor edging which you can just add to an existing Billy bookcase. Functional, low cost design? We wrote the book at IKEA ## A best seller, in anyone's books ![](images/fffcc48466786764f08253bb25088baa916c4f6f776a901ec0e5fe62cbfc58f1.jpg)

![](images/99c59f7da0a921d61b892bfc54b3bcffdcaa7a82c4aeab1eaa5ad9e9b1fe5df8.jpg) ▶InterDesign, locomotive design for ASEA-Traction 1985 # interdesign (founded 1982 Stockholm, Sweden) ![](images/f971045af0bf8c8f1403db83b8082d5b2cdc6f599ab141dd406f9a1fd4597612.jpg) In 1982, Tom Ahlström (b. 1943)and Hans Ehrich (b. 1942)established InterDesign as a subsidiary of their other design practice, A&E Design, which had been founded in Stockholm in 1968. This earlier design consultancy was highly celebrated for its design for disability and won numerous awards for its life-enhancing and ergonomic products. InterDesign was established for the development of more industrial-type products – from transportation to packaging to consumer products. In 1997, the venture became an independent industrial design consulting company and has since become one of Sweden's leading industrial design practices. Although mainly specializing in plastics, InterDesign has worked in a wide range of fields and materials. It has designed sanitary ware for Gustavsberg (1984), a locomotive for ASEA-Traction (1985), a range of toothbrushes for Colgate(1986), automatic injection devices for SHL Medical (1997), as well as dental floss and tooth pick dispensers for Jordan, electric circuit breakers for Norwesco, and lighting fixture components for Belysia. In 1992, InterDesign received the Swedish Environment Design Award for its returnable milk bottle (1992)designed for Arla. In the development of its product solutions, Inter-Design uses state-of-the-art computer-aided design (CAD) systems to rapidly determine product structure, and stereolithography to validate the ergonomics, aesthetics and integration of components – all before tooling is engaged. InterDesign states: “Designing the first product for a company is not without consequences. Designing the second and subsequent products means implementing a design strategy that includes product image in perfect coherence with user ergonomics. The whole must satisfy and attract the direct client (the distributor) as well as the client of the client (the end user). This is the way to build fidelity, notoriety and competitive differentiation." InterDesign's work exemplifies the Scandinavian approach to industrial design – the meticulous development of qualitydriven product solutions that are informed as much by human needs as by material, technological, and commercial concerns. ![](images/18b2d3d75f61f9180257a34074bab85b83960c218b66beae3b4fe14786fedd5e.jpg) ![](images/ae35cd26a9b92e458c317fce9f253ef105ba8339d971a9cf950f8f43d4bbd14a.jpg) 4InterDesign, toothbrushes for Colgate, 1986 4InterDesign, Peninject autoinjector forScandinavian Health, 1995 ∇InterDesign, returnable milk bottle for Arla, 1992 ![](images/4943ff5c9f88250bfc311d80de96b4795ea79ed39d9843f7848c3f203af102ba.jpg)

Alfred William Finch, earthenware vase, covered pot and jug for Iris Workshops, c. 1897-1902 ![](images/a5583a05a49fff966da348404b46f6e6946c897d9f006f8fb9a46ee46d3ec641.jpg) ▶ Alfred William Finch, earthenware vase for Iris Workshops, c. 1897-1902 # iris workshops (founded 1897 Porvoo, Finland) In common with other artistic collectives established around the turn of the 19th century, such as the Guild of Handicraft in England (founded 1888), the Dresdner Werkstätten für Handwerkskunst in Germany (founded 1898), and the Wiener Werkstätte in Austria (founded 1903), the Iris Workshops produced well-designed highquality wares for everyday domestic use in the “New Art” style. With its factory based in Porvoo, the workshops retailed its goods with much enthusiasm in Helsinki, but like so manyother progressive reformist design ventures its endeavours were relatively short-lived, spanning only five years. The founding of the Iris Workshops by the Swedishborn furniture designer, Louis Sparre(1866–1964) and the celebrated Swedish-Finnish artist Axel Gallén (1865-1931), who later changed his name in 1905 to the more Finnish sounding, Akseli Gallén-Kallela, together with the Anglo-Belgian ceramicist Alfred William Finch(1854–1930), a friend of the celebrated Art Nouveau designer Henry van de Velde (1863–1957), must be seen within the context of the strong nationalistic movements that were emerging in Scandinavian countries during the 1890s, which stressed a romantic involvement with nature and the historic past. In short, the founders of the IrisWorkshops were not only interested in reforming design, and thereby public taste, but were also consciously attempting to forge an independent Finnish cultural identity. The Iris Workshops were in fact the very first Finnish enterprise to be set up with this specific intention. During its brief yet influential life, the factory received and fulfilled a number of important commissions from Finland and Russia. It also retailed its wares in St. Petersburg and through the two most important galleries in Paris for the dissemination of the New Art, Siegfried Bing's “L'Art Nouveau" and Julius Meier-Graefe's “La Maison Moderne”. The Iris Workshops manufactured ceramics, textiles, metalwork, and furniture within the Arts & Crafts idiom – all of which was eminently suited as furnishings for the numerous fin-de-siècle Art Nouveau buildings being erected in Helsinki and its environs, the most notable of which were designed by the architectural practice of Eliel Saarinen, Herman Gesellius(1874–1916), and Armas Lindgren(1874-1929). FromFinch's folk-style earthenware ceramics with their abstracted wavelike organic motifs to Sparre's elegant yet rustic ![](images/55f1f351fd3d126b3d81095bcbff0c97643ae00361f19ff32b1d57f4cbbe07ec.jpg) template for the future direction of Scandinavian design, while also embodying a uniquely Finnish interpretation of the Art Nouveau style. furniture, the workshops' output promoted an unpretentious aesthetic that was based on an honest expression of the materials being used – a characteristic that has remained a defining feature of Scandinavian design. As with the other well-intentioned design collectives in Britain and Germany, the Iris Workshops often based the design of its domestic wares on vernacular antecedents that had an inherent formal simplicity. Objects such as these, however, had little if any impact on the design of products intended for ordinary working class people. Indeed, Iris designs were almost entirely the preserve of the intellectual elite – the people who were not only the most likely to appreciate the philosophy underlying the objects but also those who were best able to afford their high cost. In 1900, the Iris Workshops exhibited a room installation designed by Gallén-Kallela at the Paris “Exposition Universelle et Internationale”, which was awarded a gold medal. Although the Iris Workshops received much international acclaim, it became financially unsustainable and was forced to close in 1902. Though short-lived, the IrisWorkshops' homely yet sophisticated products can be seen to have provided an important philosophical ![](images/a3fe30d11193d8538d42bb32bab7f63197885332eb276a9c425a4d4f4aaed5dd.jpg) 4 Louis Sparre, design for a living room, 1903 ![](images/5ed9fafc4cc93deceb16974eb800c2ba20d5716fe0cea8049368c56ac0ca59a5.jpg) ![](images/22cc6af20c89ed20cd95676ce67bf93b2f065dfce57e6c2948118dd14bb93d9d.jpg) Maija Isola, Unikko (Poppy) textile for Marimekko, 1960 ## maija isola (b. 1927 Riihimäki – d. 2001 Finland) ![](images/0e832d1a9186670b6de459518b08e7a6147ad48a98ac52e41acd0c28fc8295c7.jpg) The Finnish textile designer Maija Isola studied painting at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki from 1946 to 1949. Between 1949 and 1960, she was the principal designer for the Finnish textile firm Printex, which had been established by Viljo Ratia and was under theartistic directorship of Armi Ratia(1912–1979). During her time there, Isola designed numerous printed textiles for interior use that the company manufactured. From 1951, she also produced designs for Printex's sister company, Marimekko, which had been founded with the intention of promoting the use of Printex textiles in fashion and interior design. The inexpensive cotton sheeting used for Isola's designs was completely transformed by her bold abstracted patterns into elegant yet avant-garde textiles.Her designs for dress and furnishing fabrics for Marimekko, as well as her textiles for Printex, were included in the “Design in Scandinavia” exhibition that toured America and Canada from 1954 to 1957. In 1958, her work was included in the “Formes Scandinaves” exhibition in Paris and displayed at the Brussels' World's Fair. The following year, her textiles began to be retailed in the United States, where they were admired for their startling originality. Isola's earliest designs were inspired by the African art she first saw on a trip to Norway in 1949. Initially, these were executed in brightly coloured crayons and later included hand-painted stripes in their patterns. By the mid-1950s, Isola was producing textiles decorated with floral motifs. She also designed fabrics influenced by Slovakian folk art in the late 1950s, and by traditional Karelian peasant motifs in the late 1960s and early 1970s. Many of her silk-screened textiles were translations of her own artwork. In the mid-1960s, she produced her most famous range of printed cotton textiles, which incorporated large-scale geometric patterns that were printed in strong flat colours. These bold designs such as Kaivo (1964), Melooni (1963), and Cock and Hen (1965), reflected contemporary artistic trends, most notably the influence of Colour Field painters in America. Isola's fabrics from this range had a strong graphic quality and came to epitomize not only Marimekko textiles but also a new direction in Finnish design. She received ID Prizes in 1965 and 1968, and during the later part of her life was partnered with Fujiwo Ishimoto (b. 1941), one of #

the few in-house designers working at Marimekko Isola's colour-saturated and elemental patterned fabrics, which have been widely exhibited throughout Europe, America and Australia, have had an enormous influence on contemporary textile design. ![](images/0cd3229fd71d4f735c4abeb05d9042390aae9063f4bd6e82e7a21f7efa9e8b22.jpg) △ Maija Isola, Lokki (Seagull) textile for Marimekko, 1961 Maija Isola, Melooni (Melon) textile for Marimekko, 1963 ![](images/d08cda5e808e5613e3a048ef13d184b3a8488ece323bf6f0043dfd30b465a317.jpg) Maija Isola, Kaivo textile for Marimekko, 1964 ![](images/32c39c1f3a1e623e690dcb09882be57c348727897ee8aac57c7e91794dd8e9bb.jpg) ## 22 Arne Jacobsen, set of Model no. 3100 Myren (Ant) chairs for Fritz Hansen, 1952 ▶▶ Arne Jacobsen, Model no. 3107 – Series 7 chair for Fritz Hansen, 1955 ## arne jacobsen (b. 1902 Copenhagen – d. 1971 Copenhagen, Denmark) ![](images/bd3e9586e7a6d12a2c8fbb139dd05a9e4614af93efece191953ad7a5c4a8ebb9.jpg) The most celebrated postwar Danish architect and designer, Arne Jacobsen initially trained as a mason at the Skolen for Brugskunst (School of Applied Arts), Copenhagen, from where he graduated in 1924. He went on to study architecture at the Kongelige Danske Kunstakademi (Royal Danish Academy of Fine Arts) Copenhagen and graduated in 1927. This combination of practical and artistic training enabled Jacobsen to later produce buildings, interiors, and many different types of product designs that elegantly balanced function with aesthetics. Following the completion of his studies, he worked as an assistant in the City Architect's Office (Paul Holsøe) in Copenhagen until 1929. While there, he designed a music pavilion, shelters, and entrance gates for Enghave Park in Copenhagen. His early work was influenced by Modernists such as Le Corbusier (1887–1965), Erik Gunnar Asplund and Ludwig Mies van der Rohe (1886– 1969). Jacobsen became one of the first designers to introduce Modernism to Denmark through his projects such as the circular House of the Future, which featured all mod cons including a helicopter landing pad on the roof, and which he co-designed with Flemming Lassen (1902–1984) for the building and housing exhibition held by Copenhagen's Academic Architects' Association in 1929. For this project, Jacobsen also designed several pieces of tubular metal furniture that were aesthetically suited to their Modernist surroundings. Around 1930, he established his own design office in Hellerup and began practising independently as an architect and interior designer. One of his first important architectural commissions was the functionalist Rothenborg House, Ordrup (1930), which was conceived as a Gesamtkunstwerk (total work of art). Another important early project was the beach-fronted Bellavista apartment block with the adjoining Bellevue theatre and restaurant complex (1930–1934) in Copenhagen. The interior of the Bellevue Theatre was remarkable for its sweeping balcony and its wave-like rows of steam-bent plywood auditorium seats. In 1936, Jacobsen and Erik Møller (b. 1909) won a competition for the design of the Ärhus Town Hall (1937–1942), a scheme that was distinguished by soft flowing lines that not only revealed a decidedly humanistic approach to Modernism, but also predicted the bold organic forms of Jacobsen's later projects.
