His work was shown at both the 1937 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts et Techniques dans la Vie Moderne" and the 1939 New York World's Fair, where it won much critical acclaim. Although best known for his superlative art glass pieces, Öhrström also designed glassware for domestic use that incorporated simple undecorated forms, while retaining a sculptural boldness. After leaving Orrefors in 1956, Öhrström continued to work as a glass sculptor, producing both private and public work, most notably his ![](images/041727bff260acf595611f99737f420b68576e065433a42e736d25b7e09e161a.jpg) ![](images/901cf7d1bfbe7e0793aa90bedafb8e20d115ba4c9eb003339b8bc1692f528e8c.jpg) ![](images/4f90303e56ca5ac483fa78c12e2d4827dc0c40073f2b0709b3bd237fa1875977.jpg) 120 feet Kristallvertikalaccent (Crystal Vertical Accent) sculpture (1974) in Sergels Square, Stockholm. 4Δ Edvin Öhrström, crystal glass vases for Orrefors, c. 1958 4∇ Edvin Öhrström, Edvin vases for Orrefors, c. 1943 Δ Edvin Öhrström, Man and Horse vase using Ariel technique for Orrefors, 1937 OVERLEAF 4 Edvin Öhrström, Red Panther vase using Ariel technique for Orrefors, 1938 ▶ Edvin Öhrström, Shell vase using Arie/ technique for Orrefors, 1937 ![](images/f0c5e1225f8df0d2703e69076f244d1a11a454b8b5fc193f15b21ad7c1099bf3.jpg) ![](images/5e5752c98f6c5ea7ff6ef931e8dce2f7ab7d37a60c68d44a27a32340e4f0bde6.jpg)

Peter Opsvik, Balans Variable sitting tool for Stokke, 1979 ![](images/26a2f61f40fc409fda08416a4e8504fe96d210e4718253dca4a4406eab79f681.jpg) # peter opsvik Srda, Noway) ![](images/a860252331c4158dee83ae8530116ecf24e3944466aa399cf245f62140d70aa5.jpg) The leading Norwegian furniture designer Peter Opsvik studied at Bergen College of Applied Art from 1959 to 1963 and the State College of Applied Art in Oslo from 1963to 1964, prior to working as an industrial designer for the Tandberg Radio Factory in Oslo from 1965 to 1970. In 1967, Opsvik won a design scholarship from the Norwegian Credit Bank and two years later was awarded another scholarship from the Norwegian Design Centre. He began working as a freelance furniture designer in 1970 and designed the innovative Mini Max desk and chair, which could be height adjusted to meet the needs of both adults and children and was manufactured by Stokke. Using the same revolutionary concept of Generationenstuhl (a chair that grows with the child), Opsvik later designed the best-selling Tripp Trapp high-chair/chair in 1972. This remarkable design not only elevated the child to a normal table height, but with its foot-rest also ensured that the necessary support was provided so as to allow enough freedom of movement “to explore the world in comfort and safety”. In 1974 Opsvik designed his Model no. 2010 office chair for Håg and five years later, having collaborated with Hans Christian Mengshoel (b. 1946) on the development of better performing ergonomic seating solutions, introduced the revolutionary Balans Variable sitting tool. With its balanced rocking base and provision of an ergonomically beneficial kneeling position, the Balans Variable afforded the user a good upright posture while working at a desk or table. During the 1980s, Opsvik continued to produce ergonomic seating designs for both Stokke and Håg, including his two Post-Modern products – the Balans Supporter (1983), which provided support for a semi-seated position, and the Garden Seating Sculpture (1984), which was more of an aesthetic exercise than a particularly functional solution. Since the mid-1980s, Opsvik has produced both conventional style ergonomic seating, such as the Sitti child's chair of 1993 and the Actulum rocking chair of 1995, as well as more experimental and expressive furniture designs, such as those shown at the “Dimension in Wood – Sculpture or Furniture?" exhibition held at the Applied Art Museum in Oslo in 1986. Some of this latter type of furniture, which is distinguished by quirky forms and ambiguous function, has been put into limited production by Cylindra. Although the majority of ![](images/bc6d910493aca83380c316f99a3823eaf50c4f5dfd594513d6cce6a1e88b707f.jpg) ![](images/86d75e888d36779a1afa26fa001cf853db0b12d57769e8fcc1d977a8dc71e78d.jpg) Opsvik's furniture designs exemplify the Scandinavian concern for social inclusiveness (children's furniture) and healthful living (ergonomic products), there is another side to his work that is more concerned with expressive form, which is perhaps a reflection of the Norwegian penchant for quirkiness within the visual arts. ![](images/9557740773374654d25e12a2ebaf857b655135ae4044d3492e460d51980f8ab0.jpg) Δ Photograph showing the correct ergonomic position afforded by the Balans Variable sitting tool, 1979 – This innovative design was developed with the assistance of Hans Christian Mengshoel and formed the blueprint for later ergonomic designs by Peter Opsvik Δ Peter Opsvik, Sitti adjustable child's chair for Stokke, 1993 4 Peter Opsvik, Tripp Trapp adjustable high-chair for Stokke, 1972 ![](images/481665d0fad83e593638e678c375cfcbb5c344941b80d59380041bcc934c91dc.jpg) Simon Gate, Parispokalen (Paris Cup) for Orrefors, 1925– exhibited in the Swedish Pavilion at the “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriel Modernes" in Paris, 1925 ## orrefors (founded 1898 Småland, Sweden) ![](images/b7ff93b0c595410befebabf701881bdd3de55a82f03b7f7a26ec35b877243442.jpg) The Orrefors glassworks was founded in 1898 on the site of an ironworks that had been in operation since 1726. By the late 19th century, the forge at Orrefors was in terminal decline and as a result the local economy was becoming increasingly reliant on timber. After the ironworks' eventual closure, the glassworks was established on the same site and began manufacturing medical and domestic glassware, such as glass bottles and jars. In 1913, the Gothenburg industrialist Consul Johan Ekman bought the Orrefors glassworks from Johan August Samuelsson and appointed Albert Ahlin its manager, thereby heralding a new era for the company. A year later, Orrefors began manufacturing crystal glassware, but it soon became apparent that there was a need for accomplished artists to design new forms and patterns. To this end, Simon Gate and Edward Hald were recruited in 1916 and 1917 respectively. Gate immediately instigated a thorough refinement of both overlaying and etching techniques. Working with the master blower Knut Bergkvist, he also developed a new technique that he christened Graal, in which glass blanks were decorated with cutting, etching or engraving, then reheated and coated with an outer layer of glass before being blown into their required final form. In 1917, both Gate and Hald began designing figurative patterns for glass engraving, that included a series of modestly draped nudes which were a complete departure from traditionally engraved motifs. By the mid-1920s, both designers had thoroughly perfected the art of engraving and were producing exquisitely decorated pieces, such as those designs shown by Orrefors at the 1925 Paris “Exposition Internationale des Arts Décoratifs et Industriels Modernes”. Gate and Hald had quite different approaches to decoration, however – the former being described as “a bounteously figurative artist” borrowing baroque motifs, while the latter was “a vigorous modernist” inspired by the art of his mentor, Henri Matisse(1869–1954). Apart from the artistic wares of this early period, Orrefors also produced domestic glassware by Hald and Gate in response to the Svenska Slöjdföreningen's (Swedish Society of Craft and Design) rallying call for the manufacture of beautiful and practical objects for everyday use. Around this time, the factory also began manufacturing glass light fittings for public buildings and later for domestic interiors, bothofwhich proved a commercial success. In1928, Vicke Lindstrand joined Orrefors and two years later made his debut at the 1930 Stockholm Exhibition with a series of thick-bodied Art Deco pieces that were modern in form and decorated with engraved figurativeand geometric motifs. These new designs by Lindstrand signalled a general shift towards Modernism as well as a new and more sculptural direction for Orrefors. During the 1930s, bothSven Palmqvist and Edvin Öhrström developed innovative techniques at Orrefors known asKraka and Ariel respectively – the former involved sandwiching a grid-like pattern of white or coloured glass between layers of clear glass, whilethe latter entailed sandblasting the surface of a blank which was then sheathed with a transparent exterior layer or layers of glass so as to produce pockets ofair bubbles between the different layers. During the Second World War, the glassworks experienced a severe shortage of materials, but its designers continued to experiment with new techniques such as Cut Graal and the popular Fish Graa/ with marine motifs. In 1947, Ingeborg Lundin joined the firm and the following year the glassworks showed new designs by her as well as a number by Nils Landberg and Edward Hald at the NK department store in Stockholm. These post-war designs were notable for their simple, soft forms and subtle use of beautiful colours. During the 1950s, Orrefors widely exhibited its new products throughout Europe, includingthe 1954 (X)Milan Triennale and the landmark 1955 Helsingborg “H55” exhibition, where Sven Palmqvist introduced his range of Fuga domestic glassware, which employed a new manufacturing technique that utilized centrifugal force. If the 1950sat Orrefors were exemplified by the soft, undulating organic forms of Ingeborg Lundin's Äpple vase, then the 1960s were epitomized by the bold, colourfully striped Pop glasses by Gunnar Cyrén. During the early 1970s, Orrefors began promoting a rustic aesthetic, while in the 1980s the revival of interest in limited edition studio pieces led the company to the decision in 1986 to no longer produce designs from its former catalogue and to concentrate instead on the production of new designs. A year later the glassworks also made the decision to only manufacture glass designed ![](images/641e7bfb806d3f2a0a9f94f8c17bfea1f135f9011a211ef6ff85829adfea689a.jpg) ![](images/bb365c3ef5ca0cfbbb7c5d252a287f779cd14c2674ef751266cbeff9df0da623.jpg) △Edward Hald, Karleksstigen (Lovers Lane) covered glass goblet for Orrefors, 1923 △ Vicke Lindstrand, Pärlfiskaren(Pearl Fisher) engraved vase for Orrefors, 1930-1931 0r re94 Lars Hellsten, King of the Seas dish for Orrefors, C. 1998 ∇ Per B. Sundberg, Move vases for Orrefors, c. 1998 ▶ Lena Bergström, Velvet vase for Orrefors, 2000 ![](images/b9c9d748a69efb1fed90c23794d5b3dbeb02ac044c2b35382b2ce955ab61d530.jpg) ![](images/a2a16cf8c9a336d9fe56d08afeed7eebbbf2dfc3dd2e395f35252393e89ddf19.jpg) ![](images/a2a0169537dc2e2c6ed88a341b5bf48b99e341ebd9eb17c5aab46b0962a82753.jpg) ![](images/606f9a583c6c72d323beef4359f3870bdfbfc4e91edfd4aeaba3fd786381db24.jpg) ![](images/63579e354b67a1456774eabc766d7b2aa3721d6649920153f2d22b06e42ebf7e.jpg) △ Helén Krantz, Labyrinth Blue vase for Orrefors, 1998 △>AnneNilsson, Clara carafe for Orrefors, 2000 by its own in-house designers, such as Eva Englund (b. 1937), Anne Nilsson (b. 1953),Erika Lagerbielke (b. 1960) and Helén Krantz (b. 1953). During this period, Orrefors designers were on numerous occasions presented with a new design award known as Utmärkt Svenska Form for their stylistically diverse work. The 1990s saw a revival in simple yet elegant table glass, such as the designs produced by Gunnar Cyrén and Erika Lagerbielke. The mid-1990s also saw the arrival of new designers, including Lena Bergström (b. 1961), Martti Rytkönen (b. 1960) and Per B. Sundberg (b. 1964). These designers have produced highly sculptural and technically innovative work that pays tribute to Orrefors' artistic traditions, while pioneering new forms and techniques for art glass as well as objects for everyday use. In 1999, the glass artist and ceramicist Ingegerd Råman joined the company. For over a century, Orrefors has “maintained an ambition to develop the expressive power of glass, both artistically and technically”, while striving “to ensure that Swedish glass will always be elegant, exciting, and of superb quality in both craft and design”. ▶Jan Johansson, Abina vase for Orrefors, 1998 ![](images/eca72d9884ced2c814d0543c460a3271cbcc27290d8cbbaf0130dd9cc7db796e.jpg)

![](images/555be3fa81c384982d9b8188c22556a8ed4d695384ef34074990ab9259a83b8e.jpg) HeikkiOrvola,Aurora glassware range for Nuutajärvi, 1972 – later produced by littala # heikki orvola (inish b. 1943) ![](images/2a6a7ea5f8b2ef59b3da3b75fe16caa90855f68287e71e9ad10b9905713997fc.jpg) The Finnish glassware designer Heikki Orvola studied ceramics at the Taideteollinen Korkeakoulu (University of Art and Design) in Helsinki between 1963 and 1968. Following this, he was offered a summer placement at the Nuutajärvi glassworks byKaj Franck. He was also offered a position in the product design department at Arabia, but opted instead for the greater creative freedom afforded by Nuutajärvi. As both companies were part of the Wärtsilä group, Orvola's first design for Nuutajärvi was a range of glassware that was intended to complement a ceramic tableware service that Richard Lindh (b. 1929) had designed for Arabia. Throughout the 1970s and 1980s, Orvola designed several ranges of utility drinking glasses, such as the elegant Aurora(1972), Lili (1980) and Saturno (1984), as well as bowls, vases and a candle lantern that were massproduced using blow-moulding or pressing techniques. In 1976, he studied at the Pilchuck Glass Workshop in Stanwood, Washington, which had been founded by the renowned glass artist Dale Chihuly (b. 1941) andwhich was, accordingto one of its students, “an artist's heaven, and you don't even have to die”, While in America, Orvola also became friends with the glass artist Marvin Lipofsky(b. 1938), who introduced him to the glass artist community in the San Francisco area. The highly creative and artistic nature of American art glass profoundly influenced Orvola, as did his travels to Mexico, China, Russia, Nepal and India. During this period he produced some of the most expressive art glass created by any Scandinavian designer working at the time. He used sandblasting, cutting, lustre finishes, bubbling, filigree techniques and casting, all to dramatic effect. His designs ranged widely in style and execution, from his delicate swirling filigree plates (1980) to his free-blown lidded bowls (1983), which had curious abstracted vegetal forms and incorporated the Ravenna technique that had been pioneered by Sven Palmqvist. His later limited edition pieces, such as the Maya vase (1994) for Nuutajärvi's Pro Arte collection or his sculptural totemlike Grand Cana/ bottles (1998), were explorations of material, form and decoration that clearly showed the influence of Post-Modernism. As Orvola noted, “abundant decoration is not a typical phenomenon in Finnish design. I reckoned it was time to offer people a few colourful items of tableware." In ![](images/c48fdcd2c6adbc8320635d65f7bf690b475ceac467c4f78371cbf7cbed2c67c0.jpg) contrast to his one-off glassware, Orvola also designed production ceramics for Arabia, such as theMoreeni (1992)and the 24hr(1996) tableware ranges, which have basic, undecorated functional forms that project a sense of homely simplicity. Orvola's career, which is typical of many Scandinavian designers, demonstrates hisextraordinary ability to produce designs of high integrity for both one-offstudio manufacture and industrial mass-production. Beyond glassware and ceramics, Orvola has also designed textiles for Marimekko and enamels for Järvenpää. He received the Pro Finlandia Medal in 1984 and his work was the subject of a major retrospective exhibition at the Helsinki Museum of Applied Arts in 2000 – ranging from glass sculptures to well-honed, practical tools for living. As Tapio Periäinen has observed, “Heikki Orvola is a tangible example of the way in which a designer or artist can express and thus show what kinds of industrial utensils are durable, timeless, and conserve nature, and in spite of this are beautiful. ... Orvola helps to point out a way for us all to what is called a sustainable future, an environmentally friendly way of life." Δ Heikki Orvola, Maalais filigraani (Rustic Filigree) goblets for Nuutajärvi, 1972

![](images/aa2c4c6d9c85a0fb86ed9e15c80da54f875c99a58cd1e514e7d00164d7c3efed.jpg) Steen Østergaard, Cado 290 stacking chair for Poul Cadovius (Cado), 1968 # steen østergaard Danish . 1935) Steen Østergaard trained as a journeyman joiner, receiving a Silver Medal in 1957 and graduating from the Kunsthåndværkerskolen (School of Arts & Crafts), Copenhagen, in 1960. The same year, he was awarded first prize at the Saddlemakers' and Upholsterers' Guild anniversary competition. Between 1962 and 1965, he worked in the design office of Finn Juhl, where he assisted in the development of several chairs with exceptionally highquality solid wood frames. In 1965, Østergaard established his own design practice and later designed hisfirst plastic product – theCado 290 chair (1968), which was manufactured from around 1970 by Poul Cadovius. Importantly, this design was one of the first single-material, single-form plastic chairs to be injection-moulded in polyamide (nylon). The elegant cantilevered form of this chair was obviously inspired by Verner Panton's earlier Panton stacking chair (1959-1960), which had originally been manufactured in injection-moulded Baydur (a polyurethane hard foam)in 1968and then from 1970 onwards in injection-moulded Luran-S (a polystyrene thermoplastic). Unlike Panton's design, however, the Cado 290 was available with optional seat cushions, which were screweddirectlyonto the chair's frame.Østergaard's chair could also be linked to form rows and twenty-five units could be stacked onto a specially designed trolley. There were alsoarmchair and high-backed variations of the design. In some ways the Cado 290 chair was a more economic design than Panton's chair, for its scooped out lower section ensured that less material was used. The thickness ofits wall construction was also thinner, which minimized the chair's overallweight. Østergaard has since designed a number of other chairs, but none have matched the Cado 290 – a functionally and aesthetically exciting processdriven design that fully exploited newly developed high-tech plastics technology. ∇Steen Østergaard, Cado 290 stacking chair with Cado 291 armchair, Cado265 lounge chair and injectionmoulded plastic lounge chair with footstool for Poul Cadovius (Cado), c. 1970 ![](images/8a0fbc00d1f8abb7d3d708d277f99e14e3374105c5b305ff2af27927ef4fca0a.jpg)
